## 📚 Paper List

- **[GSBR: Geometric Support Behavior Regularization for Online RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05295)**  `arXiv:2611.05295`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Charles Xu, Jacob Berg, Liyiming Ke, Sergey Levine_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Regularizing online reinforcement learning (RL) toward a pretrained base policy can improve sample efficiency and learning stability, but regularization that is too restrictive limits how much the policy can improve. Base policies -- typically diffusion or flow-matching models trained by imitation learning on large, diverse datasets -- capture many behaviors that are reasonable but suboptimal. Existing regularizers using such base policies are too restrictive in one of two ways: behavioral cloning regularizers pull the actor toward the full base-policy distribution, penalizing it for discarding suboptimal modes, while hard support constraints forbid improvements that lie just outside the base policy's support. We propose Geometric Support Behavior Regularization (GSBR), which penalizes actions by their distance from the base policy's support. Actions within the support incur no penalty, so the actor can concentrate on high-value modes; outside the support, where value estimates are less reliable, the penalty grows smoothly with distance while still permitting improvement when the value gained outweighs the penalty. GSBR estimates this distance from a finite set of base-policy samples, so it requires only sample access to the base policy. Across 19 tasks on four robotic benchmarks, GSBR raises the average success rate from 33% for the base policy to 95%, achieving the highest average success rate and throughput among all compared methods, with about 23% higher normalized throughput than the next-best RL method. Project website and open-source code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CRONOS: Benchmarking Non-Episodic Reinforcement Learning for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05080)**  `arXiv:2611.05080`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Po-Yi Wu, Djengo Cyun-Jyun Fang, Dian Cheng, Tsung-Wei Ke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is promising for adapting robot policies to unstructured real-world environments. However, standard RL pipelines rely on episodic training with frequent scene resets, which is impractical for real-world deployment due to the need for substantial human intervention. While prior work has studied non-episodic RL in relatively small policies trained from scratch, its behavior when fine-tuning modern large-scale pre-trained vision-language-action (VLA) models remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce CRONOS, a simulation benchmark for RL fine-tuning of VLAs under non-episodic scene resets. CRONOS leverages high-fidelity physics simulators and adopts shared-scene multi-task settings. Using CRONOS, we show that naively fine-tuning pre-trained policies fails in non-episodic settings due to shifts in object configurations and end-effector poses; however, these challenges can be mitigated through targeted reset allocation and by addressing biases in pre-trained models. Moreover, we find that non-episodic training enhances long-horizon manipulation and improves generalization to previously unseen object configurations and task sequences. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Fine-Tuning VLAs with Self-Demonstrated Generative Control for Multi-Task Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19490)**  `arXiv:2608.19490`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Prachi Garg, Steve Xing, Prahit Yaugand, Saurabh Gupta, Derek Hoiem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State-of-the-art vision-language-action (VLA) models such as pi0.5 exhibit strong semantic understanding, instruction following and task behavior. However, when deployed on new robots, even minor mismatches in hardware configuration relative to pretraining can cause severe performance drops. Finetuning the VLA on in-domain expert data from the new embodiment improves performance on the expert task but leads to a loss in its original instruction following and behavioral priors. In this paper, we propose a self-supervised method that generates online interaction rollouts from the zero-shot VLA as additional training data for finetuning. Our experiments show this yields strong multi-task manipulation policies on the target robot that (1) inherit pretrained tasks distilled from the zero-shot VLA without expert demos for them, (2) improve success and generalist instruction following beyond expert-only fine-tuning on expert-teleoperated tasks, and (3) mitigate forgetting of held-out tasks from the pretrained policy, not part of fine-tuning. We demonstrate the success of our approach across test sets probing generalization on a real ALOHA robot and a new simulation benchmark in RoboTwin. Video results are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[CoReMatch: Recurrent Continuous Coordinate Refinement for Real-Time Robotic Feature Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03932)**  `arXiv:2611.03932`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Robert J. Wang, Charles Ling, Boyu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feature matching underpins many robotic perception tasks, including visual localization, mapping, SLAM, and autonomous navigation. Existing semi-dense matchers predict confidence on fixed grids, limiting correspondences to predefined locations and requiring costly high-resolution grids for precise localization. We present CoReMatch (Coordinate Refinement Match), a recurrent framework that refines correspondences directly in continuous coordinate space rather than predicting discrete confidence maps. CoReMatch progressively improves correspondence estimates by propagating information across iterations. At the coarse stage, a Deep Aggregated Transformer captures global context and iteratively updates correspondence coordinates, while a lightweight regression head further sharpens their localization. Experiments on indoor and outdoor benchmarks show that CoReMatch strikes a strong balance between accuracy and efficiency, outperforming Efficient LoFTR in accuracy while running more than 10x faster on embedded edge hardware. These results demonstrate that recurrent coordinate refinement offers an efficient and scalable approach to real-time feature matching for robotic perception.
  </details>

- **[Decoding Geometry, Not Tokens: Efficient VLMs for Robot Affordance and Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39416)**  `arXiv:2610.39416`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Akila Ayanthi, Darshana Priyasad, Tharindu Fernando, Dimity Miller, Sridha Sridharan, Clinton Fookes, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting where to grasp and how to move is fundamentally a geometry problem. Vision-language models treat it as language generation instead. They tokenize spatial coordinates into text and decode them autoregressively. This mechanism ignores spatial structure and truncates continuous precision. Errors compound across coordinate tokens during decoding. In this paper, we show that compact off-the-shelf VLMs with 0.5-2B parameters already encode rich spatial information in their intermediate hidden states. Autoregressive decoding is the dominant bottleneck for trajectory prediction, while affordance prediction depends additionally on feature depth and decoder architecture. We expose the latent spatial information with a lightweight, geometry-compatible prediction head that attaches to a frozen backbone. It reads features from an intermediate hidden layer, conditions a learned query on the task instruction and refines it via cross-attention over the visual tokens to regress affordance boxes and trajectory waypoints directly as continuous values, without modifying the backbone. We evaluate on ShareRobot-Bench and AGD20K. Our 2B variant reduces trajectory error by over 60% and improves ShareRobot-Bench affordance mAP by 85% relative to the autoregressive baseline. It approaches RoboBrain on trajectory prediction (0.251 vs 0.191 DFD) at 3.8x higher throughput. These results demonstrate a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off for instruction-conditioned 2D geometric prediction.
  </details>

- **[VT-MUSE: Multimodal Unified Sequential Visuotactile Representation Learning for Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21290)**  `arXiv:2608.21290`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Congsheng Xu, Qiaochu Yang, Fangyuan Shi, Yifan Han, Baijun Chen, Yiming Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose VT-MUSE, a Multimodal Unified SEquential representation learning framework for visuotactilemanipulation. Existing approaches often encode visual and tactile observations independently before fusion, limiting their ability to capture fine-grained cross-modal dependencies. Moreover, most methods focus on observations at the current time step and overlook the temporal evolution of contact. VT-MUSE addresses both limitations through a two-stage representation learning framework. In Stage I, modality specific encoders are jointly adapted via cross-modal temporal alignment and masked-view consistency. In Stage II, a conditional variational latent model processes masked visual sequences together with full tactile histories. Auxiliary decoders reconstruct the masked recent visual observations and predict tactile depth changes, encouraging the latent representation to retain both global visual context and local contact dynamics. The learned representation is subsequently integrated into a lightweight Transformer policy through gated cross-attention. On the simulation benchmark, VT-MUSE outperforms the strongest baseline evaluated on all tasks by 11 percentage points and also achieves substantial improvements in real-world experiments.
  </details>

- **[FlowDrive: moderated flow matching with data balancing for trajectory planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21961)**  `arXiv:2509.21961`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lingguang Wang, Ömer Şahin Taş, Marlon Steiner, Christoph Stiller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning-based planners are sensitive to the long-tailed distribution of driving data. Common maneuvers dominate datasets, while dangerous or rare scenarios are sparse. This imbalance can bias models toward the frequent cases and degrade performance on critical scenarios. To tackle this problem, we compare balancing strategies for sampling training data and find reweighting by trajectory pattern an effective approach. We then present FlowDrive, a flow-matching trajectory planner that learns a conditional rectified flow to map noise directly to trajectory distributions with few flow-matching steps. We further introduce moderated, in-the-loop guidance that injects small perturbation between flow steps to systematically increase trajectory diversity while remaining scene-consistent. On nuPlan and the interaction-focused interPlan benchmarks, FlowDrive achieves state-of-the-art results among learning-based planners and approaches methods with rule-based refinements. After adding moderated guidance and light post-processing (FlowDrive*), it achieves overall state-of-the-art performance across nearly all benchmark splits. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory Evaluation via Rollout-Free World Model for End-to-end Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05072)**  `arXiv:2611.05072`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Junzhe Wang, Jinqing Zhang, Qingjie Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although existing candidate trajectory evaluators have substantially improved end-to-end planning accuracy, their underlying mechanism remains suboptimal. Model-based evaluators typically make scoring future-aware by explicitly predicting candidate-conditioned future states over the planning horizon, which we term rollout. However, rollout incurs high inference cost and accumulates prediction errors. We argue that planning does not require reconstructing a full future scene for each candidate, but only future-relevant information for reliable comparison. We therefore propose RFWorld, a Rollout-Free World model for end-to-end trajectory evaluation. RFWorld constructs a shared scene memory and shapes it into a future-readable representation via lightweight training-time temporal readout with semantic supervision, keeping training overhead low. At inference time, candidate trajectories directly query the shared memory via sparse spatial readout for scoring, reducing computation by roughly the planning horizon times the number of candidate trajectories, while avoiding recursive rollout errors. Experiments on NAVSIM and Bench2Drive show that RFWorld learns a more planning-aligned world representation and achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[LLMs as MDP Designers: A Dependency-Aware Agentic Framework for Automated Robot Policy Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04974)**  `arXiv:2611.04974`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Hong Je-Gal, Jinwoo Kim, Chanbin Yi, Hyun-Suk Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate the automated generation of executable robot reinforcement learning (RL) policies from natural-language task descriptions. Rather than using large language models (LLMs) as execution-time decision makers, we use them as designers that construct RL pipelines before deployment, including Markov decision process (MDP) formulation and simulator implementation. We identify a phase-wise structure in this problem: MDP formulation is dependency-structured and sequential, whereas simulator implementation becomes modular once the formulation is fixed. Based on this structure, we propose ARPG, a dependency-aware agentic framework that performs sequential MDP modeling, localized verification and correction, and simulator-structured implementation while externalizing intermediate artifacts. ARPG targets a key failure mode of automated policy generation: executable code may still yield non-learnable policies when state, action, reward, and termination artifacts are not semantically aligned. Experiments in Isaac Sim show that ARPG improves modeling correctness, environment executability, and policy-generation success compared with monolithic LLM baselines and off-the-shelf agentic systems. Additional demonstrations on custom robotics, standard RL, and engineering-domain MDPs further suggest that the same modeling pipeline can be reused beyond the main benchmark suite.
  </details>

- **[DiMuRe: Diagnosis-Guided Multilevel Recovery for Robotic Task Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04226)**  `arXiv:2611.04226`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Renkai Yi, Yuwei Wu, Haoyu Zhang, Penghao Dai, Zhi Gao, Jin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disturbances can invalidate the conditions required by a robot's next action even when much of its task program remains useful. A recovery system must therefore choose a response suited to the current state. We present DiMuRe (diagnosis-guided multilevel recovery), a framework for robot-use agents that uses phase-specific evidence and method preconditions to select program reuse or predefined recovery skills, including bounded vision-language-action (VLA) grasping. After the original program exits, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) can generate corrective code from images and execution feedback. Local verification checks the outcome of each recovery attempt. We also introduce LIBERO-Disturb, a benchmark for robustness and recovery under mid-execution disturbances, supporting program-based agents and VLA policies. On 801 scheduled disturbed Encore development cases, Local DiMuRe (program reuse and recovery skills) achieves 77.65% complete-task success, compared with 58.68% for the original program under the same physical-step allowance. Full DiMuRe achieves 81.90% with terminal replanning, an additional step allowance, and early handoff. Local DiMuRe also uses fewer physical steps per scheduled episode than the original program.
  </details>

- **[RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850)**  `arXiv:2611.03850`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Deng-Ke Wu, Yifan Jin, Yida Yang, Lei Qian, Zhaoye Zhou, Hongru Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation benchmarks are the standard way to compare robot policies, but each benchmark brings its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention, and evaluation is slow: 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes with one simulator and one model on one GPU take 359.7 minutes. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluator shows that simulation occupies CPU cores while the GPU averages 14.3% utilization. RoboScope Kit wraps each model once in a Python class called a Policy, and each benchmark once in an adapter around the benchmark's own evaluator, so the benchmark's rules and scoring stay unchanged. Scheduling runs simulator workers and Policy replicas as separate pools, sizes both from measured probes, and shards work deterministically across GPU instances; runtime optimization renders only the observations an action-chunk Policy reads. Adapters cover six benchmark families. On LIBERO-Long with a StarVLA checkpoint on one 48 GiB RTX 4090, the concurrency search selects twelve simulator workers sharing three Policy replicas, which evaluate 500 episodes 4.45$\times$ faster than the official StarVLA evaluation with equivalent success rates, 2.78$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 1.52$\times$ faster than vla-eval at its tuned configuration. With CUDA MPS, which lets the replicas run their kernels concurrently, the search selects sixteen workers sharing four replicas, 6.16$\times$ faster than StarVLA, 3.85$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 2.10$\times$ faster than vla-eval. On RoboTwin Easy, four GPUs with chunk-boundary observation complete the 500 episodes in 14.4 minutes, 25.04$\times$ faster than the single-GPU baseline, with identical success outcomes and terminal steps.
  </details>

- **[Closing the Lab-to-Store Gap: A Data-Efficient Post-Training and Experience-Driven Learning VLA Framework for Retail Humanoids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20345)**  `arXiv:2607.20345`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Roger Sala Sisó, Tiago Silvério, Jakob Sand, Tran Nguyen Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closing the gap between benchmark performance and reliable real-world operation remains a central challenge for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) humanoid robots, which must handle execution errors, distribution shifts, and environmental variability. This paper presents DEED (Data-Efficient Post-Training and Experience-Driven Learning), a systems-level approach evaluated on a supermarket chip-restocking task using a Unitree G1-Edu humanoid robot and the GR00T N1.6 foundation model. DEED comprises three key components: (1) a data-efficient post-training pipeline with control-frequency alignment, data curation, task-relevant visual highlighting, and reduced VLA dependence; (2) a real-world study of experience-driven refinement, adapted from RECAP via a text-based advantage prefix and a vision-language value function; and (3) a latent-space analysis tool for studying in- and out-of-distribution behavior. Our results suggest that bridging the lab-to-store gap is primarily a systems integration challenge rather than an architectural one: careful data design and targeted post-training can transform a policy that fails under naive fine-tuning into a competent real-world system using only a single GPU.
  </details>

- **[DuoBench: A Reproducible Benchmark for Bimanual Manipulation in Simulation and the Real World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11901)**  `arXiv:2606.11901`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Tobias Jülg, Seongjin Bien, Simon Hilber, Yannik Blei, Pierre Krack, Maximilian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bimanual robot systems substantially expand manipulation capabilities, but coordinating two arms introduces additional control complexity and failure modes that are not well captured by existing benchmarks. We introduce DuoBench, an extensible benchmarking framework for bimanual manipulation policies on the FR3 Duo platform. DuoBench comprises eleven tasks spanning four coordination categories, implemented in simulation and partially replicated in the real world through reproducible task recipes with 3D-printable assets. In addition, we propose a stage-based evaluation scheme that supports fine-grained semantic failure analysis beyond binary success. It includes stage-conditional pass rates with confidence intervals as well as human-teleoperated datasets for all benchmark tasks. We benchmark several imitation-learning and vision-language-action policies in simulation and on real hardware. Our results show that current policies remain challenged by bimanual manipulation, particularly at the beginning of interaction, during parallel arm execution, and in sim-to-real transfer settings. We further find that simulation reveals the same failure modes as real-world experiments. DuoBench provides a reproducible testbed for analyzing them and studying future methods for dual-arm policy learning. Code, datasets, and videos are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[DexHOT: Learning Hand-Object Configuration Transfer for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05119)**  `arXiv:2611.05119`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhewei Gui, Anthony Bolton, Jiyao Zhang, Zeyuan Chen, Qiyang Yan, Tianyu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous manipulation involves not only moving objects, but also arranging the hand around them to maintain useful contacts and enable subsequent actions. Yet many learning-based systems specify goals primarily through object poses or trajectories, leaving hand-object configurations to emerge implicitly. We introduce DexHOT, which formulates dexterous manipulation as hand-object configuration transfer: moving between feasible configurations that jointly specify hand articulation and object pose relative to the wrist. DexHOT learns a reusable relation-conditioned transfer primitive from synthetic feasible configuration pairs, without human reference motions, and composes long-horizon behaviors through closed-loop tracking of configuration subgoals. On DexHOTBench with 120 objects, a single policy achieves 80.5% transfer success on seen objects and 69.4% on unseen objects; on a real dexterous hand, it achieves 61.5% success over 200 real-robot transfers and 90% task success across six demonstration-guided tasks. These results show that explicit hand-object goals provide an expressive and composable interface for dexterous manipulation. More details and videos are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LoopQ: Fair, Closed-Loop Evaluation of Quantized Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05093)**  `arXiv:2611.05093`  `cs.RO`  
  _Syed Umer Bukhari, Magnus Almgren, Ahmed Ali-Eldin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) for vision-language-action (VLA) models promises smaller and faster VLA deployments. However, when analyzing existing VLA quantization works, we realised that it is hard to reason about the performance of the suggested quantization method compared to existing, similar, or in many cases, simpler PTQ methods. We present LoopQ, a closed-loop benchmark that compares multiple quantization policies on the same checkpoint, layers and engine, varying only the method and the kernels. We evaluate RTN, AWQ, GPTQ, ActQuant and QuantVLA on four VLAs with more than 180,000 episodes of LIBERO and LIBERO-plus, compare against QVLA's reported results on the same layers, and benchmark whether the four public VLA-quantization releases can be reproduced. We benchmark the methods on an NVIDIA H100, an L4, and a Jetson AGX Thor. Our results show that while quantization reliably saves memory, 2-3x for real low-bit models, VLA-specific PTQ methods underperform compared to simpler generic baselines. At 4 bits, RTN, AWQ and GPTQ match or exceed ActQuant and QuantVLA, plain RTN matches the calibrated methods, and plain AWQ matches the success QVLA reports for itself. Standard LIBERO hides the losses that do exist: configurations with no significant LIBERO loss lose 1.5-4.3 points under paired LIBERO-plus perturbations. Speed is a weaker story. At batch 1 on the H100, no quantized pi0.5 or OpenVLA-OFT configuration is faster than its 16-bit model (1.06-1.58x slower on library kernels); only the L4 with int8 kernels gains (4-14%), the Thor is slower on every 4-bit configuration, and the engine alone changes latency by 2.1x. Profiling explains why: pi0.5's quantized backbone is compute-bound, while its memory-bound action expert, 63% of GPU time and 13% of the parameters, stays in 16 bits.
  </details>

- **[PrimeSim: A Generalist World Model Simulator from Large-Scale Cross-Embodiment Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04870)**  `arXiv:2611.04870`  `cs.RO`  
  _Travis Davies, Wentao Ma, Lihao Guo, Tengfei Ma, Xiaowen Chang, Jiafeng Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot world model that serves as a simulator must do more than generate plausible videos: it must faithfully follow commanded actions, maintain consistency across camera views, and sustain coherent long-horizon rollouts. We present PrimeSim, an action-conditioned, multiview world model simulator that addresses these challenges through progressive midtraining and posttraining. Starting from a pretrained video generator, we midtrain on 30,110 hours of robot demonstrations spanning 22 platforms. To improve action following, we introduce a unified cross-embodiment action representation and frame-aligned dual-path conditioning to strengthen action-frame correspondence. To maintain multiview consistency, we develop view-aware interleaved attention with explicit camera identities, enabling cross-view information exchange while preserving view-specific representations. We further introduce a fast-video, slow-memory posttraining framework that transforms the bidirectional video generator into an efficient autoregressive simulator. By combining fast causal generation, self-rollout training, and hierarchical temporal memory, PrimeSim mitigates error accumulation from self-generated histories and supports coherent long-horizon simulation. PrimeSim runs at 10.1 FPS, remains stable over long-horizon rollouts on rigid and deformable manipulation tasks, and transfers to held-out platforms within 100 to 200 gradient steps where the pretrained backbone does not. Against five open-source action-conditioned world models it leads four of the five WorldSimProbe suites and posts the best overall EWMBench score, while fine-grained local calibration still trails the field; its rollouts are faithful enough to support GRPO-based policy optimization. We release the model weights, inference code, and training code.
  </details>

- **[Coordinating Humanoid Loco-Manipulation through Action-Conditioned Latent Foresight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04839)**  `arXiv:2611.04839`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ju Dong, Xinyu Xiao, Yiming Zhou, Tianshuo Yang, Xuerong Liu, Lei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinated body motion and hand interaction over time, and a plausible-looking command sequence can still drop a held object or leave an interaction incomplete. We present action-conditioned latent foresight, a latent world-action model (WAM) that evaluates proposed command sequences before execution. An action generator proposes joint motion-hand command sequences from vision, language, and the robot state. An action-conditioned endpoint predictor estimates the visual features each sequence prefix reaches and measures progress toward a demonstration-derived subgoal, while a joint temporal prior scores temporal consistency given the robot state and preceding commands. The robot executes the first commands of the best-scoring sequence and replans from the next observation. During training, action consistency regularization reconstructs the first demonstrated command from observed and predicted feature changes, encouraging the predictor to retain action-relevant information. On ten tasks in the humanoid loco-manipulation simulation benchmark SIMPLE, our method achieves 89.7% average success, compared with 73.7% for Psi-Zero and 82.0% for DiT4DiT. On seven real-robot tasks, it achieves 85.7% under standard conditions and 65.7% under unseen object configurations, while GR00T N1.7 achieves 70.0% and 48.6%, respectively. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[All-For-One: Distilling Visual Foundation Models to a Fast, Memory-Efficient Visuomotor Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04485)**  `arXiv:2611.04485`  `cs.RO`  
  _Byungkun Lee, Minho Park, Hojoon Lee, Dongyoon Hwang, Donghu Kim, Hyunseung Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Foundation Models (VFMs) provide semantic cues such as depth and segmentation useful for robotic manipulation, but directly deploying them in closed-loop control is impractical due to high computational cost, temporal inconsistency, and distribution shift between static internet images and egocentric robot observations. We propose All-For-One (AFO), a two-stage distillation framework that leverages VFMs strictly as privileged information during training, using downstream robotic data. Following the privileged learning paradigm, AFO decouples semantic representation learning from inference-time policy execution. In the All stage, a teacher policy fuses RGB observations with outputs from multiple VFMs to learn a semantically enriched representation. In the For-One stage, this knowledge is distilled in-distribution into a lightweight RGB-only student policy. Evaluated on two Adroit tasks and five MetaWorld tasks in simulation, as well as three real-world manipulation tasks, AFO outperforms prior foundation-model-based approaches while using over 30x fewer parameters than VFM-based encoders and 50x lower latency than its own teacher in simulation benchmarks, providing an efficient pathway for transferring foundation-model knowledge to robotic control.
  </details>

- **[VARP: A Visual Adaptive Rearrangement Planner for Non-Monotone Object Rearrangement Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04093)**  `arXiv:2611.04093`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hanwen Ren, Meng Zheng, Zhongpai Gao, Anwesa Choudhuri, Benjamin Planche, Terrence Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object rearrangement planning in cluttered environments is often non-monotone, requiring objects to be temporarily relocated before reaching their final destinations. Although search-based planners can solve such tasks, they incur substantial computational cost and require expensive replanning when the environment changes. We propose the Visual Adaptive Rearrangement Planner (VARP), which learns non-monotone rearrangement directly from visual state representations generated from expert demonstrations. VARP predicts pick-and-place actions from the current and goal visual state representations, and incorporates an Action Refinement Strategy (ARS) to improve action feasibility and a Progress Enforcement Strategy (PES) to mitigate progress stagnation. By continuously updating actions from the latest visual state representations, VARP adapts online to disturbances without replanning from scratch or performing online combinatorial search. In challenging rearrangement benchmarks, VARP achieves a 98.3\% success rate with only 0.4 s planning time per task, 97.5\% success under execution-time disturbances, and 94.5\% under continuous severe disturbances, while generalizing effectively to unseen object counts. These results demonstrate that VARP provides a scalable and robust solution for non-monotone object rearrangement.
  </details>

- **[DexArena: A Unified Benchmark for Long-Horizon Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02629)**  `arXiv:2611.02629`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haonan Ping, Jianshu Hu, Yimin Wang, Yi Zhu, Pai Liang, Qiyan Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon dexterous manipulation remains a fundamental challenge for robot learning, requiring the composition of multiple skills, precise bimanual coordination, generalizable visual understanding, and robust skill transitions. Yet existing manipulation benchmarks largely emphasize isolated skills or support only a limited range of end-effectors, making these capabilities difficult to evaluate systematically. We introduce DexArena, a benchmark designed to evaluate learning and generalization in long-horizon bimanual dexterous manipulation. DexArena features 10 long-horizon tasks and 30 stage-level tasks spanning diverse household and industrial scenarios. Each long-horizon task is composed of explicitly defined stages with physically grounded success conditions, enabling reliable evaluation of both intermediate progress and end-to-end task completion. A unified benchmark interface and verified human-teleoperated demonstrations enable testing different policy learning algorithms, with standardized simulation configurations, scene randomization, trajectory replay, and task-level evaluation. Through extensive baseline experiments, we identify substantial challenges in precise manipulation, generalization to scene variation, and skill composition. DexArena establishes a challenging and reproducible benchmark for developing robot learning methods that move toward generalizable long-horizon dexterous behavior.
  </details>

- **[PRIME W0: Physical Priors from World Models for Embodied Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39528)**  `arXiv:2610.39528`  `cs.RO`  
  _Huabin Peng, Fagang Jin, Yancong Wei, Tengfei Ma, Chuhang Liu, Chao Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models excel at instruction following, yet most remain feed-forward: they condition actions only on the current observation and discard both long-horizon temporal context and the hierarchical structure of VLM representations. We present PRIME W0, a hierarchical conditional flow-matching framework that closes this gap through three synergistic designs: (1)per-layer KV injection from a pretrained VLM into an Adaptive MoE action expert; (2)dynamics-aware conditioning via asynchronous world-model subgoal-image prediction and a DINOv2 historical-frame semantic bridge; and (3)Action Dynamic Manifold (ADM) flow matching with covariance-structured noise that respects robot kinematic coupling. PRIME W0 is trained in four stages---VLM pre-training, DAT/NTP visuomotor pre-training, DiT flow-matching decoder assembly with WM injection, and end-to-end SFT---and achieves state-of-the-art results on LIBERO and real-robot benchmarks, with systematic ablations validating each architectural component.
  </details>

- **[SONIC-K1: Porting a Universal Humanoid Motion Tracker to a Small Humanoid on a Single GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39327)**  `arXiv:2610.39327`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kyrylo Kolesnichenko, Irvin Steve Cardenas, Jong-Hoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present SONIC-K1, to our knowledge the first open-weights whole-body motion tracker for the Booster K1 humanoid, obtained by adapting the SONIC framework to this smaller robot. The policy is trained from scratch on a single shared GPU with a small fraction of the compute reported for SONIC. In MuJoCo simulation, it tracks nearly all held-out motion clips and performs well on external benchmarks under SONIC's success criterion. On the real K1, it tracks upper-body and slow motions, while stepping and turning remain less reliable, and simulation experiments show a sensitivity to action latency. These results show broad motion-tracking capability on a modest training budget and point to quantitative hardware evaluation as the main next step. Code, policy weights and evaluation tools are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[LLM4Robot-Bench: How Far Can GPT-6 Astra Go in Robotic Manipulation? A Hierarchical, Paired Sim-Real Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34527)**  `arXiv:2610.34527`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haibao Yu, Jiaqi Meng, Zijian Zhang, Bowen Jing, Lei Yang, Yue Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce LLM4Robot-Bench, a hierarchical, paired Sim-Real benchmark for evaluating physical interaction capabilities in contact-rich manipulation. Its 16 household tasks span three cumulative levels: contact selection and geometric alignment (L1), force regulation within sustained contact (L2), and coordinated contact reconfiguration and force regulation (L3). Each task is instantiated in physically and visually matched simulated and real-world environments, with shared task definitions and success criteria. We evaluate GPT-6 Astra zero-shot with three trials per task in each environment. Real-world success rates decline from 80.0% at L1 to 47.6% at L2 and 0.0% at L3, with corresponding simulation rates of 66.7%, 47.6%, and 0.0%. Ten of the 16 tasks have identical success counts across environments; the remaining six differ by one success out of three trials. Across both environments, recurring difficulties in spatial reasoning, contact selection, force regulation, and coordination often prevent local physical progress from accumulating into task completion. These findings characterize Astra's observed capability boundary across increasing interaction demands and, for this model, support matched simulation as a repeatable, scalable testbed for studying the sustained, coordinated physical interactions that limit reliable manipulation.
  </details>

- **[LIBERO-RECOVER: Beyond Task Success Towards Failure Recovery in Robotic Manipulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05178)**  `arXiv:2609.05178`  `cs.RO`  
  _Lin Liu, Zhicheng Bao, Lu Zhang, Ziying Song, Wu Yang, Yuzheng Zhuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) or World Action (WAM) models have recently demonstrated remarkable performance in robotic manipulation. On LIBERO, SOTA method have achieved nearly 100\% success rates, seemingly suggesting that the models are ready for deployment in real world. However, near perfect performance on existing benchmarks can be misleading: success under ideal conditions does not imply real world robustness. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate task completion from predefined initial states, while real world interactions inevitably involve failures such as failed grasps, collisions, and unintended object movements. A robot must therefore not only execute tasks successfully, but also recognize and recover from failures to continue the task. Yet this capability remains largely unmeasured, revealing a critical gap between benchmark performance and real world reliability. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Recover Benchmark, a large scale benchmark for failure recovery in robotic manipulation. Built upon LIBERO, we collect real execution failures from SOTA embodied models and construct 1,000+ scenarios across four recovery levels: (1) Action Retry, (2) Action Adaptation, (3) Object State Recovery, and (4) Environmental Recovery. We evaluate four core capabilities: spatial understanding, object structure reasoning, interaction understanding, and topological reasoning. As the first large-scale benchmark for embodied failure recovery, LIBERO-Recover shifts evaluation from \emph{Can the robot succeed?''} to \emph{Can the robot recover after failure?''}, promoting robust and generalizable embodied agents. The project will be avaible in \textcolor{blue}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Nonlinear Optimization: Many Problems One GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26341)**  `arXiv:2606.26341`  `cs.RO`  
  _John Viljoen, Johanna Haffner, Lasse Peters, Masayoshi Tomizuka, Negar Mehr_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern GPUs and software frameworks have made sampling-based methods, such as reinforcement learning and model predictive path integral control, increasingly popular in robotics. However, the logic these methods can execute per sample is limited to what has been implemented on the GPU. Nonlinear programs (NLPs) are one of the most expressive tools in robotics, reliably minimizing a cost while exactly enforcing general constraints. NLP optimizers were among the field's main workhorses, but were not designed for modern GPU hardware. Embedding NLPs such as trajectory optimization and inverse kinematics directly within each sample would be highly valuable, yet the only current option is to call a CPU solver per sample, possibly in parallel on a multi-core CPU. This bottlenecks the CPU, requires GPU-to-CPU-to-GPU transfers when the rest of the pipeline resides on the GPU, and leaves the GPU intermittently idle. To address this issue, in this work we present jaxipm, the first GPU-batched solver for generally constrained nonlinear optimization problems. Our approach is a JAX implementation of the battle-tested IPOPT algorithm, facilitated by eliminating control flow through heterogeneous iteration fusion, and minimizing GPU idle time through iteration-level batching. Our implementation enables solving hundreds of NLPs in parallel on a single GPU. We validate jaxipm on a range of quadrotor nonlinear model predictive control benchmarks, where on a single GPU we achieve between 30.7% and 69.9% of the throughput of 64 IPOPT instances running in parallel on a 64-core CPU.open-source codebase:this https URL
  </details>

- **[IntentNav: Learning Spatial-Visual Object Navigation from Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08029)**  `arXiv:2606.08029`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuxin Cai, Zongtai Li, Maonan Wang, Muyi Bao, Haokun Zhu, Ruofei Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object navigation requires a robot to search for an unobserved target in an unknown environment by deciding where to explore next under partial observability. Effective search resembles human-like exploration: selectively probing visually promising frontiers while relying on spatial memory to avoid redundant revisits. We propose IntentNav, a spatial-visual imitation framework that learns human-like ObjectNav policies from human demonstrations. To infer high-level search intent from low-level human actions, we introduce Frontier-based Human-Intent Labeling, which looks ahead in human demonstrations and labels the frontier that best explains the demonstrator's future search direction. We construct a spatial-visual candidate space, where BEV memory tracks explored regions, unexplored frontiers, and trajectory history, while egocentric visual memory provides semantic cues for each candidate. A VLM policy is trained to select among these grounded candidates, using Intent-Aligned Objective to encourage consistent and human-like exploration. IntentNav achieves state-of-the-art performance among learning-based methods on the MP3D, HM3D-v1, and HM3D-v2 ObjectNav benchmarks, attaining the best success rate among all compared methods. The proposed candidate-level navigation interface transfers zero-shot to wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid robots without further VLM fine-tuning. \href{this https URL}{Project page}.
  </details>

- **[PACE: Phase-Aware Chunk Execution for Robot Policies with Action Chunking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00537)**  `arXiv:2606.00537`  `cs.RO`  
  _Junnan Nie, Jiayi Li, Chenghao Liu, Junyi Lao, Jiachen Zhang, Tianle Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action-chunking robot policies predict sequences of future actions, but deployment requires choosing how many actions to execute before replanning. The execution horizon trades off feedback frequency against motion continuity. However, success varies non-monotonically with the execution horizon, and the best-performing horizon differs across tasks. These effects make a single fixed horizon unreliable as a default execution strategy across tasks. We propose PACE (Phase-Aware Chunk Execution), a training-free method that selects execution horizons online from predicted action chunks. PACE uses prominent low-speed valleys in the predicted speed profile as candidate replanning boundaries, exploiting the phase-dependent kinematics of manipulation trajectories. By default, PACE calibrates the valley threshold from each task's existing training demonstrations, without task-specific fixed-horizon tuning. On the 50-task RoboTwin2.0 benchmark, PACE improves average success from 57.8% to 64.2% over the strongest fixed-horizon baseline and outperforms the adaptive execution baselines AAC and AutoHorizon. With GR00T N1.7 on LIBERO, PACE achieves the highest overall success rate among the evaluated strategies, supporting its applicability across base policies, benchmarks, and action representations. On real robots, PACE improves the average task score from 60.7 to 77.7 and success rate from 50.7% to 70.4% over full-chunk execution. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ResponsibleRobotBench: Benchmarking Risk-Aware Task-Level Decision Making for Robotic Manipulation with Large Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04308)**  `arXiv:2512.04308`  `cs.RO`  
  _Lei Zhang, Ju Dong, Kaixin Bai, Minheng Ni, Yu Fu, Zoltan-Csaba Marton, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe task-level manipulation requires a planner to recognize hazards, select appropriate mitigation actions, and satisfy their execution prerequisites without abandoning the original goal. We introduce ResponsibleRobotBench, a benchmark of 23 manipulation task configurations with separately specified goals and safety conditions. It connects hazard-category predictions, planned actions, execution feedback, and terminal outcomes at the episode level. Across evaluations of five large multimodal models, terminal safety and safe task completion can diverge: defense prompts increase terminal safety for three tested models but affect safe success in different directions. Correct hazard-category predictions also fail to ensure executable mitigation. In a cutting task under the default configuration, GPT-4o mini identifies the human hazard in all 100 episodes but never requests assistance, and every episode ends unsafe. Offering a help action and actually requesting it lead to different comparisons: outcomes differ between action menus even for a model that never requests help, and repeated requests can leave execution failures unresolved. Imitation-learning evaluations and physical robot sequences illustrate the need to coordinate mitigation with goal actions. Together, these results motivate evaluating mitigation selection, action prerequisites, and recovery alongside hazard recognition and terminal outcomes. The appendix provides task definitions, checker conditions, and prompts. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GeoFaham: Domain-Specialized Multi-Agent Geospatial Analytics for Disaster Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04977)**  `arXiv:2611.04977`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Sohail Danish, Gilles Q. Hacheme, Akram Zaytar, Satej S. Soman, Inbal Becker-Reshef, Juan Lavista Ferres, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world geospatial decision support requires more than single-step retrieval or code generation. Disaster-response questions often involve multi-source workflows that combine spatial databases, map-derived features, satellite imagery, raster products, and reusable geospatial artifacts, where correctness depends on preserving spatial scope, temporal context, and intermediate execution state. While large language models and coding agents offer a promising interface for such workflows, they struggle with geospatial-specific failure modes, including handling large artifacts, selecting the wrong evidence source, losing spatial or temporal metadata, and applying invalid geometric operations. We introduce GeoFaham, a domain-specialized multi-agent system for geospatial disaster analytics. GeoFaham decomposes workflows into specialist agents for vector operations, map feature retrieval, imagery discovery, and raster analysis, coordinated by an Orchestrator with a dual-ledger control loop for dependency-aware planning, progress tracking, validation, and replanning. To support artifact-centric collaboration, GeoFaham uses a Response Store that persists full geospatial outputs and typed metadata outside the LLM context window while agents exchange compact identifiers and summaries. We also introduce GeoFaham-Bench, a 40-task benchmark grounded in 17 real-world disaster records, designed to evaluate multi-step geospatial reasoning, cross-source integration, raster-vector computation, artifact generation, and recovery under non-rigid ground truth. We evaluate GeoFaham on GeoFaham-Bench against strong general-purpose coding-agent baselines, including Codex, Claude Code, and OpenCode. GeoFaham achieves the best overall performance, scoring 0.940 versus the next-best 0.879, while using 7.5 times fewer tokens. Our code and benchmark are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MAS-Loop: Beyond One-Shot Orchestration towards Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38946)**  `arXiv:2610.38946`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Hehai Lin, Qi Yang, Chengwei Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-time multi-agent system (MAS) design seeks to learn reusable orchestration policies, yet prevailing approaches remain largely one-shot: an Orchestrator generates a system but only observes and learns from its terminal outcome. This paradigm discards execution trajectories that expose system-level failures and contain fine-grained information beyond the terminal outcome, leaving no direct basis for revising the source MAS, evaluating the updated MAS, or learning from the resulting contrast. To this end, we introduce MAS-Loop, an execution-grounded framework that closes this feedback loop by coupling an Orchestrator with a trajectory-conditioned Refiner. For each task, the Orchestrator constructs and executes a source MAS to obtain an outcome score and an intermediate trajectory. Conditioned on the trajectory, the Refiner localizes an actionable failure, formulates a trajectory-grounded diagnosis, and proposes a bounded patch to the agents, information flow, or execution configuration. The executable updated MAS is re-executed on the same task to measure its realized outcome under stochastic execution. This pair-MAS comparison provides complementary learning signals: the Orchestrator learns to generate strong systems with fewer recoverable failures, whereas the Refiner learns to expose and repair observed weaknesses, thereby driving policy-level co-evolution across training iterations. Experiments show that MAS-Loop achieves the strongest overall performance among the evaluated baselines, with consistent gains from refinement across all evaluated benchmarks. The co-evolution process also yields MAS-JudgeCorpus, a dataset of execution-grounded source-updated pairs with orchestration- and trajectory-level supervision, which can provide the community with support for MAS reward modeling and trajectory-aware investigation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Division of Labor: How Roles Shape Feedback and Recovery in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03931)**  `arXiv:2611.03931`  `cs.MA`  
  _Chih-Hsuan Yang, Xinyang Li, Rajeev Thakur, Feng Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Roles in multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems allocate decisions as well as work. In a Planner--Executor--Reviewer team, the Reviewer's approval can determine when the team submits an answer and receives corrective feedback. We study both functions by varying role instructions while holding agents, topology, call budget, and ground-truth evaluator constant, using three open-weight models on Omni-MATH and two on SciBench. Removing roles largely eliminates functional specialization without lowering first-pass accuracy. After a failed first answer, however, submission decisions matter: a role-structured Reviewer often withholds approval, so its team submits less often and receives fewer evaluator verdicts. In an exploratory comparison with feedback available to both teams, the role-free team recovers more often across all five model--benchmark settings. Two further analyses support the importance of feedback opportunities. In a post hoc comparison at equal numbers of verdicts, the pooled recovery difference is small (role-structured minus role-free: $+0.009$, 95% interval $[-0.014,+0.033]$). In an exploratory intervention on $400$ Omni-MATH problems with gemma-4, keeping the role prompts but forcing submission in every cycle raises recovery from $0.198$ to $0.613$; the paired comparison detects no difference from the role-free team. The takeaway is to evaluate roles through recovery after failure as well as specialization. For systems with reliable external feedback, this requires measuring who may submit, how often the team receives feedback, and which agents can act on it.
  </details>

- **[Federated Transfer Learning for Medical Image Classification under Mixed Skew](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05035)**  `arXiv:2611.05035`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Alexandre G. Leclercq, Sébastien Bougleux, Aurélien Corroyer-Dulmont, Romain Hérault_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning in medical imaging is constrained by data scarcity and by privacy regulations that make centralizing patient data across institutions impractical. Federated Learning (FL) addresses these constraints by keeping data local, but its distributed nature exposes training to strong statistical heterogeneity. Multicentric medical data is particularly affected, exhibiting a mixed skew that combines feature, label, concept, and quantity shifts across centers. Personalized FL mitigates heterogeneity by tailoring a model to each client, yet most methods assume training from scratch, which is unrealistic given the high dimensionality of medical features relative to the limited data available. We propose the Personalized Aligner Federated Pretrained (PAFP) framework, a personalized FL approach built on a pre-trained backbone. PAFP inserts client-specific Feature Alignment modules before the fine-tuned blocks to realign hidden feature distributions toward a server-aggregated target. On the FedISIC benchmark, PAFP attains the highest unweighted balanced accuracy while reducing inter-client performance variance roughly six-fold relative to the strongest baseline, yielding accuracy and cross-client equity jointly. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Strong Reasoners Join the Pool: Small Union Premiums and Costly Runaway Generations in LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04098)**  `arXiv:2611.04098`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Teng-Ruei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Routing benchmarks often judge a router against a hindsight oracle that counts a query as solved whenever any model's recorded answer is correct, but under stochastic decoding that oracle mixes two different objects: the clairvoyant ceiling of committing to one model per query and the premium of calling several models and keeping any correct answer. A companion study measured this premium at 3.5 percentage points (pp) for 11 small open models on MATH-500; how large it is once current reasoning models enter the pool was unknown. We measure it with a fixed-protocol, pre-committed generation on a single NVIDIA B300: all 500 MATH-500 problems, 30 seeded samples per problem, Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and NVIDIA's Nemotron Nano 9B v2 and Nemotron Super 49B v1.5 (a NAS derivative of the same 70B) under vendor sampling, a 65,536-token cap, a pinned Math-Verify scorer, and an append-only attempt ledger, for 45,000 scored responses. The two reasoning models both reach 96.1% accuracy against 76.2% for the 70B baseline. The empirical product-coupling premium is 0.215-0.588 pp across all two- and three-model pools; the empirical plug-in selection headroom over always calling Nano is 1.27 pp; and resampling Nano three times gives practically the same empirical any-correct rate as calling all three models once. Only 31 responses reached the cap, 25 of them with exactly periodic tails, yet the capped responses account for up to 60.2% of a model's output-length-squared proxy, and long generations made a fourth planned model, Llama-3.1-8B, too slow to finish. The premium is far below the companion's 3.5 pp, but models, pool size and protocol changed together; an oracle gap should therefore be reported with the pool, the protocol, the samples per cell and the decoding budget that produced it.
  </details>

- **[Replay for LLM Continual Learning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02746)**  `arXiv:2611.02746`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqiao Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) distills several domain experts into one student and is used in the post-training of recent large language models. It trains on all domains at once, so every expert must be available at the same time. In practice, capabilities often arrive one after another, and the experts of earlier domains may be gone when a new domain is learned. Distilling the experts in sequence then suffers from catastrophic forgetting of the earlier domains. Some recent pipelines store a snapshot after every stage and end with a multi-teacher recovery stage taught by all snapshots. The number of stored models then grows with the number of stages. Different from these pipelines, this paper formulates continual MOPD by what is kept from earlier stages and studies replay toward the previous student. Inside every stage, prompts of earlier domains are replayed with the student of the previous stage as their teacher, so neither earlier experts nor stage snapshots are kept. Two design choices of replay are explored. The replay budget of a stage is either shared among the earlier domains or fixed per earlier domain. Replay steps either distill the token distribution alone or add alignment of hidden states, region-pooled self-attention scores and value relations. A five-domain benchmark of logic, math, code, safety and instruction following is built from public experts of one 1.5B student. Experiments on its 14 benchmarks show that, without storing any snapshot, replay toward the previous student closes most of the gap between sequential distillation and joint MOPD. With a fixed replay share per earlier domain, it performs on par with recovery from all stored snapshots.
  </details>

- **[Imitation Dominates Reinforcement: Direct In-Context RL Is Closer to ICL Than RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11152)**  `arXiv:2610.11152`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Minchan Kwon, Seunghee Koh, Sunghyun Baek, Minsung Bae, Junmo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly improve at inference time by accumulating experience in context rather than by updating parameters. This process is often described as in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL). Whether in-context learning (ICL) can actually play the role of RL, however, has not been tested. We study this question in its simplest form, direct ICRL, where the model conditions directly on raw trajectory-reward pairs, and ask whether the reward acts as a learning signal. Through controlled experiments on four benchmarks across six models, we find that the reward is read, but its effect is small: flipping, randomizing, or removing the reward leaves the improvement curve almost unchanged, and this holds even under meta-prompts that explicitly instruct the model to explore, exploit, or reason over rewards. Trajectories drive improvement, but not through their semantic content: shuffled or corrupted trajectories work as well as real ones. These patterns closely mirror those known in ICL, suggesting that direct ICRL is better understood as a special case of ICL than as inference-time RL. This reframing has implications for agent memory design: ICL factors such as input distribution and demonstrations may matter more than RL elements such as reward shaping and exploration.
  </details>

- **[An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301)**  `arXiv:2611.03301`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Flow Equivariant World Models: Structured Memory for Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01075)**  `arXiv:2601.01075`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hansen Jin Lillemark, Benhao Huang, Fangneng Zhan, Yilun Du, Thomas Anderson Keller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied systems experience the world as 'a symphony of flows': a combination of many continuous streams of sensory input coupled to self-motion, interwoven with the dynamics of external objects. These sensory streams and the underlying dynamics of the world obey smooth, time-parameterized symmetries which existing world models ignore. Without a memory that respects this structure, partial observability presents a major obstacle to existing methods: each observation reveals only a fraction of the world, while unobserved regions continue to evolve. In this work, we introduce Flow Equivariant World Modeling, a framework that leverages time-parameterized symmetries within a latent memory for stable and accurate dynamics prediction over long horizons. The latent memory shifts and transforms equivariantly with self-motion and inferred external object motion, keeping information about out-of-view regions aligned as time progresses. We demonstrate the advantage of this framework over state-of-the-art diffusion, memory-augmented, and recurrent world model architectures on 2D and 3D partially observed video world modeling benchmarks. More broadly, our results suggest that predictive representations become more powerful when they are organized in line with the temporal and dynamical structure of the world they model. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Coarse Semantic Blocks for Efficient Training-Free Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04313)**  `arXiv:2611.04313`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sosuke Kobayashi, Shoichiro Yamaguchi, Seiya Tokui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a training-free sparse attention method that builds a prompt-specific key index to accelerate both prefill and decoding. The method clusters the keys into large semantic blocks that group tokens from noncontiguous positions. Each query position selects blocks by scoring their centroids and attends to the original keys and values within them. Queries that select the same block are processed together, so prefill stays fast even though each query chooses its own blocks. Large blocks make centroid scoring and this grouped processing cheap, so each query can attend to more tokens in the same time. Larger blocks with a larger token budget can thus be both faster and more accurate on long-context tasks than smaller blocks with a smaller budget. We evaluate two hybrid LLMs, Qwen3.5-4B and 35B-A3B, on one NVIDIA H200. Including the cost of building and using the index, whole-model wall-clock speedups over dense attention are 2.3-2.4x for prefill and 1.7-2.0x for decoding at 512K tokens, and 2.9-3.0x for prefill at 1M. The 35B-A3B model stays close to dense attention on five RULER tasks through 1M, on LongBench-v2 overall, and on MRCR through 256K. The 4B model loses accuracy mainly on multi-key retrieval, yet outperforms dense Qwen3.5-2B, whose latency is similar at 512K, on all three benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Lotus: Learning to Understand Time-series by Learning to Forecast](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18877)**  `arXiv:2610.18877`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Van Dai Do, Huu Hiep Nguyen, Minh Hoang Nguyen, Manh Nguyen, Hung Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series language models typically align numerical signals with language using labels, captions, or question-answer pairs, without explicitly requiring their representations to preserve how the underlying signals evolve. We introduce forecast grounding, a pretraining principle that requires language-aligned time-series representations to remain predictive of the future evolution before they are adapted to downstream tasks. We instantiate this principle in Lotus, which divides each time-series channel into non-overlapping patches and maps them into the LLM embedding space. It then predicts the next patch from the LLM hidden states of the time-series tokens, encouraging the learned representation to retain temporal information. To capture both temporal changes and periodic patterns, we also propose a temporal-spectral patch representation that combines each raw time-series patch with its spectral representation before mapping it into the LLM embedding space. Across three language model backbones and six downstream evaluation datasets, Lotus achieves the best average performance with minimal trainable parameters, reaching 59.91% accuracy and outperforming the strongest matched baseline by 3.36%. Our representation analyses further show that Lotus uses the numerical input and preserves temporal structure in its learned patch representations. These results establish forecast grounding as a promising pretraining principle, enabling compact time-series language models to outperform models 25-50 times their size on TSRBench. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Rethinking GA2Ms: performance and out-of-distribution generalisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05012)**  `arXiv:2611.05012`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _George Dimitriadis, Damian Machlanski, Paul Clarke, Spyridon Samothrakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalised Additive Models (GAMs) are a cornerstone of interpretable machine learning, offering transparent predictions through structured additive decompositions. Despite advances from classical GAMs to modern neural and spline-based variants, two notable gaps remain. First, comprehensive benchmarking of spline-based GAMs on large-scale tabular learning tasks, the dominant modality in applied settings, is lacking. Second, their robustness under distribution shift, a setting where structured inductive biases should confer advantages, remains unexplored. We propose a multi-resolution, higher-order Composite Generalised Additive Model (CoGA2M) and evaluate it on the TALENT benchmark suite. CoGA2M ranks in the top third across all baselines (29 for regression, 32 for classification), establishing competitive performance with state-of-the-art tabular methods. We further extend TALENT with out-of-distribution (OOD) splits exhibiting train-test distributional shift and benchmark CoGA2M against LightGBM, a consistently top-performing model on TALENT. Under distribution shift, CoGA2M outperforms LightGBM, demonstrating a clear structural advantage. Our results position spline-based GAMs as strong contenders for tabular prediction and reveal a previously unrecognised benefit of additive structure in the OOD regime.
  </details>

- **[Fault-tolerant shallow recurrent decoders for sparse sensing with missing sensor measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04893)**  `arXiv:2611.04893`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Matteo Romilio Pasqual, Matteo Tomasetto, J. Nathan Kutz, Andrea Manzoni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world sensing systems are inherently imperfect: sensors may fail, intermittently disconnect, operate with asynchronous sampling rates, and be subject to noise, leading to measurement streams that are irregular, incomplete, and heterogeneous. Nevertheless, sensing strategies for real-time state reconstruction typically assume synchronous and continuously active sensors, a constraint that substantially limits their applicability in operational environments. In this work, we introduce fault-tolerant sparse sensing strategies in the context of shallow recurrent decoders (SHRED) to enable high-dimensional state estimation from sparse, asynchronous, and missing sensor measurements. Specifically, we consider naive imputation strategies, fault-tolerant recurrent temporal encoders, and we introduce four different transformer-based masking mechanisms to make SHRED explicitly aware of sensor availability through the binary data-availability mask. Moreover, we further introduce a multi-step decoding training objective for SHRED, resulting in smoother latent trajectories and more robust predictions. Throughout a synthetic benchmark and three real-world problems in hydrodynamic and ocean modeling, we show that (i) transformer-based masking mechanisms outperform standard imputation techniques and fault-tolerant recurrent architectures, (ii) the multi-step decoding training objective enhances reconstruction fidelity and robustness in the presence of heterogeneous missing-data patterns, while (iii) the computational efficiency of SHRED is preserved, with no additional inference-time overhead.
  </details>

- **[TAPES: Task-Aligned Joint Predictive Pretraining for Transaction Event Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04747)**  `arXiv:2611.04747`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruobing Zuo, Weiyu Han, Huilin He, Haoyu Jiang, Ze Zhao, Jie Sheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transaction event sequences support diverse prediction tasks in online commerce and digital financial services. The same transaction records can yield prediction targets with different semantics, including values, changes, and relations to past events. These targets impose different constraints on history representations, but their predictability alone does not establish the downstream utility of a target set. Moreover, target-wise prediction losses do not explicitly model conditional co-occurrence among targets. To address these challenges, we propose TAPES, a task-aligned joint predictive pretraining framework for transaction sequences. TAPES constructs a finite collection of candidate target sets from admissible field-operator combinations. It selects a target set according to the downstream utility of representations learned through short joint pretraining trials under matched budgets. It then reinitializes the encoder and pretrains it on the selected targets through a joint likelihood parameterized by a shared discrete latent variable, before downstream supervised adaptation. Experiments in a real-world transaction-risk setting and on multiple public financial benchmarks spanning diverse downstream tasks demonstrate that TAPES outperforms representative baselines. Ablation studies further validate the contributions of predictive target selection and joint modeling.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04689)**  `arXiv:2611.04689`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinwu Hu, Langyu Bian, Jian Sun, Dongjin Yang, Chao Shuai, Yufeng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-set test-time adaptation (TTA) aims to adapt source-trained models to unlabeled test streams where covariate-shifted in-distribution (csID) samples and covariate-shifted out-of-distribution (csOOD) samples may coexist. Existing TTA methods are typically designed under a closed-set assumption and therefore lack explicit mechanisms for reliably distinguishing csID from csOOD samples. Consequently, csOOD samples may be mistakenly included in confidence-enhancing adaptation, producing harmful gradients that degrade known-class performance. To address this, we propose Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation (UAPA), a plug-and-play method for reliable open-set adaptation. Specifically, we first introduce Uncertainty-Driven ID/OOD Separation, which combines a classifier-prototype score with a mini-batch-normalized evidence-derived uncertainty score to distinguish csID from csOOD samples. We then propose a Dynamic Prototype Refinement strategy that periodically refines class-specific target prototypes using buffered csID features to progressively follow the estimated class directions. Lastly, we introduce Contrastive Prototype Entropy Adaptation, which applies entropy minimization to csID samples and entropy maximization to csOOD samples, while prototype-level contrastive learning aligns csID features with their corresponding target prototypes and reduces the maximum similarity between csOOD features and known-class prototypes. Empirically, UAPA outperforms the strongest baselines on the CIFAR benchmarks and Tiny-ImageNet-C, improving average OSCR by 2.08 and 1.28 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[SaMA: Morpho Adaptation via Asymmetric Expansion of Kronecker Product](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04462)**  `arXiv:2611.04462`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _An Nguyen, Anh Tong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has attracted significant attention in the parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) landscape. However, its design suffers from two fundamental limitations. First, its invariance manifold induces flat directions in the loss landscape, creating an optimization bottleneck for adaptive optimizers. Second, its effective rank scales only linearly with the parameter budget. In this paper, we propose Scalable Morpho Adaptation (SaMA), a PEFT method grounded in a principled generalization of the Kronecker product. By relaxing the shared-block constraint in the perfect-shuffle factorization, SaMA spans from low to full rank and achieves a practical rank that scales quadratically with the parameter budget, up to full-rank. Furthermore, we show that its invariance manifold collapses to a diagonal group, making it structurally more optimizer-friendly. Empirically, SaMA consistently outperforms strong PEFT baselines on commonsense and arithmetic reasoning benchmarks while being more parameter-efficient.
  </details>

- **[What Is the Influence of Task Order in Continual Learning? An Empirical Study and Order-Aware Evaluation for Motion Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04309)**  `arXiv:2611.04309`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Li, Yunlong Lin, Haohan Yang, Xiaocong Zhao, Chen Lv_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aggregate scores for continual learning algorithms rest on a distribution over task orders that is rarely stated. We formulate algorithm comparison as an expectation over a declared order distribution, which separates training variation from order variation, and apply it to motion forecasting with four road-layout tasks from the INTERACTION dataset, all 24 task orders, four algorithms, and three training replicates (288 runs). Task order accounts for 65\% to 91\% of the variation in current-task error and backward transfer, and 90\% to 99\% of this order effect comes from the identity of the starting task: the smallest task cannot be learned from a cold start. Beyond the first stage, the order effect is comparable to training variability. In most orders, the leading algorithm in current-task error and backward transfer is within one replicate standard deviation of the runner-up. Backward transfer depends on the order mainly through its reference term, which weights the first task most heavily, so relative improvement and final error identify different leading algorithms for displacement error. A transition-direction descriptor computed from task statistics tracks the position of the smallest task, and weighting orders by it leaves the algorithm ranking unchanged. These results call for benchmarks that declare their order distribution and report order-resolved, starting-task-resolved, warm-start, and final errors alongside aggregate scores. The code and data are available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[Multi-layer Indexer: Overlapping Selection and Attention in DeepSeek Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03768)**  `arXiv:2611.03768`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Serdarcan Dilbaz, Arjun Krishna, Kiran Kamble, Daniel M. Bikel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention cuts the cost of long-context inference by making the attention matrix sparse: each query attends only a subset of the sequence, chosen by a fixed pattern or, in recent production LLMs, learned per query. In DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA), the attention of DeepSeek-V3.2 and the GLM-5 family, every layer is preceded by a lightning indexer that scores the whole cache and keeps the k=2048 highest-scoring tokens for the layer to attend. At long context this indexer scan, not the sparse attention it feeds, is where the decode time goes. But adjacent layers select nearly the same tokens, so a layer can attend the selection of the previous indexer layer instead of its own. This removes the dependency: each indexer then runs concurrently with the attention of its layer. The resulting Multi-layer Indexer (MLI) needs no retraining and no new weights. Its default realization changes no kernel, running inside SGLang's production decode graph; an optional fused decode kernel accelerates it further on DeepSeek-V3.2. MLI composes with IndexCache (the cross-layer reuse GLM-5.2 ships as IndexShare): IndexCache drops the indexer from some layers, and MLI hides the scan on the rest. We serve DeepSeek-V3.2 and GLM-5 under IndexCache's protocol at retentions 1/2 to 1/32 (the fraction of layers that keep an indexer) and contexts 10K to 195K. At equal retention the composition decodes faster than IndexCache: up to 14.5% faster on DeepSeek-V3.2 and 9.8% on GLM-5 at 1/2, and 7.9% and 5.1% at 1/4, largest where IndexCache keeps the most indexers and growing with context. At retention 1/r it is faster still than IndexCache at 1/(2r) while keeping twice the fresh selections: at 128K it reaches 1.32x the unmodified model's decode throughput at 1/4, above IndexCache's 1.28x at 1/8. Its LongBench, RULER and LongBench v2 accuracy stays within a few percentage points of IndexCache's.
  </details>
