- **[DIKT: A Diagnostic and Interpretable Knowledge Tracing Framework for Prediction and Model-based Learning Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02774)**  `arXiv:2611.02774`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Qiang Wu, Shaohui Chen, Hanlin Li, Baolin Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge tracing is a key component of intelligent educational systems. However, most existing methods mainly focus on response prediction, providing limited support for explaining predicted outcomes and improving students' learning states. This paper proposes Diagnostic and Interpretable Knowledge Tracing (DIKT), which integrates response prediction, skill-level diagnosis, and model-based learning analysis. DIKT generates bounded cognitive-state trajectories and decomposes the final response probability into skill-wise probabilities. The trajectories reveal students' evolving strengths and weaknesses, while the skill-wise probabilities identify the skills that most strongly constrain the prediction. Based on these diagnostic results, DIKT supports direct cognitive-state intervention and effective-learning-duration-based what-if analysis to examine how hypothetical adjustments affect current predictions and subsequent state evolution. DIKT combines Bayesian multi-head attention, conditional variational recurrent inference, a logistic-growth decoder, and multiplicative skill-wise prediction. Experiments on three benchmark datasets show that DIKT outperforms all evaluated baselines, with an average absolute AUC gain of 2.04 percentage points. Other experiments further demonstrate that DIKT supports educationally meaningful diagnostic analysis and provides informative diagnostic indicators for downstream applications, extending knowledge tracing from response prediction to interpretable diagnosis and model-based learning analysis.
  </details>

- **[CIS-Transolver: A Cross-layer Incremental-State Neural Operator for Fine-Scale Automotive Aerodynamic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26337)**  `arXiv:2610.26337`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianglong Liu, Zhicheng Zhu, Jia Hao, Liangyue Jia, Wenxuan He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators offer an efficient data-driven approach to predicting complex automotive aerodynamic flows. However, spectral bias limits their ability to reconstruct fine-scale flow variations around geometric features such as side mirrors and wheel arches. Reliable assessment of local geometric changes therefore requires models that capture fine spatial detail together with global flow interactions. Inspired by iterative refinement and state evolution, we propose CIS-Transolver, a cross-layer incremental-state neural operator that integrates local multiscale information into global flow modeling. The method extracts local feature variations and encodes them as zeroth- to second-order geometric moments. These encodings are combined with information from earlier layers to form a cross-layer memory. Memory-derived corrections enter the value branch of Physics-Attention, allowing local details to inform global physical-state interactions throughout the network. We systematically evaluate CIS-Transolver on three public automotive aerodynamics benchmarks, ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, covering surface pressure fields and volumetric pressure and velocity fields. CIS-Transolver achieves the lowest mean relative L2 error among the evaluated methods for all five prediction metrics across the three benchmarks. Relative error reductions average 8.72% compared with the best baseline for each metric. Also achieves the lowest high-frequency spectral errors across all five metrics, with an average relative reduction of 16.06% compared with the best baseline. These results support cross-layer reuse of local information for fine-scale automotive flow prediction.
  </details>

- **[Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21375)**  `arXiv:2610.21375`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.
  </details>

- **[CausalBind: Causal Modeling and Learning for Protein-Molecule Virtual Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07340)**  `arXiv:2610.07340`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Loka Li, Jin Tian, Kun Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein-molecule virtual screening is increasingly cast as a problem of representation learning in a shared embedding space. Existing methods rely on dense holistic alignment, entangling invariant binding determinants with nuisance correlations and limiting transfer to new targets. Binding in protein-molecule systems involves sparse cross-modality interactions: binding is governed by a small contact interface and a few decisive local interactions (e.g., hydrogen bonds, hydrophobic contacts, and salt bridges) rather than the global structures of the protein and molecule. We hypothesize that uncovering and leveraging sparse interaction patterns is critical for generalization beyond the training data, as these patterns are reusable and expected to improve performance across different scenarios. Since the training data contain only observed binding pairs, we formalize this prior via a V-structure causal model under Heckman-style selection, and establish three theoretical results: (i) when the compatibility between concepts is bilinear, the latent concepts of interacting proteins and molecules are not identifiable, even if the interaction is sparse; (ii) with a non-bilinear compatibility function, these concepts and their sparse interaction support are component-wise identifiable under a sparse antichain condition; and (iii) a low-rank relaxation yields subspace identifiability of the interaction modes, and applies to bilinear compatibility as well. Inspired by these principles, we propose CausalBind with three implementation variants. Extensive experiments on DUD-E and LIT-PCBA benchmarks show that all variants consistently outperform strong retrieval baselines, with the largest gains on LIT-PCBA early enrichment, and further generalize to target- and scaffold-level out-of-distribution splits. Code and scripts are freely available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Neglected Free Lunch from Post-training: Progress Advantage for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26080)**  `arXiv:2606.26080`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Changdae Oh, Wendi Li, Seongheon Park, Samuel Yeh, Tanwi Mallick, Sharon Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process reward models enable fine-grained, step-level evaluation of LLMs, yet building them for agentic settings remains prohibitively difficult: long-horizon interactions, irreversible actions, and stochastic environment feedback make both human annotation and Monte Carlo estimation infeasible at scale. In this work, we show that reinforcement learning (RL) post-training already provides the ingredients for effective step-level scoring, eliminating the need for dedicated reward model training altogether. Concretely, we derive an implicit advantage under a general stochastic Markov decision process, which we term progress advantage -- log-probability ratio between the RL-trained policy and its reference policy exactly recovers the optimal advantage function. This formulation makes the resulting signal annotation-free, domain-agnostic, and available as a byproduct of the standard RL post-training pipeline. We validate the effectiveness of the progress advantage across three different applications: test-time scaling, uncertainty quantification, and failure attribution on five benchmarks and four model families. Across all settings, it consistently outperforms confidence-based baselines and, despite requiring no task-specific training, surpasses dedicated trained reward models. We complement these results with deeper analyses on characteristics of progress advantage, offering practical guidance for adoption in real-world agentic systems.
  </details>

- **[Robust Learning under Non-Gaussian Noise with Adaptive Log-Correntropy Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16050)**  `arXiv:2606.16050`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mainak Kundu, Ria Kanjilal, Ismail Uysal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust deep learning under heavy-tailed and impulsive noise remains challenging because conventional losses such as mean squared error (MSE) exhibit unbounded sensitivity to outliers. Although correntropy-based objectives improve robustness, many conventional formulations rely on fixed kernel parameters that must be empirically tuned and remain static during training. To address these limitations, we propose an Adaptive Log-Correntropy Loss (ALCL), a heavy-tailed loss formulation that adaptively learns its robustness geometry during optimization. ALCL introduces a logarithmic residual model whose shape and scale parameters are learned jointly with network weights through differentiable reparameterization. This yields a heavy-tailed robust optimization objective whose psi-function is formally bounded and redescending, allowing the loss geometry to adapt dynamically to evolving residual statistics while suppressing extreme outliers. Comparative experiments on four widely used benchmark datasets spanning grayscale and red-green-blue (RGB) image data under mixed heavy-tailed and impulsive noise demonstrate that ALCL consistently outperforms MSE and optimally tuned generalized correntropy losses in both reconstruction fidelity and downstream classification accuracy. While performance differences remain small under low-noise conditions, under high-noise regimes ALCL improves median accuracy by up to 4.75% on grayscale benchmarks and 4.51% on RGB datasets, with reduced variance across runs. These results demonstrate that adaptive robustness through joint learning of loss parameters provides a computationally efficient alternative to static correntropy-based losses for deep learning in non-Gaussian environments.
  </details>

- **[HISA: Efficient Hierarchical Indexing for Fine-Grained Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28458)**  `arXiv:2603.28458`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yufei Xu, Fanxu Meng, Fan Jiang, Yuxuan Wang, Ruijie Zhou, Zhaohui Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Token-level sparse attention mechanisms, exemplified by DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA), achieve fine-grained key selection by scoring every historical key for each query through a lightweight indexer, then computing attention only on the selected subset. While the downstream sparse attention itself scales favorably, the indexer must still scan the entire prefix for every query, introducing an per-layer bottleneck that grows prohibitively with context length. We propose HISA (Hierarchical Indexed Sparse Attention), a plug-and-play replacement for the indexer that rewrites the search path from a flat token scan into a two-stage hierarchical procedure: (1) a block-level coarse filtering stage that scores pooled block representations to discard irrelevant regions, followed by (2) a token-level refinement stage that applies the original indexer exclusively within the retained candidate blocks. HISA preserves the identical token-level top-sparse pattern consumed by the downstream Sparse MLA operator and requires no additional training. On kernel-level benchmarks, HISA achieves up to speedup at 64K context. On Needle-in-a-Haystack and LongBench, we directly replace the indexer in DeepSeek-V3.2 and GLM-5 with our HISA indexer, without any finetuning. HISA closely matches the original DSA in quality, while substantially outperforming block-sparse baselines.
  </details>

- **[FatigueFormer: A Physiology-Informed Model for Upper-Limb Muscle Fatigue Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26841)**  `arXiv:2603.26841`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tong Zhang, Hong Guo, Shuangzhou Yan, Dongkai Weng, Jian Wang, Hongxin Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surface electromyographic (sEMG) signals provide information about muscle activity and function during movement. Fatigue alters multiple properties of these signals, with patterns that vary across individuals and contraction intensities. However, physiological insights into fatigue remain underused in sEMG modeling, particularly for cross-subject recognition. We introduce FatigueFormer to recognize fatigue states defined by ratings of perceived exertion (RPE). The model incorporates physiologically motivated trend grouping into sEMG encoding by organizing descriptors into increasing- and decreasing-trend streams and combining inter-window context with within-window feature interactions. To assess fatigue-state discriminability across contraction intensities, we further present an sEMG fatigue dataset collected from 30 participants at four levels of maximum voluntary contraction (MVC) and benchmark representative machine learning and deep learning methods. We evaluate performance offline under leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) and within-subject protocols and use the SWIFTIES dataset for external validation. Results support FatigueFormer's effectiveness across the examined contraction intensities and both datasets, while indicating that fatigue-related cues vary across MVC levels. An exploratory adaptation analysis shows higher FatigueFormer scores with more labeled target-user data on complete target recordings, which include calibration samples.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Unsupervised Paradigm in Time Series Anomaly Detection: An Empirical Study and Minimalist Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16145)**  `arXiv:2511.16145`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhijie Zhong, Zhiwen Yu, Kaixiang Yang, Yongheng Liu, Jun Jiang, C. L. Philip Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) research predominantly focuses on unsupervised architectures to model normal data dynamics under label scarcity. However, practical deployments frequently archive historical operational incidents, questioning whether continuous unsupervised architectural scaling remains the most cost-effective pursuit. In this paper, we empirically quantify the trade-offs between supervisory signals and model complexity. We introduce STAND, a deliberately minimalist supervised baseline based on bidirectional recurrent encoding. Across five benchmark datasets evaluated under a strictly disjoint out-of-sample temporal protocol, minimalist supervised models achieve competitive or superior accuracy relative to complex unsupervised alternatives. Furthermore, controlled label-scaling and few-shot evaluations confirm that these supervisory advantages persist under constrained historical event budgets, while STAND exhibits higher prediction certainty and sharper event delineation than reconstruction-based baselines. These findings encourage the TSAD community to complement algorithmic complexity with data-centric label utilization when annotations are accessible. The source code, benchmark datasets, and an extended Supplementary Document are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Free Draft-and-Verification: Toward Lossless Parallel Decoding for Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00294)**  `arXiv:2510.00294`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shutong Wu, Jiawei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) have emerged as a compelling paradigm for language modeling, offering the unique potential for highly efficient inference via multi-token parallel decoding. However, unlocking this potential remains challenging. High-quality generation typically defaults to one-token-per-step static decoding, while existing parallel algorithms rely on heuristic confidence thresholds, introducing a fragile trade-off between decoding efficiency and generation quality. To overcome this, we introduce Free Draft-and-Verification (FreeDave), a novel training-free and model-free fast decoding algorithm. FreeDave capitalizes on the inherent properties of dLLMs by treating future token predictions as ``free drafts'' and rigorously self-verifying them in subsequent forward passes. Theoretically, FreeDave searches for a shorter decoding path that is guaranteed to reproduce the exact sequence generated by static decoding, achieving algorithmically lossless acceleration. With a sufficiently large draft budget and a verification frontier that does not retreat, it can use the fewest possible model forward passes among feasible paths. Extensive evaluations on math reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that FreeDave can boost the number of generated tokens per model forward (TPF) by up to 5.48$\times$ and tokens per second (TPS) by up to 3.94$\times$, while robustly maintaining generation quality and avoiding the task-specific performance degradation faced by heuristic methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SLogic: Subgraph-Informed Logical Rule Learning for Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00279)**  `arXiv:2510.00279`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Trung Hoang Le, Tran Cao Son, Ishtiaq Ahmed, Huiping Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical rule-based methods offer an interpretable approach to knowledge graph completion (KGC) by capturing compositional relationships in the form of human-readable inference rules. While existing logical rule-based methods learn rule confidence scores, they typically assign a global weight to each rule schema, applied uniformly across the graph. This is a significant limitation, as a rule's importance often varies depending on the specific query instance. To address this, we introduce SLogic (Subgraph-Informed Logical Rule learning), a novel framework that assigns query-dependent scores to logical rules. The core of SLogic is a context-aware scoring function. This framework is in line with the specificity principle in commonsense reasoning. This function determines the importance of a rule by analyzing the subgraph locally defined by the query's head entity, thereby enabling a differentiated weighting of rules specific to their local query contexts. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show that SLogic achieves competitive performance among rule-based methods. It also generates query-dependent, human-readable logical rules that serve as explicit explanations for its inferences.
  </details>

- **[$Φ$asmaNet: A Staged Spectral Token-Refinement Architecture for Fast Gravitational-Wave Signal-to-Noise Ratio Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05309)**  `arXiv:2611.05309`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ioannis Tsioulis, Anastasios Tefas, Nikolaos Stergioulas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantifying selection effects in gravitational-wave population studies requires millions of costly signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) calculations. We present $\Phi$asmaNet, a compact neural architecture for structured tabular regression, with staged spectral refinement as an explicit architectural prior. Learned input tokens are refined through ordered attention-based $\Phi$-Stages, each augmented by a $\Phi$-Refractor subspace-mixing module, then revisited through gated top-down $\Phi$-Feedback. Applied to the 11-dimensional parameters of precessing, higher-mode binary black hole signals (IMRPhenomXPHM), it predicts phase-averaged optimal SNR for a single detector at fixed Advanced LIGO design sensitivity. With approximately 732k parameters, it outperforms MLP, 1D-ResNet and Transformer-encoder baselines under matched training across $10^5$--$10^6$ samples. At $10^6$ samples, it achieves $100-\mathrm{MAPE}=98.73\%$ on the held-out test set, $99.29\%$ after rank-preserving monotonic (PCHIP) calibration, and $99.39\%$ ($R^2=0.9998$) in the detection-relevant regime $\rho>8$, with stable accuracy across independent dataset realizations. On a single GPU, it sustains approximately $1.2\times10^5$ SNR evaluations per second and processes a $10^6$-injection catalogue end-to-end in approximately 8.3 s, about two orders of magnitude faster than conventional waveform-and-inner-product calculations on a 16-core CPU. Exact distance rescaling through $\rho\propto1/d_L$ supports repeated catalogue-wide detectability evaluations. The fully documented pipeline is intended as a reproducible benchmark, while results on public tabular datasets indicate competitiveness beyond gravitational-wave applications. Although demonstrated for a fixed single-detector configuration, the construction supports extensions to detector networks and epoch-specific sensitivities. (Abridged)
  </details>

- **[Beyond Foundation Models: PFN Priors Improve Tabular Hyperparameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05141)**  `arXiv:2611.05141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Artem Chistyakov, Renat Sergazinov, Artem Babenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic priors have so far served one purpose: pretraining tabular foundation models. We ask whether the same priors can improve conventional tabular models, which are trained from scratch on each dataset. We propose PriorTune, a method that meta-tunes hyperparameter configurations on synthetic tasks alone. Offline, for a given model, we fit a pool of configurations on datasets sampled from a published PFN prior and greedily select the subset with the highest winrate over the random baseline. Online, a new dataset fits only the selected configurations, so the gain comes at no extra cost. Empirically, we observe that scaling both the number of configurations and synthetic datasets has an equal scaling effect on the performance of the final subset of configurations, which we also explain theoretically. On the full BeyondArena benchmark, LightGBM with TabPrep features with our configurations ranks above TabPFN-3 and RealMLP, while being much more efficient at inference. Overall, our results establish existing PFN priors as a scalable source of meta-training data for hyperparameter selection for non-foundational tabular models.
  </details>

- **[Modulating Merging Strengths via Joint Loss Estimation for LoRA-based Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04931)**  `arXiv:2611.04931`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kun Gu, De Cheng, Zhipeng Xu, Lingfeng He, Di Xu, Nannan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning (CL) aims to adapt models to new tasks while preserving previously learned knowledge. Recently, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), a representative Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method, has gained increasing attention in CL due to its efficiency and scalability. Several LoRA-based merging methods maintain a single inference model by scaling the newly learned LoRA update with one coefficient and merging it into the previous weights after each task. However, they usually control the whole update with a shared scalar factor, although different update elements may affect previous and current tasks in different ways. To address this issue, we propose Merging Strength Modulation via Joint Loss Estimation (M2LE). It consists of Hessian Information-guided Adaptive Merging (HIM) and Task Simulation Perturbation for Merging (TSP). Instead of searching for a global merge coefficient, HIM directly solves for the target merged update under a joint objective over previous and current tasks. Solving this objective gives an analytic solution that naturally takes the form of element-wise modulation over the current task LoRA update. To improve the stability of the learned update under subsequent merging, we introduce TSP which simulates different tasks interference. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that M2LE consistently outperforms existing PEFT-based continual learning methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Anomaly Detection on Resource-Constrained Devices with Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04835)**  `arXiv:2611.04835`  `cs.LG`  
  _Marco Angioli, Mathias Persson, Hiroto Tamura, Hadi Banaee, Amy Loutfi, Gouhei Tanaka, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection at the edge requires the deployment of models that achieve competitive performance, while minimizing computational cost and memory footprint. In this article, we investigate the suitability of the Mahalanobis Distance of Reservoir States (MDRS), an unsupervised time-series anomaly detection method, for resource-constrained devices and propose two optimizations to reduce its computational requirements. Specifically, we replace random reservoir connectivity (MDRS-Rand) with a circular shift (MDRS-Circ) and show that the anomaly score for an entire time series can be computed from the final reservoir state alone. Furthermore, we show that exploiting the reservoir's temporal memory enables more efficient input preprocessing, further reducing computational cost. We evaluate the MDRS variants on the MLPerf Tiny anomaly detection benchmark against the reference autoencoder and other anomaly detection methods, and deploy their inference kernels on a NUCLEO-F401RE microcontroller to assess latency, estimate energy consumption, and measure memory footprint. Among the evaluated methods, only MDRS and the autoencoder meet the benchmark's performance target, with MDRS requiring fewer operations and smaller models. With optimized preprocessing, MDRS achieves an ROC AUC of 0.869, a model size of 24 KB, an estimated energy consumption of 24 uJ per inference, and a latency of 0.32 ms, corresponding to a 75.9x speedup over the autoencoder with standard preprocessing.
  </details>

- **[Koopman Generative Operators for Efficient Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04760)**  `arXiv:2611.04760`  `cs.LG`  
  _Raz Marshanski, Liran Nochumsohn, Mayank Jauhari, Boris N. Oreshkin, Omri Azencot_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic time-series forecasting requires models that simultaneously capture structured temporal dynamics, expressive uncertainty, and efficient inference, yet existing approaches fall short of this goal: latent dynamical models impose structure but rely on restrictive generation mechanisms, while modern generative methods such as diffusion and flow matching achieve flexibility at the cost of iterative and computationally expensive sampling. We introduce the Koopman Generative Operator (KGO), a probabilistic forecasting method that conceptualizes prediction as the evolution of structured uncertainty. KGO integrates three core components: (1) Koopman Patch Embedding (KoPE) for temporally consistent latent trajectory extrapolation; (2) Koopman Flow Matching (KoFM), which enables fast, single-step generation through a closed-form matrix exponential in a Koopman latent space, bypassing iterative sampling; and (3) an Adaptive Uncertainty Gate (AUG), which provides probabilistic forecasts by adapting uncertainty per-variable and per-horizon, corresponding to a learned decomposition of aleatoric uncertainty without manual tuning. By unifying these components, KGO achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on 13/17 ProbTS benchmarks in CRPS and 16/17 in NMAE. Further, by eliminating iterative sampling, KGO delivers at least a 25$\times$ reduction in inference time compared to iterative generative models. These results position KGO as a practical and principled framework that advances the accuracy-efficiency trade-off in probabilistic time-series forecasting, combining leading accuracy on the evaluated benchmarks with fast, single-step generation.
  </details>

- **[Sheaf-MPNN: A Library for Sheaf Neural Networks as Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04670)**  `arXiv:2611.04670`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alessio Borgi, Mario Severino, Luke Braithwaite, Steve Azzolin, Rémi Bourgerie, Olga Zaghen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) implicitly assume that neighboring nodes share the same feature space, which contributes to oversmoothing and poor performance on heterophilic graphs. Sheaf Neural Networks (SNNs) remove this assumption by giving each node and edge its own vector space and learning restriction maps that transform features as they move between neighbors. These maps define a sheaf Laplacian that drives diffusion over the graph, but existing implementations often assemble it explicitly as a global sparse block matrix, which adds memory and computational overhead and can hinder scalability. In this paper, we introduce Sheaf-MPNN, a PyTorch Geometric library that reformulates sheaf diffusion as local incidence-wise transport and aggregation, eliminating the need to construct the global Laplacian. Sheaf-MPNN implements fifteen families of SNNs under a unified message-passing framework, each supporting both homogeneous and heterogeneous graphs, and directed and undirected settings, with diagonal, general, orthogonal, and low-rank restriction maps and attention-based predictors. We further provide a unified benchmarking suite covering node- and graph-level tasks, heterogeneous graph learning, and computational efficiency, enabling systematic and reproducible comparisons across architectures. We establish theoretical equivalence between these message-passing formulations and their original counterparts and identify the architectural mechanisms needed to preserve their propagation dynamics. This unified perspective provides a principled foundation for implementing, comparing, and extending sheaf-based architectures within standard graph learning frameworks. Source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571)**  `arXiv:2611.04571`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea Cenzato, Lukas Karch, Andre Luckow, Yoran Pichon, Francesco Pasqua, Abouzar Ghasemi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets.
  </details>

- **[SKILL2STEER: One-Pass Hypernetwork for Mapping Natural-Language Skills to Low-Rank Post-Block Activation Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04497)**  `arXiv:2611.04497`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ran Li, Lei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compiling a natural-language agent skill into model parameters removes the skill text from the prompt and leaves behind a small artifact that can be distributed and reused. Existing methods differ in where they inject the compiled quantity, and all of them assume that the artifact carries the content of the skill. We test that assumption. Through three controls on a retrieval agent, we find that a compiled skill shift carries a domain-general behavioural prior, not the content of the skill. A wrong skill document performs on par with the correct one, a single averaged bundle matches per-skill routing under a two-sided equivalence test, and a same-norm random direction collapses to the no-injection baseline, which shows that the trained direction stays load-bearing. We then introduce SKILL2STEER, a one-pass hypernetwork that reads a skill document once and maps it to low-rank activation shifts added at the output of every decoder block. Each skill compiles to 2.36M parameters, 9.25 times fewer than a weight-space adapter, while the base weights stay frozen and the generator runs only at compile time. The shift can be scheduled across retrieval steps and deployed as one routing-free bundle. On SearchQA, a seven-dataset benchmark, SKILL2STEER reaches 37.9 exact match, 9.8 points above the frozen model and 2.3 points above a weight-space method that ships 9.25 times more parameters per skill.
  </details>

- **[What Does 4-Bit Microscaling Cost a Wearable Activity Recognizer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04185)**  `arXiv:2611.04185`  `cs.LG`  
  _Megan Healy, Hossein Valavi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearable sports sensors can recognize actions on the device itself, where lower-precision numbers reduce model storage and memory traffic. Two 4-bit block-scaled formats, MXFP4 from the Open Compute Project, introduced in 2023, and NVFP4 from NVIDIA, introduced in 2025, are drawing interest. Published evaluations cover general deep-learning benchmarks and large language models; to our knowledge, nobody has yet measured what they cost a small wearable-sensor model.We train a 159,492-parameter 1D CNN to recognize four basketball actions from wrist-accelerometer data in Hang-Time HAR and test it on players it has never seen, using five-fold cross-validation grouped by player. Every comparison between number formats is made fold by fold.In full precision the model reaches 69.26\% macro-F1. Measured TFLite FP16 and INT8 models lose at most 0.16 percentage points. In simulation, 4-bit weights cost 1.45 points with MXFP4 and 0.72 points with NVFP4. When activations are quantized to 4 bits as well, MXFP4 loses 5.14 points and NVFP4 only 0.75, and NVFP4 comes out ahead in all five held-out-player folds.The ranking survives two alternative simulator settings, with NVFP4 leading by 3.49 to 4.40 points. A $2 \times 3$ ablation with 4-bit weights and activations (two block sizes and three scale rules) points to scale selection as the main source of MXFP4's loss. The reference MXFP4 conversion we simulate rounds the scale down, which can pick scales that are too small, so many blocks clip their largest values. Rounding the scale up recovers 3.18 points with 32-value blocks and 3.63 points with 16-value blocks, and switching to an 8-bit floating-point scale, as NVFP4 does, adds another 0.84 and 1.20 points. Block size matters less. Under this round-down rule, 16-value blocks score 0.44 points below 32-value blocks in all five folds; under the other two rules there is no consistent effect.
  </details>

- **[ModelBlaster: Closing the Performance Gap through Learning-Augmented Agents Across Models, Kernels and Hardware Backends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04104)**  `arXiv:2611.04104`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shengjun Kris Dong, Dima Nikiforov, Sahil Modi, Sana Damani, Edward C Lin, Siva Kumar Sastry Hari, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing accelerator code across hardware architectures is difficult. ML compilers search predefined spaces of schedules and micro-kernels, while LLM-guided optimization can explore alternative algorithms, operator decompositions, and hardware-specific techniques. However, existing LLM agents typically target a single programming interface, toolchain, or accelerator family, focus on kernel-level optimization without model-level transforms, and offer limited reuse of experience across hardware targets.We present ModelBlaster, a Memory-Augmented In-Context Reinforcement Learning (MAIC-RL) framework for optimizing data-parallel kernels and whole models across heterogeneous SIMD and SIMT accelerators. Its mutable policy is a persistent knowledge base updated at inference time by Actor-Critic Textual Gradient Descent (AC-TGD). The knowledge base maps performance states to actions, outcomes, and constraints, enabling experience reuse across kernels, models, and hardware targets. A model-level optimizer uses kernel-level knowledge to inform whole-model decisions. The same optimization loop and knowledge schema support CUDA on NVIDIA GPUs, OpenCL on Qualcomm GPUs, and RVV intrinsics on RISC-V vector cores, with only lowering and measurement layers specific to each target.Compared with PyTorch, ModelBlaster achieves geometric-mean speedups of 1.85x, 5.58x, and 5.18x on KernelBench Levels 1, 2, and 3, and 1.69x on SOL-ExecBench Levels 1 and 2 on a B200 GPU. On Qualcomm Adreno GPUs, it achieves 5.8x over the vendor SDK and 1.7x over Apache TVM; on RISC-V vector cores, it achieves 2.7x over ExecuTorch with XNNPACK across 70 KernelBench problems.
  </details>

- **[Structured Priors: Towards an algebra of component priors for prior-data fitted networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04056)**  `arXiv:2611.04056`  `cs.LG`  
  _Thomas Bartz-Beielstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior-data fitted networks are typically trained using synthetic data generated by a single, manually constructed generator. In cases where such a generator combines multiple components, e.g., in TabPFN, Mitra, or TabICLv2, this is achieved through a mixture using manually specified weights, the impact of which on the network only becomes apparent after pre-training. Instead of such a hand-built generator, this work proposes a structured prior: a small set of named component priors, linked by operators whose algebraic properties are formally proven and whose influence on the trained network has been validated. Within a measure-theoretic framework, the operators of mixture, convolution, amplitude scaling, local transform, and projection are defined. Their governing laws are proven. These operators are implemented in the macl2l software package. Experiments demonstrate that the implemented generators reproduce the identities within the resolution of a permutation test. Furthermore, it is shown that trained networks obey the distributive law; that a mixed prior composed of small components performs as well as or better than any individual component in seven out of eight open regression benchmarks; and that on two of these tables a structured prior reaches, with a ninth of the data budget, an error that a generic random-function prior does not reach even with the full budget. A theorem on model averaging demonstrates that networks trained on individual components constitute the Bayes predictor for the mixture, provided that the component networks are themselves the Bayes predictors for their respective priors. However, the pre-registered test of this averaged predictor with trained component networks failed, because the networks identify the generating component too rarely. The paper proposes a research program focused on the algebra of structured priors for prior-data fitted networks.
  </details>

- **[Decision Transformer with Task-Aware Representations for Offline Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04010)**  `arXiv:2611.04010`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xinbo Zhao, Yuhang Liu, Xin Zhang, Lei Le, Zhaohan Xi, Yingxue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To enable effective generalization in offline meta-RL, it is essential to accurately infer the underlying task through latent task representations, which provide a compact and transferable summary of task-specific characteristics for guiding policy generation. In this paper, we propose Contrastive Task-Aware Decision Transformer (CoTA-DT), a DT-based offline meta-RL framework that learns task representations by jointly integrating four complementary components: (i) contrastive learning from offline trajectories, (ii) alignment with natural-language task descriptions, (iii) one-step supervision via a task-conditioned world model, and (iv) long-term supervision via a task-conditioned Q function. The learned task representations are used as conditioning signals for a DT-based policy, enabling task-aware meta-policy learning. To further support generalization in both zero-shot and few-shot settings, we design a language-grounded, self-adaptive strategy that enables effective and reliable adaptation to unseen tasks. Notably, in CoTA-DT, the pretrained LLM serves as a shared backbone for language encoding, world modeling, Q-function estimation, and policy learning. This unified design facilitates parameter sharing, improving both efficiency and generalization. Extensive experiments across diverse offline meta-RL benchmarks demonstrate that CoTA-DT consistently outperforms prior methods, particularly in challenging zero-shot generalization scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Sampling Interventional Distributions with Orthogonal MMD Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03962)**  `arXiv:2611.03962`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bariscan Bozkurt, Hugh Dance, Zonghao Chen, Alexandre Galashov, Arthur Gretton, Houssam Zenati_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many causal inference problems require sampling from interventional outcome distributions rather than estimating low-dimensional summaries. Existing approaches often rely on fitting generative models of the target distribution together with additional nuisance components, which can be computationally demanding for high-dimensional outcomes. We propose Interventional Maximum Mean Discrepancy Flow (IMMD-Flow), a particle-based method that instead represents the target interventional distribution through its kernel mean embedding. This kernel embedding admits a doubly robust, Neyman-orthogonal estimator with finite-sample guarantees and can be efficiently reused to sample from the interventional distribution via MMD gradient flow. IMMD-Flow accommodates both hard and soft interventions, admits theoretical guarantees for estimation and particle approximation, and extends to high-dimensional image outcomes through frozen encoders. Across image-based benchmarks, IMMD-Flow matches or improves upon state-of-the-art generative approaches while showing significant speed advantages.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating FastDEC Clustering Algorithm with GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03934)**  `arXiv:2611.03934`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mi Li, Eibe Frank, Bernhard Pfahringer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast Dominance Estimation Clustering (FastDEC) is an efficient variant of Density Peak Clustering (DPC) that achieves high clustering quality through dominance-based density estimation, but remains constrained by the computational cost of exact k-nearest-neighbor (k-NN) construction and dominance component (DC) processing. We present SymWarp-FastDEC, a GPU-accelerated implementation of FastDEC. At its core is SymTrikNN, an exact self-k-NN kernel that exploits search symmetry using upper-triangular traversal and warp-cooperative top-k selection. We also introduce WP-DC, a warp-parallel DC reduction algorithm designed for modern GPUs. Combined with memory-adaptive execution planning and stream scheduling, WP-DC eliminates DC reduction as a runtime bottleneck. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that SymWarp-FastDEC produces clustering outputs identical to the original FastDEC on all evaluated equivalence benchmarks. Compared with a FastDEC variant using FAISS GpuIndexFlatL2 for exact k-NN search, our approach achieves approximately 1.7x to 4.9x higher end-to-end performance, with the advantage generally increasing with dimensionality. Compared with the original single-threaded Python FastDEC implementation, speedups reach approximately 2.5 x 10^4 in a very high-dimensional case where its k-d-tree-based search degrades; this extreme result is not representative of typical acceleration.
  </details>

- **[When Latent Dynamics Meet Data Assimilation: A Benchmark of Koopman-Type Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03916)**  `arXiv:2611.03916`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiwei Zhang, Wenxuan Yuan, Xiaoyuan Cheng, Pouria Behnoudfar, Chuanqi Chen, Charlotte Moser, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Koopman representations describe nonlinear dynamics through linear evolution in a transformed space, offering an attractive framework for their linear simplicity and generality. Yet these advantages do not necessarily translate into improved performance in deep latent data assimilation (LDA). We systematically compare seven representative methods and structural baselines across five systems spanning approximately periodic to strongly chaotic dynamics and a broad range of state dimensions. We evaluate forecast and assimilation accuracy, physical state recovery, latent-trajectory behavior, and dynamical consistency, and assess Gaussian error assumptions, objective convexity, and uniqueness of analysis solutions. Our results reveal a trade-off between representational flexibility and optimization tractability. Linear evolution of the latent state can stabilize short-term forecasts but restrict expressiveness in strongly chaotic and high-dimensional systems, without consistently improving assimilation accuracy. Parameterized linear operators offer greater flexibility, yet their joint optimization with the encoder--decoder remains nonconvex. Methods with closed-form analysis updates generally perform better and exhibit greater robustness in our experiments, although matrix inversion remains a computational bottleneck. These findings show that the practical benefits of Koopman structure depend on both sufficient observational information and the choice of assimilation procedure.
  </details>

- **[ExaTune: Exhaustive Hyperparameter Landscape Exploration at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03867)**  `arXiv:2611.03867`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kevin Dick, Katie Lonsway_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) returns a single optimal configuration, systematically discarding the explored performance landscape that encodes how results depend on configuration choices. Fitness landscape analysis comprises a robust collection of methods from evolutionary computation offering ruggedness indices, autocorrelation functions, and fitness-distance correlation that have never been applied to machine learning hyperparameter spaces at scale, given that fine-grained ground-truth enumeration data has not existed. We present ExaTune, an open-source HPC system to produce a new artifact class: the hyperparameter landscape map. ExaTune exhaustively evaluates every point on a discretized hyperparameter grid via $K$-fold cross-validation, aggregates results into reproducible parquet benchmarks, and applies a suite of five formalized landscape metrics: spatial autocorrelation (Moran's $I$), ruggedness, local optima density, fitness-distance correlation (FDC), and variance-based parameter importance. We release a benchmark collection of 8 complete landscape maps (totalling 285,080 exhaustively evaluated configurations) spanning five model families (SVMs, random forests, XGBoost, ridge regression, and $k$-nearest neighbors) across six classification and two regression benchmarks, hosted on HuggingFace. Empirical analysis demonstrates that Bayesian optimization, Optuna, Hyperband, and Random Search at standard trial budgets systematically miss instability regions, secondary optima, and parameter interaction effects that are visible in complete landscapes; information that is structurally unavailable to any optimizer by design.
  </details>

- **[Dr. No: Feature Steering for Abstention in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03862)**  `arXiv:2611.03862`  `cs.LG`  
  _Farid Hasanov, Riccardo Renzulli, Bahram Zonooz, Mykola Pechenizkiy, Decebal Constantin Mocanu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large foundation models often answer even when the available visual or textual evidence is insufficient to support a reliable conclusion. This failure to abstain makes them hard to trust in high-stakes or information-seeking settings, where a confident but unsupported answer can be worse than no answer at all. It also raises a natural question: can the internal mechanisms behind this behaviour be identified and controlled at inference time? We present Dr. No, a feature-steering approach that mines sparse autoencoder (SAE) features associated with abstention and commitment, then modulates a small set of them during the prefill pass, without retraining the base model.Dr. No is evaluated on LLaVA-1.6 Mistral 7B across multimodal abstention benchmarks, including UNK-VQA and MM-UPD, and additionally benchmarked on language reasoning tasks using Qwen3.5-2B on AbstentionBench subsets. Against multiple uncertainty-based abstention baselines, feature steering achieves a more favourable balance between correct answering and appropriate abstention than post-hoc rejection methods. The gains are strongest on UNK-VQA and on MM-UPD cases involving image--question or question--answer incompatibility, and transfer most clearly to mathematical reasoning subsets of AbstentionBench. However, performance is more limited on absent-answer detection and retrieval-heavy multi-hop QA, such as MuSiQue. These results suggest that sparse internal features provide lightweight control handles for shaping uncertainty-aware behaviour in foundation models, while also showing that abstention control is source-dependent rather than governed by a single universal signal.
  </details>

- **[CaRe-KT: Continuity-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning for Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03744)**  `arXiv:2611.03744`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mehrnoush Mohammadi, Kamal Berahmand, Mohammad Morsali, Shazia Sadiq, Wojtek Tomaszewski, Hassan Khosravi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge tracing (KT) estimates learners' evolving knowledge states from interaction histories to support adaptive learning. Retrieval-augmented KT improves these estimates using cross-learner behavioral evidence, but existing approaches typically separate evidence retrieval from downstream prediction. This creates a retrieval-inference alignment gap because evidence relevance varies with the learner's evolving state and prediction context. We propose CaRe-KT, a query-adaptive framework that maintains relevance continuity between evidence acquisition and inference without equating retrieval relevance with predictive utility. CaRe-KT introduces an Evidence-Graph Cache (EGC) that combines persistent question-level relational compatibility with interaction-level temporal validity to construct query-specific evidence subgraphs. Historical learning trajectories retrieved through these subgraphs are encoded alongside the target learner's history in a shared representation space using outcome-supervised contrastive learning. An attention mechanism combines acquisition relevance with learned query-evidence compatibility, allowing relevance to inform, rather than determine, each trajectory's predictive influence. Experiments across five benchmarks show that CaRe-KT outperforms strong KT baselines and remains robust under sparse-history and cold-start conditions. These findings support relevance continuity as a design principle for retrieval-augmented knowledge tracing.
  </details>

- **[Auditing a Model-Hub Benchmark: Data Quality, Training Budgets, and Side Information in HuggingBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03727)**  `arXiv:2611.03727`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michal Podstawski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks built from model hubs combine interaction data, knowledge graphs (KGs) and resource documentation, but valid files do not ensure that these artifacts describe the same objects. Treating the release as a data product, we audit HuggingBench (SIGIR 2025), a benchmark for recommending Hugging Face resources, through data validation, a replication of its eleven recommendation methods and controlled experiments with three training seeds. In three of its four external KGs, the files pass key and range checks but violate the item-entity identity constraint on which KG recommenders rely. Correcting this join improves every KG-based method in every seed, by up to 73% in mean Recall@10, and consistently reverses ten of 27 comparisons between KG configurations. The protocol matters as well: the 100-epoch budget ends before early stopping in seven to eight of 29 validation-selected settings, and 21 of 23 such setting-seed pairs improve with a longer budget. The released item text, which no reported method uses, is the strongest signal: closed-form linear models with text and the item graph exceed the best reported Recall@10 by 18% while fitting in minutes on a CPU. Checks of other recommendation releases show that individually valid releases of one dataset can use incompatible identifiers. We release corrected files, mappings, validators and logs, and derive practices for preserving identity and provenance across the artifacts of benchmarks and model lakes.
  </details>

- **[The Cost of Mismatch: Noise Amplification in Zeroth-Order Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03260)**  `arXiv:2611.03260`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lianmin Chen, Junbin Qiu, Chenxing Wei, Yao Shu, Kun He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization has emerged as a promising approach for memory-efficient large language model (LLM) post-training, yet its reliability in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) remains largely unexplored. Motivated by the distinct data regimes of on-policy RL and fixed-data optimization, we develop a generic variance-theoretic framework to investigate what limits the reliability of ZO gradient estimation. Our analysis reveals that estimation reliability is structurally determined by whether the two finite-difference evaluations are performed on matched data. On-policy reinforcement learning (RL) under the mismatched-data regime produces a $1/\mu^2$ oracle-noise amplification that sets an irreducible noise floor as the perturbation scale $\mu$ decreases, whereas Boltzmann Targeted SFT (BOLT) under the matched-data regime eliminates this amplification via noise cancellation. We further derive convergence guarantees with explicit constants that cleanly separate the two regimes. These results motivate the ZO-BOLT algorithm, which builds on BOLT's matched-data advantage for reliable ZO optimization of an RLVR-aligned objective. Controlled perturbation-scale sweeps and matched-versus-mismatched end-to-end comparisons validate the predicted effect of data matching. Experiments across three models and two mathematical reasoning benchmarks further show that ZO-BOLT consistently improves over the pretrained baseline while retaining a near-inference memory footprint. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Unit-Circle Eigenvalues: Invariant Bases for Stable State Space Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02174)**  `arXiv:2611.02174`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiaqian Zhu, Yang Zhang, Junhua Ding, Xiaowei Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diagonal state space models process long sequences at linear cost, yet the same transition is applied at every step of a rollout, so an unconstrained recurrence holds no quantity fixed and the state decays or grows without bound. Current designs constrain one quantity, and preserving one does not guarantee preservation of the others, so a model can hold its norm to machine precision while momentum drifts by hundreds of percent. We claim that the conserved quantities of a diagonal recurrence are layered, that each layer requires a strictly stronger condition on the operator, and that a unit-modulus spectrum reaches only the outermost. Acting on the claim, we enforce the conditions required for every layer by construction. The generator of the Kronecker-Cayley transition (KC) is a graph Laplacian over object subspaces, $\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{L}\otimes I_m$, advanced by a Cayley transform, and the step stays diagonal at $O(N^3+N^2m)$ cost. The conserved set is exactly a $2m^2$-dimensional family of quadratic forms, held at every parameter value, so the quantities and the dynamics the construction reaches are fixed before training. On four conservation benchmarks, KC holds total mass at or near the reference floor while every diagonal baseline loses it by orders of magnitude. We further compare against mass correction and find that two correctors fail once the underlying prediction diverges, which shows that assembly rather than correction is necessary for a guarantee that holds across the rollout. A free modal basis trained under a conservation penalty conserves only by freezing its spectrum, whereas the assembled operator conserves with every mode but one free to rotate. Beyond these benchmarks, KC holds the linear momentum of three $N$-body systems and, at the operator level, the conserved quantity in molecular dynamics, chemical kinetics, pharmacokinetics and power-grid swing dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Budgeted Task-Aware Acquisition of Dynamic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05862)**  `arXiv:2609.05862`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zihe Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning on dynamic graphs is difficult when changes in the underlying network are only partially observed. Acquiring current graph information incurs observation and computational costs, making complete updates impractical under limited resources. This paper focuses on budgeted task-aware acquisition on dynamic networks, where a model needs to decide which stale graph information to refresh for a downstream task. We propose Scout, a lightweight framework that learns the task value of querying each node from the maintained graph and observation history. Our evaluation covers one synthetic and 4 real-world dynamic networks, 2 downstream tasks, 9 acquisition baselines, and several query budgets. Scout achieves the highest mean downstream performance in 15 of the 21 benchmark-budget settings and ranks within the top two in 19 of them. Its average rank is 1.4, compared with 3.2 for the strongest baseline. On the same dynamic network, task-matched acquisition improves link-prediction AUC by 0.005-0.007 and node-classification accuracy by 0.007-0.017 over task-mismatched acquisition. These results show that useful graph observations depend on the downstream task and that limited observation budgets can be allocated effectively by learning directly from downstream utility.
  </details>

- **[DeSyR: A decoupled symbolic recovery framework for PDEs with PINN-guided structure discovery and physics-based coefficient refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00530)**  `arXiv:2609.00530`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pancheng Niu, Jun Guo, Qiaolin He, Jingcai Guo, Yanchao Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering explicit symbolic solutions of partial differential equations (PDEs) from physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) is attractive, yet selecting and fitting expressions against neural predictions can lock the recovered solution into the teacher's approximation error. We propose DeSyR (Decoupled Symbolic Recovery) to break this dependence. DeSyR uses PINNs only to discover candidate structures and initial coefficient estimates; once a structure is fixed, the governing equations and constraints alone determine the refined coefficients. Multiple candidates with different fitting-error--complexity trade-offs are retained, evaluated after physics-only refinement, and reduced to the simplest expression within a physically near-optimal set. Joint refinement extends the same principle to coupled fields. For fixed candidate parameterizations, affine error identities and local nonlinear analysis show how teacher errors enter fitted coefficients and establish conditions under which physical residuals control coefficient and solution errors. Across seven benchmarks covering scalar evolution equations, multidimensional elliptic problems and coupled incompressible flows, DeSyR attains mean relative solution errors on the order of $10^{-14}$ or smaller and lower reported mean physical residuals than PINNs, physics-informed symbolic networks and teacher-fitted symbolic distillation. With the expression structure held fixed, estimating coefficients from physical constraints further yields more accurate frequency estimates and solutions than fitting teacher predictions. DeSyR therefore turns neural approximations into guides for structure discovery, while physical constraints determine the final explicit solution.
  </details>

- **[Does Source-Free Time-Series Adaptation Really Require Updating the Encoder?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23120)**  `arXiv:2606.23120`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shichang Meng, Linquan Wu, Xuan Ai, Yi Wu, Mingyang Liu, Linqi Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a pretrained model to an unlabeled target domain without accessing source data, enabling the reuse of time-series predictors across sensing systems. Existing methods commonly update the encoder using unlabeled target objectives. Yet changes in users, equipment, and operating conditions also alter the spectral characteristics of input signals, raising a fundamental question: does adaptation require changing the encoder, or can we instead adapt the signal it receives? We propose Source Free Frequency-Prototype Alignment (SF-FPA), which learns a spectral input transformation while freezing the source encoder and classifier parameters. First, a frequency adaptation layer applies amplitude and phase corrections to target signals. Separate branches adjust spectral magnitudes and phase relationships, with circular phase encoding avoiding discontinuities at the angular boundary. Second, confidence-filtered prototype alignment guides target representations toward source class prototypes. Combined with information maximization, this objective propagates gradients through the source predictor to learn spectral corrections that support class discrimination. Extensive experiments on three real-world time series benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art average Macro-F1 performance. Component ablations and controlled comparisons with encoder adaptation and a parameter-matched time-domain adapter further support spectral input adaptation as an effective approach to reusing source-trained predictors. The implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Dataset Distillation for Classification: Do Distilled Sets Outperform Coresets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18209)**  `arXiv:2606.18209`  `cs.LG`  
  _Trisha Mittal, Akshay Mehra, Joshua Kimball_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dataset distillation (DD) has emerged as a prominent approach in data centric machine learning, aiming to synthesize compact training sets for efficient training by compressing the information in large datasets into a small number of synthetic samples. However, DD methods are often evaluated under inconsistent evaluation protocols, ranging from standard ERM to single/multi-teacher supervision, making it difficult to isolate the effectiveness of distilled data from evaluation. Moreover, many prior methods claim that DD outperforms data pruning approaches such as coreset selection (CS), based on the assumption that restricting condensed datasets to subsets of real samples fundamentally limits their expressiveness. In this work, we critically evaluate DD methods through large-scale experiments using standardized datasets and evaluation protocols to assess their intrinsic effectiveness. We benchmark seven state-of-the-art (SOTA) DD methods on ImageNet-1K, ImageNet100, and ImageNette, using three widely adopted training protocols against four CS strategies. Our results show that while some DD methods fail to outperform even simple random subsets, the SOTA DD approaches are comparable to or worse than coresets on large-scale datasets and incur a substantially higher cost for construction. Beyond accuracy, we also evaluate the representativeness, diversity, and quality of condensed sets, and find that coresets consistently achieve better coverage of the original data distribution. These findings highlight the limited practical advantages of current DD methods and show that coresets remain competitive and are often a more computationally efficient alternative for data-centric learning.
  </details>

- **[Re-examining low-rank adaptation for private LLM fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01137)**  `arXiv:2510.01137`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Dadsetan, Frank Rudzicz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Privacy is a central concern when fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on sensitive data, and differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DP-SGD) provides formal privacy guarantees. When performance at a fixed privacy level largely saturates on established benchmarks such as GLUE, we ask whether the same performance can be reached with less computation. Both theory and practice show that lower-rank models are better suited to DP training, a property especially relevant for LLMs, whose fine-tuning gradients exhibit a strong low-rank structure. Methods such as DP-LoRA exploit this by expressing each layer's weight update as a sum of only a few non-zero rank-one components. However, we argue that while having few non-zero components is important, the isotropic noise injected by DP-SGD inflates the singular values of the gradient matrix, disrupting their naturally fast decay. We investigate whether this noise-induced spectral distortion slows optimization. Experiments on language classification (GLUE with RoBERTa) and table-to-text generation (E2E and DART with Qwen and Llama models up to 4B parameters) show that partially restoring the original singular-value profile significantly accelerates DP-SGD convergence without compromising privacy guarantees. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Visibility-Aware Reference Generation for Benchmarking Visual 3D Reconstruction in Vegetation-Rich Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05361)**  `arXiv:2611.05361`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhipeng Zhong, Matthew Coombes, Cunjia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable evaluation of visual 3D reconstruction and depth estimation in vegetation-rich outdoor environments is limited by the scarcity of geometric ground-truth data and the cost of complete scanner surveys. We present a LiDARbased reference-generation pipeline for evaluating the zero-shot performance of feed-forward visual-geometry models alongside conventional structure-from-motion (SfM) methods. The pipeline reuses camera images, LiDAR scans, and reference poses from existing multi-sensor localization datasets to generate per-view depth maps and visible reference point clouds. To address visibility errors in accumulated LiDAR scans, we introduce the LargeDepth-Gap Visibility Score (LDGVS) for point-wise visibility filtering and automatic scale selection in Hidden Point Removal(HPR). Experiments on TartanAirV2 and TartanGround show that, compared with Poisson surface reconstruction, which is an approach suited to completed scan surveys, the combined HPR and LDGVS filtering retains approximately three times the point density at comparable absolute relative error. We also apply the pipeline to the BotanicGarden dataset to systematically compare depth estimation and 3D reconstruction methods. Feedforward methods produce dense depth predictions efficiently, but their 3D reconstructions can contain duplicated structures. Conventional SfM generally produces more coherent geometry, but its reconstructions remain sparse and more prone to failure in challenging vegetated scenes. The benchmark toolkit and datasets will be released.
  </details>

- **[PadLoc: Training-Free Floor-Plan Localization by Sparse Geometric Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05172)**  `arXiv:2611.05172`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Marian Bookhahn, Frank Neumann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose PadLoc, a training-free localization back-end that places a moving sensor within a known floor plan. Floor plans are cheap, persistent, and robust to appearance change, but recent learned-depth methods localize against them by filtering per-frame depth through dense $(x,y,\theta)$ pose grids. PadLoc keeps the swappable depth front-end and replaces the dense grid back-end with sparse geometric matching: dominant wall orientations define a small pose-candidate set, masked normalized cross-correlation scores observed free space against the plan, and a moving-pose Gaussian-mixture Bayes filter plus consensus readout decodes the trajectory. Holding each competitor's depth fixed, PadLoc matches or beats UnLoc's forward filter and the F3Loc filter at every Gibson trajectory length, and UnLoc's offline refinement from $T=35$ on; it exceeds Z-FLoc's published results with a shared MapAnything front-end. On Bonn LiLocBench, PadLoc improves pooled SR@1m over UnLoc by 23-32 pp under wheel odometry and 35-43 pp with ground-truth motion; with full-FOV LiDAR plus wheel odometry the same readout reaches 100% SR@1m at 9-10 cm median error on the static sequence. On the Hilti-Trimble Localization Challenge PadLoc localizes a camera-only fisheye rig on construction floors hundreds of metres across at 0.90-0.98 online SR@2m on three of five sessions; adding learned wall isolation, applied to every session, covers all five at 0.67-0.92 with the localizer unchanged throughout. PadLoc thus matches or outperforms dense grid back-ends while preserving sensor modularity and a compact state that does not grow with trajectory length.
  </details>

- **[iMED: A Multi-Endoscope Dataset for Surgical 3D Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04755)**  `arXiv:2611.04755`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Sierra Bonilla, Fengyi Jiang, Chinedu Nwoye, Jingpei Lu, Kailey Reardon, Humphrey W. Chow, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce iMED, the first synchronized multi-endoscope dataset for robot-assisted minimally invasive surgery (RAMIS), designed to address a fundamental limitation in surgical vision benchmarking: the absence of independent held-out viewpoints. Existing surgical datasets capture single-trajectory sequences from a single endoscope, making it impossible to distinguish geometric generalization from photometric interpolation along a narrow forward-facing path. iMED provides 340 sequences ~170K synchronized timepoints with 4 views per timepoint) recorded simultaneously from two independent stereo endoscopes across ex vivo, postmortem, and live surgical settings, spanning 14 specimens with diverse anatomical regions and motion regimes. The dataset includes calibrated camera intrinsics, ArUco-based frame-wise pose estimates with uncertainty quantification, instrument segmentation masks, and rich clinical metadata. Using a train-on-one-endoscope, test-on-another evaluation protocol, we benchmark 23 state-of-the-art methods across rigid and deformable novel view synthesis, pose estimation, feature matching, and monocular depth estimation. Our experiments show that methods relying primarily on photometric supervision degrade substantially under this held-out-endoscope setting, while methods with explicit geometric regularization are more robust across views in our benchmark. Notably, large-scale foundation models transfer surprisingly well to surgical imagery, often outperforming domain-specific models, suggesting pitfalls in current fine-tuning protocols. iMED establishes a new evaluation protocol for geometric generalization in surgical vision.
  </details>

- **[SimuScene: Simulation-Ready Compositional 3D Scene Reconstruction from a Single Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03994)**  `arXiv:2606.03994`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Inhee Lee, Sangwon Baik, Sungjoo Kim, Hyeonwoo Kim, Hyunsoo Cha, Hanbyul Joo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing interactive, simulation-ready 3D scenes from a single image is a critical bottleneck for robotic manipulation. While recent single-image lifters recover plausible per-object shapes, composing them yields scenes that collapse under physical simulation due to interpenetrating, hovering, or sinking objects. Existing physics-aware compositional reconstruction methods mainly address this as a post-hoc layout correction, leaving the underlying geometric errors unresolved. To address this, we introduce SimuScene, a compositional 3D reconstruction pipeline that puts physics in the loop of shape and layout estimation. Rather than using physics merely for layout cleanup, we utilize the physics engine as a diagnostic measurement tool during the generative process itself. By diagnostically simulating reconstructed objects under gravity, we convert penetration and support failures into quantitative correction signals that drive amodal shape resampling, with gravity-axis stretching as a lightweight correction for minor errors. This physics-informed feedback loop mitigates accumulated reconstruction errors and produces a stable, simulation-ready compositional 3D scene. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on physical stability and geometric alignment benchmarks. We further highlight SimuScene's utility by deploying reconstructed environments in humanoid control and robot-arm manipulation tasks.
  </details>

- **[PLANTEUR: A Crop-Type Segmentation Benchmark for Tropical Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05223)**  `arXiv:2611.05223`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Piper Wolters, Gabriel Tseng, Favyen Bastani, Joseph Redmon, Yawen Zhang, Hadrien Sablon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce PLANTEUR, a benchmark for crop-type mapping from satellite images in the French overseas territories. Building on the PASTIS design, PLANTEUR provides georeferenced and aligned Sentinel-2, Sentinel-1, and Landsat-8 sequences with parcel-derived labels spanning six tropical regions and an 8-class label space. We define both a standard test split and a leave-one-island-out protocol to measure cross-territory generalization, and evaluate fully supervised models, linear probes, and fine-tuned foundation models. The benchmark code and data are open source and available on GitHub and Hugging Face.
  </details>

- **[Perturb, Repair, Verify: Self-Play Vision-Language Verifiers for Compositional Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05158)**  `arXiv:2611.05158`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zijun Huang, Yingjun Du, Cees G. M. Snoek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language verifiers judge whether an image faithfully matches a textual description, yet existing training pipelines typically rely on static supervision from stronger external judges or curated hard-negative data. Such supervision requires external generation or annotation, is teacher-bounded, and is difficult to adapt as model failures evolve. We introduce PROVE (Perturb-and-Repair Optimization for Vision-Language Evaluation), a self-play framework that trains verifiers through a closed perturb-verify-repair-verify loop. Starting from a known-correct image-caption pair, a Mutator introduces a typed perturbation over objects, attributes, counts, or relations. The verifier then judges the perturbed caption and provides textual feedback, which a Repairer uses to recover a faithful caption for re-verification. These two verification rounds produce structured rewards for informative mismatches and visually grounded repairs. Since perturbation, verification, and repair are role-conditioned behaviors of the same evolving multimodal backbone, updating the shared parameters also improves the verifier itself. Experiments show that PROVE improves fine-grained compositional verification across model scales and families and strengthens visual-detail robustness on the reference backbone, with limited, benchmark-dependent changes on broader multimodal tasks.
  </details>

- **[A Universal Raw-IQ Radar Encoder for Cross-Sensor Human Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04534)**  `arXiv:2611.04534`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jonas Leo Mueller, Dario Zanca, Sebastian Hoefler, Jan Petermann, Thomas Altstidl, Naga Venkata Sai Jitin Jami, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radar-based human sensing typically relies on end-to-end task-specific models trained on hand-chosen signal projections, which generalize poorly across unseen users, tasks, and hardware. We argue that the dominant signal processing choice, the micro-Doppler spectrogram, is a poor starting point for transfer because it keeps the Doppler axis, which carrier frequency rescales, and discards the angular axis, which carrier frequency leaves unchanged. We instead pretrain a convolutional beta-VAE without labels on clutter-removed raw radar measurements from four platforms spanning 60 and 77 GHz and 4 to 12 virtual antennas, aligned by a unified spectral interpolation grid. For every downstream benchmark the encoder stays frozen, and only a small supervised temporal classifier is trained on its embeddings. We compare against supervised models trained end to end on each benchmark and a pretrained radar foundation model across ten settings covering activity, gesture, sign-language, and gait recognition. When the test users and the radar are seen during training, our encoder is best on all five benchmarks. For users never seen in training, it leads on three of four benchmarks, by 4.8 to 23.2 points. On subject-disjoint gait recognition, it is best for each of the four held-out subjects and 8.3 points above the strongest baseline on average, even though its pretraining excluded every recording of this radar. Excluding the test radar from pretraining costs at most 5.3 points on any benchmark. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do We Still Need Rotation-Invariant Architectures? A Statistical Robustness Benchmark for Point Cloud Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03952)**  `arXiv:2611.03952`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Abdulla Nasir Chowdhury, Gulpi Qorik Oktagalu Pratamasunu, Kazuhiro Fukui_
