  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A substantial line of work builds rotation invariance directly into point cloud architectures. We benchmark 10 architectures (PointNet, PointNet++, DGCNN, RIConv, RIConv++, RISurConv, PointMLP, PointTransformer, and the equivariant VN-PointNet and VN-DGCNN) across 5 domains under pose, noise, and occlusion, and analyze the results with confirmatory hypothesis tests rather than point estimates. The three rotation-invariant (RI) architectures achieve the best pose robustness but rank among the lowest on noise robustness in 4 of 5 domains. We ask whether this cost is intrinsic to invariance or specific to how invariance is obtained. We retrain all 10 architectures unchanged, with a simple invariance loss on the embedding and three training seeds each, and evaluate all models on the independently designed ModelNet40-C. Invariance training transfers narrowly but reliably. It improves exactly the corruption families its perturbations target wherever the baseline has headroom (mean occlusion +10.5, lidar +13.4 percentage points of accuracy). At matched training budget the best standard backbone and the best RI architecture end on par on unseen corruptions (within 2.5 percentage points in every seed), and invariance-trained RIConv++ is the most seed-stable top model. Once rotation augmentation is widened, standard backbones concede only a flat, angle-independent accuracy offset under full-sphere rotation. We release the benchmark along with a package for analyzing point cloud robustness and reproducing our results.
  </details>

- **[More Accurate, Less Human: Gestalt Grouping in Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10195)**  `arXiv:2608.10195`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Sudhanva Manjunath Athreya, Sai Phani Kumar Malladi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human vision organizes what it sees into wholes: same-colored points group into series, similar marks cohere into categories, and shapes complete into recognizable objects. These are the Gestalt operations that visualization design builds on. Whether vision models organize visual content this way has not been systematically tested. We introduce a behavioral battery that scores models against human data from prior perception studies on four grouping tasks: mark-color odd-one-out, color-series counting, silhouette recognition, and object odd-one-out. We apply it to 45 models across five training families: supervised, self-supervised, and contrastive vision-language encoders, open-weight VLMs, and closed foundation models. The battery reveals that agreement with human responses captures aspects of perceptual organization that conventional performance metrics fail to distinguish, with several closed models exhibiting substantially lower alignment than their benchmark accuracy would suggest. Scoring against published perception data therefore gives visualization research a reusable yardstick, requiring no new user study, for auditing whether the models now entering visualization pipelines organize what they see the way their human audience does.
  </details>

- **[Distill to Think, Foresee to Act: Cognitive-Physical Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21139)**  `arXiv:2605.21139`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yang Wu, Qiang Meng, Zhaojiang Liu, Youquan Liu, Jian Yang, Jin Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current end-to-end autonomous driving models are fundamentally constrained by the behavioral cloning ceiling of imitation learning. While reinforcement learning offers a path to smarter autonomy, it demands two missing pieces of infrastructure: (1) a cognitive foundation that understands traffic semantics and driving intent, and (2) a foresighted physical environment that can anticipate the consequences of candidate actions. To this end, we propose CoPhy, a CognitivePhysical reinforcement learning framework for autonomous driving. To distill to think, we distill VLM knowledge into the BEV encoder and then discard the VLM entirely, retaining cognitive ability at zero inference cost while releasing the cognitive channel as a pluggable interface for optional human language commands. To foresee to act, we build an auto-regressive BEV world model that explicitly predicts future semantic maps conditioned on candidate actions, serving as an interpretable physical sandbox from which safety metrics are directly derived. Built upon this dual infrastructure, we optimize the driving policy via GRPO with a novel dual-reward mechanism: a physical reward derived from BEV rollouts enforces hard safety constraints, while a cognitive reward from a language-aligned scorer ensures intent compliance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPhy not only achieves state-of-the-art results on NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, but also enables safer driving via cognitively informed scene compliance and flexible intent control through user-defined language instructions. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CurEvo: Curriculum-Guided Self-Evolution for Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26707)**  `arXiv:2604.26707`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Guiyi Zeng, Junqing Yu, Yi-Ping Phoebe Chen, Chen Xu, Wei Yang, Zikai Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent self-evolution frameworks for video understanding have demonstrated the potential for autonomous improvement from unlabeled videos. However, existing approaches often suffer from weakly controlled optimization and capability progression because they lack structured guidance for generating, evaluating, and reusing supervision throughout the iterative learning process. We present CurEvo, a curriculum-guided self-evolution framework that organizes this loop as a competence-aware progression. Curriculum-Guided Self-Evolution maintains a competence-aware curriculum state and cumulative training pool across iterations. Within this loop, Multi-Dimensional Question Generation supplies complementary supervision, from directly verifiable structural questions for perception to type-conditioned questions for recognition and understanding. Type-Adaptive Evaluation separately assesses question quality and answer reliability with criteria matched to each type's evidence; its scores determine which pairs enter the pool and how the next curriculum state is updated. Across seven backbones and four VideoQA benchmarks, CurEvo consistently improves benchmark accuracy and benchmark semantic score. For Qwen2.5-VL(7B), accuracy rises from 67.84% to 69.91% on ActivityNet-QA and from 76.94% to 79.61% on NExT-QA. CurEvo thus establishes self-generated supervision as a stable route to autonomous video understanding without task-specific human QA annotations during self-evolution.
  </details>

- **[What Does the Model See? Representation Validity in Multimodal LLM-Based Video Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05181)**  `arXiv:2611.05181`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Christian Pipal, Eva-Maria Vogel, Morgan Wack_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) allow social scientists to analyze video at scale, but researchers must decide which parts of a video to provide as input. We use the term representation validity to examine how text, audio, video, and combined inputs affect measurement and subsequent analysis. We compare input representations across six open-weight MLLMs and three human-annotated video datasets. No representation consistently performs best. On seven news-related constructs, native-video performance varies little across models (macro-F1 = .70-.77), while extracted-text performance ranges from .52 to .80. Three models perform better with text and three with video. Combining inputs rarely improves performance. Higher classification accuracy also does not reliably produce estimates closer to those based on human codes. The highest-F1 representation best reproduces human-coded regression coefficients in 17 of 42 comparisons. Even when each model uses its highest-F1 deployable representation, 45% of sample-prevalence estimates differ from the human-coded benchmark by more than ten percentage points. Input representation therefore requires validation for both the construct being measured and the quantity estimated from the resulting labels.
  </details>

- **[Heads That Write, Not Just Point: Image Retrieval Heads in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04903)**  `arXiv:2611.04903`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Junsung Park, Sangwon Yu, Donghun Kang, Yeongtak Oh, Jaewon Jeong, Gyeongtae Yoo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) can process interleaved text and multiple images in a single context, but how they internally identify the image relevant to a question remains poorly understood. We study this grounding step as in-context image retrieval and introduce ICIR-MCQ, a controlled probe for head-level analysis of image retrieval. Using each head's attention mass over image-token spans, we first identify sparse pointer heads that reliably point to the target image. These heads transfer beyond the probe: a single pointer head is competitive with external vision-language retrievers on a naturalistic multi-image retrieval benchmark, showing that LVLMs contain a strong internal image retrieval signal. Attention alone, however, shows where a head reads from, not what its output passes on to later layers, so we introduce a complementary output-side analysis. For each head, we train a lightweight image retriever on its value-weighted output and use it to identify writer heads, whose outputs contain enough information to identify the target image. Surprisingly, the pointer and writer criteria select substantially different head sets, with only 35 to 47 heads overlapping among the top-100 heads under each criterion. Targeted set-partition ablations show that the heads most important for standard multi-image inference are concentrated in this pointer-writer intersection, while heads selected by only one criterion show less consistent separation from random-head controls. The most consistent causal effects arise from heads selected by both the attention-based pointer and output-side writer criteria. Code and data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ColorConceptBench: A Benchmark for Probabilistic Color-Concept Understanding in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16836)**  `arXiv:2601.16836`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Chenxi Ruan, Yihan Hou, Yu Xiao, Guosheng Hu, Wei Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) models have advanced considerably in generating high-quality images from textual descriptions. However, their ability to associate colors with concepts remains largely constrained to explicit color names or codes, while their capacity to handle \emph{implicit concepts}, such as emotions and visual states, remains underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce ColorConceptBench, an expert-annotated benchmark that systematically evaluates color-concept associations through probabilistic color distributions. ColorConceptBench moves beyond explicit color specifications by examining how models interpret 1,281 implicit color concepts, grounded in 6,584 human annotations. Our evaluation of nine leading T2I models reveals that performance varies substantially across semantic categories, and models exhibit a significant lack of sensitivity to abstract semantics. These limitations persist even when applying classifier-free guidance scaling at inference time, suggesting that achieving human-like color understanding demands a shift in how models learn and represent implicit semantic meaning.
  </details>

- **[DreamX: Video Diffusion Model is a Universal Latent Action Learner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05377)**  `arXiv:2611.05377`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Jisoo Kim, Chaehyun Kim, Junsu Kim, Jiho Park, Seongchan Kim, Eunbeen Hong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning a universal action representation from video requires modeling dynamics that are consistent across different executions of the same action yet invariant to changes in embodiment and appearance. Existing latent action models infer actions from visual transitions, but their representations often entangle the underlying action with irrelevant visual variations, limiting transfer across embodiments. We introduce DreamX, which repurposes a pretrained video diffusion model as a universal latent action learner. Our approach extracts compact latent actions from the video model's rich spatiotemporal features, compressing each latent video frame by $\boldsymbol{300\times}$ ($\boldsymbol{1{,}200 \rightarrow 4}$ tokens). In addition, by leveraging the video model's generative prior, these latent actions capture action-relevant dynamics disentangled from visual appearance. The resulting latent space serves as a shared action interface across embodiments (human and robot), environments, and action parameterizations. On LARY-Bench, DreamX achieves state-of-the-art performance among latent action models. Moreover, actions encoded in one setting remain effective in another: the latent space supports any-to-any video transfer and zero-shot action transfer across diverse embodiments and environments. Furthermore, this shared interface also enables policy learning in a compact latent action space, reducing the computational cost of video-diffusion-based policies.
  </details>

- **[TFusionOcc: T-Primitive Based Object-Centric Multi-Sensor Fusion Framework for 3D Occupancy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06400)**  `arXiv:2602.06400`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhenxing Ming, Yaoqi Huang, Julie Stephany Berrio, Mao Shan, Stewart Worrall_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The prediction of 3D semantic occupancy enables autonomous vehicles (AVs) to perceive the fine-grained geometric and semantic scene structure for safe navigation and decision-making. Existing methods mainly rely on either voxel-based representations, which incur redundant computation over empty regions, or object-centric Gaussian primitives, whose ellipsoidal per-primitive geometry can limit representation efficiency for complex, non-convex, and asymmetric structures under a fixed primitive and computational budget. In this paper, we present TFusionOcc, a T-primitive-based object-centric multi-sensor fusion framework for 3D semantic occupancy prediction. Specifically, we introduce a family of Student's t-distribution-based T-primitives, including the plain T-primitive, T-Superquadric, and deformable T-Superquadric with inverse warping, where the deformable T-Superquadric serves as the key geometry-enhancing primitive. We further develop a unified probabilistic formulation based on the Student's t-distribution and the T-mixture model (TMM) to jointly model occupancy and semantics, and design a tightly coupled multi-stage fusion architecture to integrate camera and LiDAR cues effectively. Extensive experiments on nuScenes demonstrate competitive performance against recent state-of-the-art methods and strong performance among object-centric multi-sensor approaches, while additional evaluations on nuScenes-C show robust performance across most corruption scenarios. Further evaluation on the separate SemanticKITTI benchmark demonstrates cross-dataset applicability. The code will be available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[DenseTwins: Dense Modality Decoupling for Multimodal Self-Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04710)**  `arXiv:2611.04710`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Carlo Sgaravatti, Daniele Kopyshevskiy, Davide Secco, Giacomo Boracchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal perception is central to many computer vision applications, including robotics and autonomous driving, where different sensing modalities provide complementary cues. Self-supervised learning offers a scalable solution for exploiting large amounts of unlabeled multimodal data. However, existing methods either neglect modality-specific information by enforcing feature-space alignment between modalities or learn only global representations without preserving spatial structure. We propose DenseTwins, a multimodal self-supervised framework that learns dense representations by explicitly decoupling the dense feature space into shared and modality-specific components. The proposed approach combines dense cross-modal correspondences with redundancy-reduction objectives, enabling the model to capture both shared semantics and modality-specific cues within a structured dense embedding space. DenseTwins jointly optimizes an intra-modal objective across augmented views and a cross-modal objective that aligns shared features while decorrelating modality-specific information. We evaluate DenseTwins across multiple multimodal benchmarks and sensing setups, achieving state-of-the-art or competitive performance across multimodal self-supervised baselines. Code available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Remains in Sight? Autoregressive Video Decoding as Representation-Guided Context Rewriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04164)**  `arXiv:2611.04164`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lixuan He, Jihyeon Je, Yang You, Leonidas Guibas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon video generation is increasingly moving toward autoregressive rollout, where each newly generated segment becomes part of the evidence used for future prediction. Recent causal decoders, long-context training, and KV-cache mechanisms have extended feasible duration, but most inference pipelines still decide the visible past implicitly through recent windows, compressed caches, or external memory. This raises a direct question: \textbf{can the choice of which past frames or segments to show to the decoder be treated as an explicit test-time decision?} We answer this question with \textsc{ReCR}, a training-free method that formulates autoregressive video decoding as representation-guided context rewriting. At each step, \textsc{ReCR} builds a selected visible context from the generated history by using internal decoder representations to favor context units that match the global rollout state, avoid redundancy with the recent continuation, preserve temporal boundary coverage, and widen the range of spatial layouts in view. The selected units are then mapped onto a compact causal axis before decoding the next block. Across long-horizon text-to-video benchmarks, \textsc{ReCR} consistently improves multiple autoregressive backbones under matched visible-token budgets. Empirical analyses such as representation-space diagnostics and transition-count prompt switching further show that improving what remains visible is an effective path toward more stable long-video decoding.
  </details>

- **[Query-Conditioned Evidential Keyframe Sampling for MLLM-Based Long-Form Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01002)**  `arXiv:2604.01002`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yiheng Wang, Lichen Zhu, Yueqian Lin, Yudong Liu, Jingyang Zhang, Hai "Helen" Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown strong performance on video question answering, but their application to long-form videos is constrained by limited context length and computational cost, making keyframe sampling essential. Existing approaches typically rely on semantic relevance or reinforcement learning, which either fail to capture evidential clues or suffer from inefficient combinatorial optimization. In this work, we propose an evidence-driven keyframe sampling framework motivated by an information-theoretic perspective. We consider maximizing the conditional mutual information between the selected frames and the answer given the query as an idealized objective, and use a modular singleton-gain surrogate to motivate a tractable frame-wise selection criterion. Guided by this motivation, we train a query-conditioned evidence scoring network using annotated answer-supporting segments. A contrastive objective encourages greater aggregate score mass on frames from evidence regions than on background frames, providing a practical evidence-based criterion beyond semantic similarity. Experiments on long-form video understanding benchmarks show that our method consistently outperforms prior sampling strategies under comparable frame budgets. With Qwen2.5-VL-7B under the same 32-frame budget, our method improves over uniform sampling by 10.1 points on LVBench and 4.4 points on the long-video subset of VideoMME.
  </details>

- **[VIGOR: Visual Gain Ordering for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Discrete Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05066)**  `arXiv:2611.05066`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junzhe Chen, Tian Qin, Eugenie Shi, Tianshu Zhang, Qiang Ju, Lijie Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate responses through iterative mask prediction and offer a promising alternative to autoregressive large vision-language models. However, they still suffer from hallucination, producing outputs that are plausible under language priors but insufficiently grounded in the image. We argue that hallucination in multimodal dLLMs is not only a token prediction problem, but also a premature commitment problem: during iterative denoising, some masked positions are unmasked too early because their confidence is driven more by language priors than by visual evidence, and these early commitments can bias later generation. To address this issue, we propose Visual Gain Ordering (VIGOR), a training-free and plug-and-play inference strategy that reorders masked positions according to counterfactual visual evidence. At each denoising step, VIGOR compares the confidence of each candidate token under the original image-conditioned pass and an additional visual-ablation pass, and prioritizes positions whose predictions are more strongly supported by the image. Experiments on LLaDA-V and Lumina-DiMOO show that VIGOR consistently reduces hallucination and improves multimodal reasoning performance across benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VisSearch-Bench: Evaluating Long Horizon Visual Search in Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04894)**  `arXiv:2611.04894`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _ZhengXian Wu, Qianhao Nie, Zhuohong Chen, Junjie Gao, Yuyao Zhang, Yunyao Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents use web and image tools to answer questions beyond their internal knowledge. Some search tasks use images mainly to identify an initial entity, then rely on text retrieval. These tasks offer limited insight into how agents connect information across images, objects, and search steps. We therefore focus on visual context management. This involves organizing and using visual information and linking it to other findings during search. Visually intensive tasks require agents to combine object appearance, local details, or spatial relations with external information. We introduce VisSearch-Bench, with 204 human-written tasks across 7 main domains and 25 subdomains. It covers Set-wise Visual Search, Sequential Visual Search, Hybrid Wide-Deep Search, Competitive Visual Disambiguation, and Visual-Structure-Guided Search. Each task provides a reference answer, traceable evidence, and scoring rules. Accuracy measures complete task success, while Process Score measures the correctness and completeness of individual results and subquestion answers. Across 16 multimodal models, the best system fully solves only 26.47% of tasks, even with search tools. These results highlight the challenge of linking visual evidence with search results to produce complete answers.
  </details>

- **[ScienceVision: Can AI Observe and Reason Like a Scientist?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04087)**  `arXiv:2611.04087`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyi Ma, Jing Gu, Haozhe Zhao, Liang Chen, Yunfan Zhang, Haijun Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery builds on accurate observations interpreted through domain knowledge and reasoning. For scientific 3D data, this requires understanding structures and spatial relationships, from protein contacts to anatomical organization. Existing multimodal benchmarks largely evaluate text and 2D images, while scientific 3D evaluations vary in representations and protocols across disciplines, limiting cross-disciplinary comparison. To assess these foundational capabilities, we introduce ScienceVision, a cross-domain benchmark for scientific 3D understanding and reasoning through expert-level visual question answering (VQA). We construct 250 questions, with 50 each from Biology, Chemistry, Medical Imaging, Mechanical CAD, and Architecture, through a pipeline combining synthetic question generation with domain-expert review and annotation. Each task pairs a native 3D artifact with a scientific question, a specified response format, and a task-specific reference answer, requiring domain knowledge to be applied to geometric or topological evidence. We evaluate 12 models on the same questions in three settings: visual question answering with native 3D artifacts accessed through an agent harness's file and code-execution tools; the same workflow augmented with expert-inspired viewer inspection and measurement tools exposed through the Model Context Protocol (MCP); and visual question answering from prescribed rendered 2D images using an agent harness. All settings use the same task-specific deterministic final-answer scoring. The inspection environment supports view changes, target isolation, and slice inspection. In this main comparison, the best mean answer score is 0.53 out of 1. Mean scores in the native-artifact workflow without additional MCP tools exceed those in the rendered-image workflow for every model, by 17.2 percentage points on average.
  </details>

- **[To See or To Please: Uncovering Visual Sycophancy and Split Beliefs in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18373)**  `arXiv:2603.18373`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Hong, Shuxue Quan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When Vision-Language Models (VLMs) answer correctly, do they rely on the image? We introduce a Tri-Layer Diagnostic Framework that asks three questions of every (image, question) pair when the image is blanked: does the model register that the image is missing (Latent Anomaly Detection, LAD), does its output distribution depend on the image (Visual Necessity Score, VNS), and does it nonetheless commit to a content answer? The cascade assigns each pair of any existing benchmark to one of four modes: Perceptual Blindness, Language Shortcut, Visual Sycophancy (the blank image registers and matters, yet the model commits to a fabricated answer), or Robust Refusal. Across nine open-weight VLMs and 9,000 model-sample pairs, 28.1% are Visual Sycophancy, 18.6% Language Shortcut, 5.6% Perceptual Blindness, and 47.8% Robust Refusal. The modes are strongly model-specific: the Qwen3-VL models refuse on blank input in 93-98% of pairs, whereas Pixtral-12B commits to an answer in 72%. Within-generation scaling increases Visual Sycophancy in both Qwen2.5-VL (7B->72B: 25.5->40.1%) and Llama-3.2-Vision (11B->90B: 34.3->48.0%), while the newer Qwen3-VL generation nearly eliminates it. A blinded human study confirms that LAD and VNS track what annotators observe, and that 80% of Visual-Sycophancy responses carry no textual sign that the image was blank.
  </details>

- **[VidVec: Unlocking Video MLLM Embeddings for Video-Text Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08099)**  `arXiv:2602.08099`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Issar Tzachor, Dvir Samuel, Rami Ben-Ari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies have adapted generative Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) into embedding extractors for text-vision tasks, typically by fine-tuning them to produce universal representations. However, their performance on video-text retrieval often remains below that of dedicated Video Foundation Models. In this paper, we revisit the video-text retrieval capabilities of MLLMs. We first analyze the zero-shot retrieval capabilities of pretrained MLLMs and show that they already encode substantial retrieval-relevant information: combining intermediate-layer embeddings with a calibrated MLLM head yields strong performance without any training. To further exploit these capabilities, we introduce a lightweight text-based optimization strategy that requires no paired multimodal data. Our method uses dense video captions as rich textual surrogates for video semantics and short summaries as compact, query-like views, showing that a carefully designed text-alignment objective can improve multimodal alignment through the shared MLLM backbone. We backup these findings through in-depth analysis. Remarkably, our lightweight text-only optimization outperforms its counterpart trained with paired video-text supervision, which is substantially more computationally expensive. Furthermore, our method outperforms existing approaches, often by a substantial margin, and achieves state-of-the-art performance across common video-text retrieval benchmarks. More broadly, our findings highlight the untapped retrieval capabilities of pretrained MLLMs and establish text-only optimization as a more efficient and effective substitute for large-scale visual supervision.
  </details>

- **[Low-Density 3D Point Cloud Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05291)**  `arXiv:2611.05291`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ahmed Baha Ben Jmaa, Faten Chaieb_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D point cloud classification, a task in computer vision, has recently gained substantial interest due to its extensive applications across various domains such as augmented and virtual reality, robotics, and autonomous driving. However, classifying 3D point clouds becomes challenging in real-world scenarios where the density levels vary. These variations are due to limitations inherent to current sensing technologies and variable environmental conditions, which often prevent the capture of detailed and comprehensive geometric representations. Existing classification methods, which have demonstrated impressive results on dense point clouds, may be affected and could experience reduced performance when dealing with low-density point clouds. Addressing this challenge, this paper introduces LD-PointNet++, an architecture optimized for the classification of low-density point clouds. To pave the way for LD-PointNet++, we conduct the first benchmarking study that systematically evaluates state-of-the-art point cloud classification methods across varying densities, revealing significant performance declines in low-density environments. Experimental results on the ModelNet benchmark dataset demonstrate that LD-PointNet++ not only outperforms these methods in low-density scenarios but also demonstrates consistent accuracy above 90% across different density levels.
  </details>

- **[Anchored Rank-Adversarial Fine-Tuning Pretrained Visual Place Recognition Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05286)**  `arXiv:2611.05286`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mostafa Kira, Bader Rasheed, Manuel Mazzara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual place recognition (VPR) localises a camera by retrieving similar geo-tagged images, and current foundation-model VPR models are accurate across cities, seasons and illumination, but not robust to adversarial perturbations: under a white-box attack on the ranking at an imperceptible budget of 1/255, SuperVLAD and BoQ keep at most 0.2% top-1 recall (R@1) on four benchmarks. We propose anchored rank-adversarial fine-tuning (ARAFT), which fine-tunes a released model with a listwise rank loss on each adversarial query's hardest positive and an anchor to the clean descriptor of the frozen pretrained model. Checkpoints are selected under a clean-accuracy budget fixed in advance, and the full trade-off is reported. Within five validation R@1 points of clean cost, plain adversarial training has no admissible checkpoint, whereas ARAFT raises robust R@1 from zero to 47.3 on SuperVLAD and 55.3 on BoQ; plain adversarial training exceeds this only at about three times the clean cost. Against unbudgeted plain adversarial training on the test sets, ARAFT matches its robust R@1 at 2/255 with about ten more clean R@1 points. At 4/255, plain adversarial training is 6-9 robust R@1 points ahead, while ARAFT still keeps its ten-point clean advantage. Developed on SuperVLAD, ARAFT transfers to BoQ without re-tuning, and its robustness holds against stronger optimisers, with no sign of gradient masking.
  </details>

- **[YANAMI: A Large-Scale Multimodal Dataset and Benchmark for Multiturn Stateful Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05280)**  `arXiv:2611.05280`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shengyu Wang, Tengyue Nong, Junming Liu, Haosong Peng, Siyu Li, Ruiqi Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) integrate visual understanding, generation, and editing within a single unified framework. However, existing unified multimodal datasets and benchmarks remain dominated by single-turn tasks. Even in recent multi-turn datasets, many turns can be solved independently without tracking information across turns. Consequently, they provide limited supervision and evaluation for multimodal stateful interaction, which requires models to understand, maintain, update, and faithfully realize evolving dialogue states across turns. To address this gap, we introduce YANAMI, the first large-scale multimodal dialogue dataset and benchmark for stateful interaction, comprising YANAMI-400K and YANAMI-Bench. YANAMI-400K contains 400K multi-turn multimodal dialogues, with more than 2M images and 2.6M dialogue turns. The dialogues span four representative scenarios: Multi-Image Fusion, Image Editing, World Modeling, and Visual Reasoning, and require models to continuously track, preserve, and revise evolving multimodal dialogue states across turns. YANAMI-Bench comprises 400 multi-turn dialogues for systematically evaluating multimodal stateful interaction. It jointly evaluates Dialogue State Understanding and Visual Realization, characterizing the gap between models' ability to understand evolving dialogue states and their ability to faithfully realize them in visual outputs. Experiments show that fine-tuning BAGEL on YANAMI-400K improves image editing, generation, and multimodal stateful interaction by 10.3%, 2.0%, and 13.23%, respectively, while preserving its general visual understanding capabilities. Evaluations on YANAMI-Bench further reveal that existing UMMs still struggle to maintain, update, and visually realize evolving multimodal dialogue states.
  </details>

- **[Route Anything: A Study of Model and Policy Routing for General-Purpose AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05275)**  `arXiv:2611.05275`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengfei Zhou, Yixing Ma, Hexin Wang, Weidong Tang, Zhenglin Wan, Wangbo Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial general intelligence (AGI) requires a method that can understand, generate, reason, and act across diverse domains and modalities. Rather than relying on a single model to master every capability, a promising path toward AGI now is to orchestrate a heterogeneous ecosystem of specialized models, tools, and policies (candidates) into a unified AI system. At the heart of this vision lies a fundamental question: how can an AI system dynamically select and coordinate the most capable candidate for each task while balancing performance and computational cost? In this work, we present Route Anything, a unified framework for studying model and policy routing across general-purpose AI systems. We develop a unified theoretical foundation that characterizes optimal routing under quality--cost trade-offs and establishes how the information available to a router determines its attainable performance. To systematically investigate routing across diverse capabilities, we first construct an omni-modal trajectory matrix spanning 25 benchmarks across language understanding, image, video, and speech generation, coding and workplace agents, and robotic manipulation, comprising 374,401 scored runs from 452 candidate configurations. Building on this framework, we train and test different routers to investigate the potential of omni-modal model routing, the capabilities of learned routers, and the conditions under which routing improves efficiency. Our results reveal substantial opportunities for capability orchestration: per-input oracle routing outperforms the best fixed candidate by 5.4 points, and learned routers outperform it by 0.3 points across 16 benchmarks, by 2.1 points on video generation, and by 1.1 points on robot manipulation while using 24% less policy time. More importantly, routing raises efficiency by 8.82x on average compared with the reference model.
  </details>

- **[Robust Reconstruction of Power Lines from Incomplete Mobile and Aerial LiDAR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05197)**  `arXiv:2611.05197`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mohamed Gaha, Damos Ayobo Abongo, Philippe Massicotte, Eya Ben Kacem, Wael Jaafar, Rami Langar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate vegetation-encroachment assessment and risk mitigation require electric utilities to accurately locate and characterize distribution-network assets, including poles and power lines, and distinguish among medium-voltage, low-voltage, neutral, and telecommunication lines. Mobile and aerial Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) point clouds provide detailed 3D representations of these assets and their surroundings. However, power-line detection and reconstruction remain challenging because conductor returns are often sparse, incomplete, noisy, or occluded by vegetation. These challenges are especially significant in distribution networks, where lower pole heights and complex line configurations place conductors close to trees. In addition, mobile and aerial LiDAR datasets differ substantially in point density, viewing geometry, spatial coverage, and occlusion patterns. We propose a robust and automated reconstruction method combining scaled density-based clustering, robust polynomial fitting, and iterative fragment merging to recover individual conductors under severe data incompleteness and occlusion. Following semantic classification, the same reconstruction pipeline processes both mobile and aerial LiDAR data without modality-specific code or parameter tuning. The method is evaluated through degradation experiments, benchmarking, parameter sensitivity analysis, and large-scale validation. On the aerial dataset, it achieves 96.87% precision, 96.66% recall, a 96.76% F$_1$-score, and a geometric RMSE of 0.127 m. On the mobile dataset, where conductor-level reference annotations were unavailable, it achieves a point-to-model RMSE of 0.045 m and a reconstruction coverage of 95.27%. Source code, documentation, and evaluation data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Cell Crops Share a Parent: Measuring and Isolating Acquisition Leakage in Cervical Cytology Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05145)**  `arXiv:2611.05145`  `cs.CV`  
  _Snehasish Ghosh, Soham Mitra, Antareep Majumdar, KC Santosh, Nibaran Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public cervical cytology benchmarks release many cell crops cut from far fewer microscope fields or slides and are often split at the crop level, so test cells share acquisition parents with training cells. In a preregistered study, crop-level splitting raised SIPaKMeD accuracy by 0.97 percentage points across twelve architectures and twenty seeds (95% CI 0.59-1.39), and CRIC accuracy by 12.08 points (11.16-12.99). On SIPaKMeD, a factorial that fixes training crop and parent counts attributes the gain to sibling exposure (1.73 points, 0.95-2.51), not to the extra parent diversity of crop-level splits (0.00). Parent identity is decodable at about 100 times chance, yet erasing 82% of its above-floor linear recoverability left the gap unchanged, and Grad-CAM did not distinguish leaking from clean models; matching test cells' color statistics to an unseen same-class field removed most of the advantage. Decodability and saliency cannot substitute for a parent-disjoint split.
  </details>

- **[Manifold Embedding of Deep Image Features for Image Matching via Neural Adjoint Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05083)**  `arXiv:2611.05083`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nafie El Amrani, Moritz Heep, Florian Bernard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating semantic correspondences between different object instances of similar categories in real-world images is a fundamental challenge in computer vision. While the recent progress in foundation models has significantly advanced solutions for such difficult image correspondence problems, respective methods are often insufficiently regularised. For example, common nearest-neighbour matching with foundation model features often results in noisy correspondences, which is particularly prominent when solving for dense correspondences. In this work, we tackle image matching via functional maps, which have been popularised for 3D shape matching due to their powerful spectral formalism utilising an efficient low-dimensional linear representation. However, functional maps require that domains (in our cases the rectangular image grids) are equipped with an informative and non-trivial geometry. To define a meaningful manifold structure for images, we use Galerkin's method from finite elements to derive a discrete Laplace-Beltrami operator (LBO) based on a manifold embedding of deep image features. Further, we leverage a neural adjoint map that allows to represent non-linear mappings. We demonstrate that our method sets the new state of the art among zero-shot image correspondence methods on multiple dense correspondence benchmarks of real-world images. Our code is publicly available:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ProAlign: Progressive Positional and Prototype-Guided Alignment for Aerial-Ground Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04980)**  `arXiv:2611.04980`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiao Li, Jing Chen, Cong Wu, Kun He, Ruiying Du, Lei Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial-Ground Person Re-Identification (AG-ReID) aims to match pedestrian images captured by unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) and ground-based surveillance cameras. The task remains highly challenging due to severe viewpoint discrepancies, frequent occlusions, and substantial domain gaps between aerial and ground imagery. Beyond these observable factors, existing methods often overlook two fundamental structural issues: cumulative positional drift during hierarchical feature extraction and semantic inconsistency across cross-view feature distributions. To address these challenges, we propose ProAlign, a Progressive Positional and Prototype-Guided Alignment Network for AG-ReID. ProAlign comprises two key components: a Layer-wise Progressive Positional Embedding (LPPE) module and a View-Aware Prototype Contrastive Learning (PCL) module. LPPE performs adaptive positional calibration throughout transformer layers by employing conditional positional encoding in shallow layers to mitigate local spatial distortions, while introducing learnable static positional embeddings in deeper layers to reinforce global semantic priors. Meanwhile, PCL maintains view-specific aerial and ground prototypes for each identity and enforces cross-view semantic consistency via a dual-prototype contrastive objective. Extensive experiments on CARGO and two real-world AG-ReID benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of ProAlign, achieving state-of-the-art overall retrieval performance on CARGO with improvements of 1.60% in Rank-1 accuracy and 5.01% in mAP over the strongest competing methods.
  </details>

- **[NightIQA: A Comprehensive Dataset and Benchmark for MLLM-Based Nighttime Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04811)**  `arXiv:2611.04811`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiding Tian, Jiaojiao Yi, Jiayuan Lu, Pufan Xu, Jinlin Wu, Changyuan Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Nighttime images are inherently susceptible to complex, compound degradations, making accurate quality assessment exceptionally challenging. Score-based IQA methods condense quality into a single number, lacking the multi-dimensional, language-grounded reasoning required for nighttime evaluation. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer a more comprehensive alternative, yet remain constrained by a critical data gap: existing real-world IQA datasets are overwhelmingly daytime-centric and often built from outdated captures, while synthetic alternatives lack nighttime-specific degradation modeling, yielding artifacts far removed from authentic nighttime conditions. To bridge this gap, we present NightIQA, a dataset and benchmark suite purpose-built for MLLM-based nighttime IQA. It comprises three core components: NightIQA-Real, a real-world dataset of 1,769 high-resolution images that we captured ourselves with modern equipment, encompassing six canonical distortion types and structured multi-dimensional annotations; NightIQA-Bench, a benchmark of 1,078 visually grounded multiple-choice questions tailored to MLLM-based nighttime IQA, organized around a four-level evaluation hierarchy spanning Perception, Technical Assessment, Localization, and Reasoning; and NightIQA-Syn, an inherently scalable, scene-aware synthesis pipeline that jointly models global-level and instance-level degradations, with an initial release of 80k high-fidelity images alongside the full generation code. Fine-tuned on NightIQA with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Qwen3-VL-8B outperforms open-source IQA methods and achieves higher overall accuracy than leading closed-source models, highlighting the value of domain-specific data and structured supervision.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Local Refinement: Wide-Area Precise Cross-View Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04759)**  `arXiv:2611.04759`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qingguo Hu, Xinyu Ma, Yujie Lin, Yaoxiang Wang, Qi Shi, Zonghao Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained cross-view localization aims to precisely localize a ground camera against geo-referenced aerial imagery, but current methods typically assume a narrow geographic prior, substantially limiting their applicability when upstream localization remains coarse. To bridge this gap, we study this task over much larger aerial regions. However, this seemingly simple extension fundamentally changes the localization problem: the growing disparity in spatial extent between ground and aerial views makes reliable cross-view matching difficult to establish directly, while the increasing number of look-alike locations in larger regions introduces substantial visual ambiguity. To address these challenges, we propose RingMatch, a search-and-verification framework for wide-area cross-view localization. RingMatch first leverages predictive uncertainty to uncover plausible locations and progressively zooms into their surroundings to obtain increasingly fine-grained evidence, yielding a compact set of promising candidates. We then introduce the depth ring, a novel geometric representation that reveals subtle variations in the spatial layout around a location and serves as a view-shared spatial signature for an LVLM verifier to resolve residual ambiguity. To benchmark this setting, we construct VIGOR-Pro by composing VIGOR aerial tiles into contiguous regions with spatial extents of up to 400 m. Experiments show that RingMatch maintains strong localization performance as the aerial region expands, consistently outperforming representative end-to-end and retrieval-based methods.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Unlabeled Photographs for General Oral Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04709)**  `arXiv:2611.04709`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhong-Shi Zhang, Zi-Li Yu, Xiao Xiao, Huan Liu, Gui-Song Xia, Cui Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Oral photographs contain rich clinical information and hold significant potential for artificial intelligence applications; however, most models are trained for individual tasks and rely on costly expert annotations. We hypothesized that self-supervised training on unlabeled oral photographs could produce transferable visual representations for heterogeneous vision tasks. To test this hypothesis, we developed DOVE (Dental Optical Visual Encoder) by domain-adaptive self-supervised pretraining on approximately 530,000 unlabeled oral photographs collected from publicly accessible sources. DOVE was benchmarked against 3 established baselines across 5 classification datasets and 4 segmentation datasets, covering both frozen backbone evaluation and end-to-end finetuning. DOVE achieved the strongest overall performance, ranking best in few-shot transfer and label efficiency on most benchmarks. Under the 20-shot frozen-backbone protocol, DOVE outperformed the second best DINOv3 by 4.37 percentage points in mean macro-F1 across classification datasets and by 4.49 percentage points in mean macro-Dice across segmentation tasks. Under end-to-end finetuning, DOVE achieved at least 80% of the strongest full-data baseline's performance across most evaluation datasets using only 10% of the labeled training data. Qualitative retrieval and feature analyses further indicated better organization of image-level and spatial representations. These findings support DOVE as a generalizable and label-efficient backbone for oral vision research.
  </details>

- **[BhopalMLS: A LiDAR Dataset for Semantic Scene Understanding in Heterogeneous Urban Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04654)**  `arXiv:2611.04654`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pyare Lal Chauhan, Vaibhav Kumar, Bharat Lohani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce BhopalMLS, a large-scale Mobile Laser Scanning (MLS) dataset from a dense, heterogeneous urban environment in Bhopal, India, with mixed traffic, informal structures, and narrow unplanned streets. It spans approximately 10 km of road corridors with over 1.2 billion points and 19,518 annotated instances across 62 fine-grained static categories, grouped into 12-class and 21-class benchmark taxonomies; 6 optional moving-class subclasses support dynamic-object analysis. Existing 3D datasets, collected in well-planned cities, are structurally more uniform than such unplanned settings, and lack their subtype diversity. On semantic and instance segmentation at both granularities, fine-tuned foundation models surpass supervised baselines: Utonia(L) reaches 71.28% mIoU (vs. PTv3's 68.04% from scratch) and 44.07% mAP50 on the 12-class benchmark. Cross-region experiments against four established datasets (Ke-MLS, Toronto-3D, Paris-Lille-3D, SemanticKITTI) reveal a consistent asymmetric transferability: models trained on BhopalMLS transfer better than the reverse on every pair, for both Utonia(L) and Concerto(L) (mean 21.5, up to 36 mIoU points), holding even when the target is denser. Factor-controlled retraining at a fixed label budget, repeated on both models, attributes this to the coverage of BhopalMLS's subtype inventory rather than to dataset size or corridor geometry: collapsing each class to a single subtype costs 13.0 and 13.3 mIoU points for Utonia(L) and Concerto(L). BhopalMLS therefore offers a testbed for 3D perception beyond well-planned cities. Data and an interactive viewer are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MambaHand: Local Identity and Behavioral Sequence Learning with Mamba for Dynamic Hand Gesture Authentication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04626)**  `arXiv:2611.04626`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zenan Lin, Junduan Huang, Xilai Wang, Wenxiong Kang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic hand gesture authentication (DHGA) is an emerging biometric technique that contains both physiological and behavioral identity characteristics. However, existing methods primarily rely on convolutional networks or manually designed motion representations, making it difficult to jointly capture fine-grained local identity differences and long-range behavioral dependencies during hand gesture execution. To address these challenges, we propose a two-stream identity representation learning framework, MambaHand Net, to combine CNNs with Mamba for DHGA. The proposed framework consists of a Local Identity Stream (LIS) and a Behavioral Sequential Stream (BSS). The LIS extracts frame-wise local identity cues with a convolutional network, while the BSS preserves spatial tokens from intermediate visual features and constructs first-order and second-order temporal difference sequences to model multi-order behavioral variations. Moreover, the bidirectional Mamba is incorporated into the BSS to model semantic motion across different spatial locations, enabling efficient long-range modeling of hand gesture execution habits. Finally, attention-guided spatial token aggregation and a two-stream fusion strategy are used to integrate local identity cues and multi-order behavioral patterns into a discriminative identity representation. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) average performance on the SCUT-DHGA benchmark dataset, demonstrating that combining CNNs with Mamba is an effective new solution for DHGA.
  </details>

- **[Gradient-Guided Orientation Learning for Weakly Supervised Oriented Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04616)**  `arXiv:2611.04616`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyong Jang, Juheon Park, Minyoung Jeon, Jaehyup Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weakly supervised oriented object detection (WS-OOD) aims to reduce the annotation cost of rotated bounding boxes by leveraging cheaper annotations such as horizontal boxes or points. However, these weak annotations provide no explicit orientation information, forcing existing methods to infer object angles through indirect geometric consistency or equivariant constraints. Such indirect supervision often becomes ambiguous and unstable under real-world appearance variations, including illumination changes, color shifts, shadows, and background clutter. In this paper, we present a simple yet effective gradient-guided orientation learning strategy for WS-OOD. Motivated by the observation that the input image itself contains useful orientation priors, our method extracts dominant gradient-based orientation cues from object regions and converts them into pseudo-angle labels for auxiliary supervision, enabling the detector to learn orientation-aware representations without requiring rotated bounding box annotations or extra learnable parameters. Furthermore, since AP can mask substantial angular errors when spatial IoU remains high, we introduce an orientation-aware evaluation metric that incorporates angular discrepancies into the standard AP protocol. Extensive experiments on weakly supervised oriented detection benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed pseudo-angle supervision improves both detection performance and orientation estimation quality, while the proposed metric provides a more faithful assessment of angle prediction than conventional AP alone.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Path Mamba Diffusion for 3D Point Cloud Semantic Scene Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04583)**  `arXiv:2611.04583`  `cs.CV`  
  _Li Liang, Naveed Akhtar, Jordan Vice, Anwaar Ulhaq, Ajmal Saeed Mian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D semantic scene completion plays a critical role in enabling various downstream tasks within autonomous systems by estimating both missing geometric structures and semantic information in scene data. In this work, we introduce a unique neural framework that integrates state space modeling with diffusion-based generative approaches to enhance 3D point cloud semantic scene completion. We propose \textit{MPath Mamba} diffusion network, leveraging a skip-triple Mamba structure, dimensional decomposition residuals, and multi-directional dilations within multi-path data to efficiently process long-sequence data for feature extraction. The proposed method operates in the conditioned latent space of a variational autoencoder, enhanced by a state-space mechanism to improve 3D point cloud representation and reconstruction. Extensive experiments conducted on the SemanticKITTI benchmark demonstrate that our approach achieves highly competitive performance for 3D point cloud semantic scene completion.
  </details>

- **[SEEK-VAU: Towards Evidence-Faithful Video Anomaly Understanding via Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04554)**  `arXiv:2611.04554`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minghe Wang, Weijun Zeng, Dapeng Luo, Xinyue Wang, Nong Sang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most video anomaly understanding (VAU) systems still infer anomaly judgments from evidence collected in a feed-forward way under predefined rules, typically sampled frames, or events clustered from such frames. This protocol fails when the collected evidence is incomplete under imperfect predefined rules, because once reasoning begins the model can no longer search for the missing evidence. We address this limitation with SEEK-VAU, which reframes VAU as an agentic search-and-verify interaction protocol under a bounded search budget: instead of passively reasoning over pre-collected evidence, the policy actively gathers evidence across the three stages of an anomaly event chain (precursor, trigger, and aftermath) and verifies its sufficiency before finalization, turning event-chain completeness into an explicit design rather than an outcome of predefined rules. A bounded budget caps the cost of this search, but does not by itself teach the policy where to look or when to stop. To make this behavior learnable, we introduce evidence-faithful counterfactual verification (EFCV), which rewards selected evidence that remains supportive, compact, and necessary under counterfactual verification. We further introduce SEEK-Bench, featuring video-level episodes with temporal interval annotations, semantic QA and event-chain stage labels. Together, SEEK-VAU and SEEK-Bench establish a strong foundation for evidence-faithful and actively verifiable VAU.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Falsify: Hierarchical Multiple Instance Learning for High Resolution AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04529)**  `arXiv:2611.04529`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoran Chen, Daiqi Gao, Xinyuan Li, Yongwei Wang, Lei Zhu, Rui Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in generative models have made synthetic images increasingly photorealistic, posing growing challenges to reliable and generalizable detection of AI-generated images. Existing methods typically rely on holistic image-level classification, which may overlook local artifacts, especially in high-resolution images. To address this limitation, we formulate this task as a falsification-driven multiple instance learning problem, where a single fake patch can falsify the whole image, while a real image requires all patches to be authentic. Based on this formulation, we propose \textit{Learning to Falsify (LTF)}, a hierarchical multiple instance learning framework that models an image as multiple bags of instances across different spatial levels and aggregates their predictions under the falsification-inspired principle. We further introduce Cross-Shuffle during training to encourage the model to learn local artifact evidence rather than image-level shortcuts. Furthermore, we propose the \textit{Falsification-driven Test-time Aggregation Strategy (FATAS)}, a plug-and-play module that extends the same falsification-inspired principle to existing detectors by aggregating patch-level predictions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LTF achieves strong generalization and robustness across multiple benchmarks, while FATAS substantially improves representative detectors in high-resolution scenarios without retraining. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[3DGS-VBench+ and 3DGSV-Assessor: A Multi-Dimensional Subjective Benchmark and a Reference-Flexible Objective Model for 3D Gaussian Splatting Video Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04498)**  `arXiv:2611.04498`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuke Xing, William Gordon, Jiarui Wang, Yujie Zhang, Guangtao Zhai, Yiling Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables real-time novel view synthesis (NVS), but its heavy storage makes compression essential, and both training and compression introduce representation-specific distortions. Since generative compression in many methods treats geometric and color attributes via different parameters, it can induce dimension-specific distortions, motivating multi-dimensional assessment. Moreover, real-world captures generally lack an aligned ground-truth video for a synthesized, continuous camera trajectory, leaving only sparse captured views as references. To enable multi-dimensional quality assessment of 3DGS videos with scarce aligned references, we first present 3DGS-VBench+, a large-scale subjective video quality assessment (VQA) benchmark comprising 3{,}036 3DGS reconstructions, each paired with a rendered video, across 11 real-world and 22 synthetic scenes, generated by 6 representative compression algorithms at systematically designed levels and annotated with 9{,}108 MOSs covering overall, geometry, and color quality. Based on 3DGS-VBench+, we propose 3DGSV-Assessor, a reference-flexible, large multimodal framework that scores all three dimensions in one generation pass and fuses a ranking-based reference cue into the frozen regressor through a bounded gate, supporting both reduced-reference (RR) and no-reference (NR) assessment. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art correlation with human perception on 3DGS-VBench+ and strong zero-shot generalization to other NVS benchmarks. The dataset and code will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Is Forgetting Unified? Exploring Concept Unlearning in Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04467)**  `arXiv:2611.04467`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhongkai Xue, Junkai Chen, Mingcong Lei, Rumo Zhang, Yixiao Liu, Zhimin Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal models (UMMs) integrate image generation and visual understanding, but are existing single-pathway unlearning methods sufficient to suppress target concepts across both? In this work, we study cross-pathway concept unlearning. Our goal is to prevent a model from both generating images of a target concept and identifying it in input images, while preserving other capabilities. To this end, we introduce UUBench, a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 target concepts and 60 semantically related neighboring concepts. Experiments on representative UMMs show that the evaluated single-pathway methods achieve limited joint forgetting: generation-side editors strongly suppress target generation but yield limited forgetting in visual understanding, while understanding-side baselines struggle to achieve substantial forgetting even in their own pathway. Their direct composition also yields limited joint forgetting. We therefore propose UniForget, achieving joint forgetting rates of 80.0% on BAGEL-7B-MoT and 92.7% on Janus-Pro-7B, outperforming representative baselines. Further analysis reveals trade-offs between joint forgetting and capability preservation.
  </details>

- **[VidUEU-Agent: A Data-Curation Agent for Multimodal Understanding, Editing, and Unified Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04418)**  `arXiv:2611.04418`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tengju Ru, Weitong Lian, Zecong Tang, Lingyi Meng, Haoran Li, Zhejun Cui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of multimodal learning is heavily constrained by the scarcity and high annotation cost of high-quality instruction data. While raw videos offer an abundant and dynamic source of visual knowledge, transforming them into precise instruction data remains highly challenging. To bridge this gap, we present VidUEU-Agent, an automated pipeline that synthesizes instruction data from raw videos for multimodal understanding, editing, and unified tasks. VidUEU-Agent follows a four-stage pipeline: it performs global perception to filter low-quality shots, mines keyframes and multimodal signals, constructs metadata and routes the metadata to synthesize instruction samples. More importantly, our agent framework can autonomously iterate the data distribution based on downstream training results, thereby optimizing data quality, balance, and training effectiveness. Scaling this pipeline, we construct VidUEU-100K, a large-scale dataset of 100K training samples spanning multiple domains and tasks, providing a robust foundation for developing comprehensive multimodal capabilities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning baseline models on VidUEU-100K yields significant and consistent performance gains across diverse representative benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of our proposed agent framework and dataset.
  </details>

- **[Efficient and Faithful High-Resolution Image Editing with Sparse Cross-Resolution Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04416)**  `arXiv:2611.04416`  `cs.CV`  
  _Maosen Zhao, Xianfang Zeng, Pengtao Chen, Peng Ye, Gang Yu, Jiayuan Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-based image editors still primarily operate at 1K, whereas practical editing increasingly requires faithful 2K/4K outputs. Dense high-resolution adaptation preserves the editing trajectory but incurs expensive target-source attention; editing at 1K followed by external refinement is cheaper but can separate detail restoration from the editing trajectory. We introduce FLASH, a native high-resolution editor based on sparse cross-resolution denoising. A frozen 1K branch provides a semantic and structural anchor, while a target-resolution branch reconstructs local detail within the same Transformer. An asymmetric topology retains full 1K attention, guides high-resolution queries with spatially aligned low-resolution noisy-target states, and confines high-resolution target-reference interaction to local Halo neighborhoods. Resolution-specific diffusion-state mapping and spatial YaRN adapt the pretrained editor to larger grids. An optional, training-free progressive path replaces early high-resolution denoising with a 1K prefix and completes refinement in the same editor. On VINS-4KEval, the direct model achieves the strongest structural fidelity among the compared methods at both 2K and 4K. A separate 4K forward benchmark with R = 1 records 4.2x faster total forward execution and 25.3x faster attention than dense target-reference attention. Progressive inference reduces end-to-end 4K latency by 30.5%, with a modest quality cost.
  </details>

- **[EndoS3:Endoscopic Semantic Splatting SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04358)**  `arXiv:2611.04358`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mona Sheikh Zeinoddin, Benjamin Wright, Zafer Tandogdu, Greg Shaw, Evangelos Mazomenos, Danail Stoyanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: Despite advances in 3D surgical scene understanding, semantic simultaneous localisation and mapping (SLAM) remains under-explored for two key reasons. First, it requires solving three challenging tasks in parallel: camera pose tracking, high-fidelity deformable reconstruction, and semantic mapping, making progress particularly demanding. Second, the scarcity of datasets providing the necessary ground truth to evaluate all three tasks comprehensively under conditions that closely reflect real surgical procedures environments. Method: We propose EndoS3, a Gaussian Splatting-based semantic SLAM framework that uses instance masks to guide all three tasks. An instance-level motion decomposition module separates static and dynamic instances by evaluating residual flow after compensating for camera motion, accounting for flow noise and comparing motion across neighbouring instances. The camera is tracked against the static Gaussians, while an instance-aware warp field gives each dynamic instance its own control nodes and deformation network. A semantic node allocation module redistributes a fixed node budget across instances according to their size and non-rigidity. We also introduce CRCD++, an ex-vivo Cholecystectomy dataset with RGB-D frames, camera pose and instance annotations for full semantic SLAM evaluation. Results: EndoS3 surpasses all state-of-the-art general-purpose and surgical semantic SLAM methods in tracking, reconstruction and semantic mapping on the CRCD++ and SemSup datasets. Conclusion: Semantic-aware motion and deformation modelling is vital to surgical semantic SLAM. EndoS3 and CRCD++ offer a method and benchmark for improved geometric and semantic understanding in endoscopy.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Spatial Blindness in Multiple Instance Learning for Whole-Slide Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04337)**  `arXiv:2611.04337`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangyu Li, Jincheng Li, Xing Xie, Bin Guan, Shaohua Kevin Zhou, Rui Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole-slide multiple instance learning can predict diagnostic and prognostic labels from tissue composition while making limited use of spatial organization. Providing coordinates or a graph does not establish that a trained predictor uses tissue neighborhoods. We examine this gap as spatial blindness and hypothesize that early composition learning can weaken supervision of a spatial pathway. We introduce ResTopoMIL, which freezes a learned composition predictor before training a graph residual with a coordinate-reassignment separation loss. Permutation and information analyses distinguish architectural symmetry, available spatial information and the proposed optimization mechanism. On a controlled spatial task, staged training achieved an AUC of 0.987, compared with 0.684 for the same jointly trained architecture. Performance remained high on a composition-only task. Across four WSI classification and five survival benchmarks, ResTopoMIL improved mean primary performance on eight tasks; four improvements remained significant after Holm-adjusted patient-cluster tests. Coordinate-free residual controls recovered some of the gains, while neighborhood destruction reduced performance close to the composition anchor. Neighborhood-preserving transformations and anatomy-stratified perturbations further characterized this dependence. These results support fixing a composition reference as a practical intervention for learning spatial corrections, while retaining additional appearance capacity as a contributing explanation.
  </details>

- **[SteelHuman: Predicting Operator Estimates of Steel-Scrap Composition from Sparse RGB Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04317)**  `arXiv:2611.04317`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jovan Kamchev, Zoran Ivanovski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Steel scrap composition estimation is an important part of yield planning and fair supplier compensation. Measuring the composition of a multi-ton pile is an inherently laborious process, so the assessment is often left to trained human operators. Recent works tackle composition-level tasks, but require structured acquisition and multi-stage preprocessing. We study a simpler approach to steel scrap load assessment by aggregating features from sparse, handheld views of the pile. On 600 out-of-fold piles, frozen DINOv2 tokens aggregated uniformly across views reach 13.4% total variation (TV) distance against operator estimates. Most remaining error lies in allocation among four thickness-defined heavy melting scrap (HMS) classes, yet image-dependent allocation reduces within-HMS TV from 25.1% under a training-set split prior to 11.2%. Due to the lack of public datasets and benchmarks for predicting composition, external evaluation relies on the public DOES dataset intended for classification. A classifier using the same frozen features reaches 93.97% accuracy, exceeding the published results we compare against. The composition model also aligns with related DOES categories without adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Weight-Space Programming for Improving Image-Restoration Diffusion Models: Technical Conjectures and a Recursive Improvement Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04268)**  `arXiv:2611.04268`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuoran Zheng, Doubao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can an LLM directly edit the weights of a diffusion-based image-restoration model to \emph{improve} restoration quality---without gradients, paired data, or fine-tuning? Recent experiments show frontier LLMs can fix structural bugs by modifying as little as \(0.016\%\) of parameters, but cannot instill new capabilities. We ask whether weight-space programming (WSP) can move from \emph{debugging} to \emph{improvement} in the precise-regression setting of image restoration (deblurring, super-resolution, denoising, inpainting). Restoration diffusion UNets present a middle ground: low-level signal-processing layers are concentrated enough for targeted weight-direction edits, while high-level semantic layers remain distributed and resistant to sparse modification. We propose three technical conjectures: (C1) an LLM can localize the layer behind a quality gap (over-smoothing, color shift, hallucinated texture) within a 200 forward-pass budget; (C2) editing a small set of identified weight directions (e.g., scaling high-frequency filter norms, correcting output-bias channels) can measurably improve PSNR and perceptual quality; (C3) a recursive loop---diagnose \(\rightarrow\) edit \(\rightarrow\) evaluate \(\rightarrow\) re-diagnose---can compound gains across rounds, forming a minimal recursive self-improvement (RSI) loop for vision. We analyze the required AI capability (arguing targeted edits need not superintelligence, while general capability generation does) and detail the challenges of precise pixel-level regression. We provide a PyTorch-style deployment pipeline and outline a benchmark. This manuscript is conjectural, intended to open a new subtopic for image restoration.
  </details>

- **[Sketch-Omni: Instance-Level Sketch-Based Visual Prompting for Perception MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04262)**  `arXiv:2611.04262`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dongyu Wang, Dar-Yen Chen, Zhiming Hong, Arkaprabha Basu, Yi-Zhe Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Promptable perception, from GroundingDINO to Rex-Omni, puts one model behind one prompt interface and returns coordinates for any localisation task. Every model in that line was built for photographs; none has been given a sketch. We present Sketch-Omni, to our knowledge the first perception MLLM built to work on sketches and from them. On a sketch, the sketch is the image, and text prompts detect, point, resolve object and spatial referring expressions, and ground affordances in it. From a sketch, the sketch is the prompt, and a drawing of one object localises that object in a photograph, even among several of the same category, a query that neither a category name nor a photo exemplar can pose. Two obstacles stand in the way. A photo-trained model's attention is tuned to colour and texture, which a sketch does not have, and no dataset pairs a drawing with the one instance it depicts among others of its kind. We build the training data instead of collecting it. An edge detector turns every labelled photograph into a sketch-like drawing that keeps the photograph's labels, and two constructions produce sketch-to-photo instance pairs from existing data. On top of this, photo-guided relation alignment passes each photograph and its edge sketch through the same model and trains the sketch pass to reproduce how the photograph pass relates phrases to patches and patches to each other. Not one training label is drawn by hand. Sketch-Omni reaches 91.4 F1 on sketch-prompted localisation against 81.8 for the strongest general-purpose MLLM, with a lead that doubles when the photograph holds several instances of the queried category, and it is best in 39 of 40 evaluation cells across six tasks. Code, model, and a hand-annotated benchmark of 2754 sketch-to-photo links will be released.
  </details>

- **[CounterSketch: Physics-Grounded Counterfactual Reasoning for Sketch-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04244)**  `arXiv:2611.04244`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hien Trinh, Tan-Hiep To, Dinh-Khoi Vo, Tam V. Nguyen, Minh-Triet Tran, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual sketch animation explores how a static sketch evolves under a hypothetical intervention. We present CounterSketch, a physics-grounded framework that transforms natural-language ``What if...?'' queries into physically plausible sketch animations. The framework integrates VLM-guided scene grounding, causal event-chain reasoning, constraint-based trajectory generation, and verification. It models physical transitions to update the scene graph, computes continuous object trajectories, and validates them against physical, geometric, and semantic constraints. The verified trajectories are then projected into object-level motion bounding boxes to guide downstream sketch-video synthesis. Experiments on CounterSketchBench (80 questions) demonstrate that physics-grounded trajectories provide stronger motion guidance than direct VLM predictions.
  </details>

- **[ReconDreamer#: Self-Evolving Closed-Loop Synergy of Reconstruction and Generation for Dynamic Driving Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04173)**  `arXiv:2611.04173`  `cs.CV`  
  _Songling Liu, Guosheng Zhao, Xiaofeng Wang, Chaojun Ni, Guan Huang, Jiwen Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic driving scene reconstruction has made remarkable strides in recent years by leveraging generative priors, particularly in the quality of novel trajectory rendering. Nevertheless, a significant gap remains between the generated data and real-world sensor observations, severely hindering further improvements in reconstruction fidelity. In this paper, we present ReconDreamer#, a self-evolving framework that synergizes reconstruction and generation for dynamic driving scenes, which substantially bridges the gap between synthesized novel trajectory data and recorded real-world observations. Within this self-evolving closed-loop framework, the generative and reconstruction models mutually reinforce each other through iterative refinement, ultimately yielding a significant enhancement in the 3D representation capability of the reconstruction model. Additionally, we introduce a geometry-prior-based adaptive ground model to further enhance the rendering quality of the reconstruction model. To validate the effectiveness of the proposed method, we conduct comprehensive evaluations on Waymo, nuScenes, PandaSet, and EUVS benchmarks. Specifically on Waymo, at a 6-m lateral shift, ReconDreamer# improves NTA-IoU and NTL-IoU by 8.8% and 3.0%, respectively, and reduces FID by 15.1% relative to ReconDreamer++.
  </details>

- **[CHROMA-MILL: Joint Low-Light Enhancement and White Balance under Colored Illumination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04092)**  `arXiv:2611.04092`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pol Riubrogent-Comas, David Serrano-Lozano, Maria Pilligua, Michael S. Brown, Ramon Baldrich, Javier Vazquez-Corral_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Capturing an image in a low-light environment produces a weak, noisy sensor signal. Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) aims to restore it.
