These results measure retrospective agreement with recorded diagnoses; agreement between metric rankings and physician judgments remains to be validated. CardioVascBench supports research on diagnosis prioritization and knowledge-enhanced generation.
  </details>

- **[MIMIC-Note-Extract: A Large-Scale Structured Dataset Extracted from MIMIC-IV-Note Discharge Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04685)**  `arXiv:2611.04685`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kristian Schwethelm, Jonas Kuntzer, Conrad Ketzer, Eva Wende, Moritz Schlager, Daniel Rueckert, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  MIMIC is one of the most widely used public hospital datasets for machine learning on electronic health records, yet clinically important information, such as past medical history, allergies, and diagnostic findings, remains confined to free-text discharge summaries. Studies requiring this information must therefore build their own extraction pipelines, duplicating substantial effort across projects. We release MIMIC-Note-Extract (MNE), a large-scale dataset that uses a hybrid extraction pipeline combining deterministic parsing with schema-guided large language model generation to transform 16 sections from 300,720 discharge summaries into structured tables. MNE adds information that has no structured MIMIC counterpart and enriches related tables with broader coverage and greater clinical detail, including 10 times more patient-history entries and medication histories for more than twice as many admissions. Validation by medical doctors confirms extraction accuracy above 92% across sections. Using MNE, we construct a clinical decision-making benchmark for LLM agents, demonstrating how reusable structured representations of clinical narratives reduce data curation effort.
  </details>

- **[Typological Feature Induction as a Benchmark for Inductive Metalinguistic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04421)**  `arXiv:2611.04421`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chen Zhang, Seth Aycock, Yusuke Miyao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing metalinguistic reasoning benchmarks require both inference of rules and their application, making failures hard to diagnose. We introduce typological feature induction (TFI) which targets the former step. Given sentences in an unnamed language paired with translations in a known pivot language, models must predict a typological feature of the unnamed language. From Grambank and multi-parallel corpora, we automatically build evaluation questions over 195 languages. We find that LLMs identify 59--80% of these languages from only 16 sentence pairs; and misaligning the translations barely affects their accuracy, suggesting reliance on prior knowledge of the language. Enciphering text to prevent recognition increases the corpus' utility, but mainly for features where target and pivot languages agree. On languages which models cannot identify, neither open model outperforms a majority-value baseline. Since induction from raw text aligns with field linguists' use-case, this work indicates the models tested are not yet ready for this role. TFI offers a scalable path to track progress in this direction.
  </details>

- **[Do Multi-Turn Conversational Capabilities Carry Over from Text to Speech? A Parallel Multilingual Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04390)**  `arXiv:2611.04390`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaming Zhou, Pei Zhang, Shiwan Zhao, Zhijun Wang, Baosong Yang, Yong Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Successful multi-turn voice interaction requires models to retain information from earlier turns, follow persistent instructions, and track revisions to conversational state. These abilities are usually evaluated from transcripts, leaving open whether successes demonstrated from text carry over when the same turns are spoken, and whether this transfer is consistent across languages. We introduce Omnilingua-AudioMC, a strictly parallel multilingual extension of Audio MultiChallenge, to study this question. The benchmark contains 354 conversations and 1,404 rubric items across ten languages, with content, turn structure, cross-turn dependencies, and evaluation criteria aligned across transcript and speech conditions. Across six speech-language models, the shift from reference transcripts to direct speech input is strongly model- and language-dependent: models with similar transcript performance can diverge substantially under speech, and their relative strengths across languages can change. Paired rubric-level analysis shows that aggregate transcript-speech gaps can obscure which conversational requirements are preserved, because losses of transcript-demonstrated successes can be offset by gains on other items. Replacing preceding user turns, the current turn, or both with model-generated transcripts localizes recoverable degradation: for the two models with the largest gaps, history substitution yields a larger macro-level improvement than current-turn substitution in all ten languages, while the strongest direct-speech model gains little from either. These results show that transcript performance is an unreliable proxy for multilingual spoken conversational ability and identify the representation of preceding spoken context as an important source of recoverable degradation. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[COMPREHEND: Ultra-Fine-Grained Benchmark Understanding by Entailment Hierarchy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04249)**  `arXiv:2611.04249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sivaraaman Balakrishnan, Prakriti Shetty, Jeremiah Hong, Hamed Zamani, Haw-Shiuan Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding what kind of prompts users typically ask and how well an LLM performs on these prompt categories is crucial for LLM practitioners. The existing methods satisfy the demand by hierarchically clustering the user prompts and synthesizing the cluster names using an LLM to build a taxonomy. However, the cluster names from these methods are usually too general for humans to understand the detailed prompt distributions and identify the exact strengths and weaknesses of LLMs. In this study, we propose COMPREHEND, which automatically constructs an ultra-fine-grained entailment hierarchy for the prompts. The entailment hierarchy replaces the cluster names in the taxonomy with sentences to provide more precise cluster descriptions for more fine-grained categorization. Compared with EvalTree, a state-of-the-art taxonomy construction method, COMPREHEND improves an entailment/faithfulness metric from 22.6 to 41.7 and an informative metric from 16.3 to 39.1 in WildBench. Our experiments also show that the weakness report generated based on our entailment hierarchy is much more informative than EvalTree in terms of predicting the response scores in the benchmark. All the code is released inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Shared Context Candidate Scoring with Jointly Adapted Readout Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04218)**  `arXiv:2611.04218`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jingyang Du, Yiben Fu, Yang Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated decisions over a shared document create two distinct opportunities: reusing document computation and adapting how question representations score candidate answers. We present Q64, a candidate-scoring system that preserves a frozen 64-layer Qwen3.8-27B text backbone, reuses its hybrid prefix state and intermediate document representations, and jointly trains a residual adapter with a four-layer decoder and candidate-conditioned readout. We used ZiYor, a self-evolving AI system, to explore and refine the Q64 design. We specified the scoring method, implemented the system, and evaluated its accuracy and latency. The final adaptation stage updates 88.66 million parameters using 8,192 selected training views. On a fixed 5,112-item evaluation combining ARC-Challenge, ANLI-R3, and stratified subsets of MMLU-Pro and CMMLU, joint adaptation improves four-benchmark macro accuracy from 66.46% to 74.32%. It remains below the native 27B baseline at 75.68% and AnyJev Qwen3-32B L0 at 76.68%. On a separate synthetic workload with 4,096 document tokens and 50 questions, median cold-request latency is 2.237 seconds, compared with 5.383 seconds for the native baseline and 56.872 seconds for the measured batched AnyJev configuration. Short single-question requests do not share this advantage. The evidence supports an accuracy-latency tradeoff in the evaluated systems, rather than a backbone reduction or an isolated component advantage. Public benchmarks informed earlier development, and real-document multi-question validation remains open.
  </details>

- **[PACT: Certifying What Is Written in Parallel Decoding of Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04152)**  `arXiv:2611.04152`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanchao Li, Yusu Zhang, Jiaqing Xie, Yi Ban, Ben Gao, Zhongyu Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel decoding speeds up masked diffusion language models by writing several tokens per forward pass. Published commit rules choose these tokens by testing the predicted distributions. Under sampling the written tokens are drawn, and these tests do not see them. We find that answer quality follows the reliability of the tokens actually written. An answer's log probability splits into this reliability and the dependence within each step. We propose PACT, a training-free commit rule that draws the tokens first, certifies the set it will write and spaces the positions of each step apart. This certificate holds at every temperature, and we show that no rule with the same guarantee commits more positions. Experiments use four models of the LLaDA and Dream families and HumanEval, MBPP, GSM8K, MATH500 and IFEval benchmarks. PACT reaches the quality of published commit rules with fewer forward passes. Spacing removes positions from each step, yet it usually lowers the total cost because later steps then write more tokens. At the same speed, published decoders also write a word twice in a row far more often than PACT. These results suggest that commit rules should certify the tokens they write. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AdSteer: Native Advertising in Large Language Model Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04147)**  `arXiv:2611.04147`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuhang Lin, Chuhao Zhou, Zhenyu Wang, Xiao Lin, Changsheng Wang, Saibo Geng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) services face rapidly rising serving costs. To sustain free or affordable access for a broad user base, alternative monetization mechanisms are needed. Native advertising offers a promising approach, as it creates economic value by aligning user intent with merchant offerings. Yet how to integrate sponsored content naturally into LLM responses remains largely underexplored. Existing approaches primarily rely on prompt-level injection, multi-pass rewriting, or fine-tuning, which can compromise response quality or introduce additional computational costs. We propose AdSteer, a lightweight decoding-time advertising framework that adaptively adjusts token probabilities to integrate contextually appropriate advertisement into responses. AdSteer calibrates the strength and duration of advertising influence, making sponsored content a natural part of the response. Experiments on GEM-Bench across three application scenarios show that AdSteer achieves an average improvement of 14.1% in overall score over the strongest baselines and 22.5% fewer total tokens on average than rewrite-based methods. These results demonstrate that AdSteer provides an effective and practical solution for native advertising in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Dory1.0-4B: A Looped Normalized-GPT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03998)**  `arXiv:2611.03998`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maxim Fishman, Simeng Sun, Shantanu Acharya, Faisal Ladhak, Brian Chmiel, Santiago Akle Serano, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Dory1.0-4B, a 4-billion-parameter looped (latent-recurrent) language model with elastic depth: a single checkpoint serves different inference compute budgets. Dory1.0-4B is built on a normalized Transformer (nGPT) backbone with interleaved sliding-window and full-attention layers, organized into an input block, a shared recurrent block, and an output block. At inference, the recurrent block can be applied a variable number of times. We pre-trained Dory1.0-4B on 6 trillion tokens. The first 4.5 trillion tokens were trained in NVFP4, and additional pre-training then extended the context length to 256K tokens. Next, we post-trained the model using Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), with a difficulty-aware loop curriculum that routes harder prompts to deeper loops. Increasing the loop count improves accuracy, with the largest gains on coding, agentic, and long-context benchmarks. With two or more loops, Dory1.0-4B outperforms larger models such as Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma 4 12B, and Gemma 4 26B-A4B on average across 12 benchmarks. Looping also makes Dory1.0-4B more token-efficient on agentic benchmarks: with four loops, it generates 1.3-2.5x fewer tokens than with one loop, as the extra computation is spent in latent space rather than on longer outputs. To reduce memory during looping, Dory1.0-4B caches keys and values only from the last loop, keeping its KV-cache footprint equal to that of a non-looped model at any loop count while retaining most of the accuracy gains. We open-source the BF16, MXFP8, NVFP4, and GGUF quantized checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[EvoGroup: A Self-Evolving Harness for Group Agents in Shared Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03868)**  `arXiv:2611.03868`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyi Xu, Xiaoyang Li, Yucheng Feng, Wenqing Chen, Youliang Yuan, Wei Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rise of Group Agent such as Claude Tag, agents increasingly serve as digital workers for long-term team collaboration. These agents operate over shared contexts that continuously accumulate through multi-user interactions. However, existing agent memory systems are mainly designed for individual users and struggle to capture group-level knowledge in shared contexts, including group relationships and collaboratively formed knowledge. Manual memory strategies further limit adaptation to diverse and evolving shared contexts. To this end, we propose \textbf{EvoGroup (EvoG)}, a self-evolving harness for group agents that starts from a minimal six-tool cold start and adapts through interaction with shared context. EvoG uses confidence and task outcomes to select informative trajectories, while also eliciting self-reflections on uncertainty in low-confidence cases. The analysis agent then examines selected trajectories through adaptive progressive disclosure, followed by bucketed analysis to synthesize high-level findings that guide the evolve agent in revising the Harness. Empirically, pass@1 on held-out split of EverMemBench improves from 71.07\% to 89.58\%, surpassing both self-evolving memory and human-designed harnesses, including Claude Code (85.83\%). To evaluate generalization, we freeze the evolved harness and evaluate it on the unseen GroupMemBench, where, with GPT-5.5, it demonstrates effective generalization and outperforms both memory baselines and human-designed harnesses.
  </details>

- **[NitiBench+: A Temporal Thai Legal Question Answering Benchmark Across 17 Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03576)**  `arXiv:2611.03576`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pawitsapak Akarajaradwong, Sarana Nutanong, Chompakorn Chaksangchaichot_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Law changes over time. A court decides a case under the law in force on that day, and legal QA benchmarks grade answers against today's law. We checked 156,650 decisions of four Thai courts. Up to 67% of them cite law that has since been amended or repealed. We introduce NitiBench+, the sequel of our NitiBench, a Thai legal QA benchmark with 1,670 items across 17 domains, built from decisions of three courts. For each cited section it holds both the text in force on the decision date and the current text, and its retrieval corpus holds every dated version of every Thai statute section. Answers are graded on the Thai Bar Council rubric, the Bar-standard score, and on the overlap between the cited sections and the court's. The report has two parts. Part I measures the cost of the wrong text on 13 open-weight models. Reading the current text of amended law drops the Bar-standard score by 0.25 and missing repealed law drops it by 0.79. The penalty comes from rule changes, 0.45 on the 428 rule-changed items and none on the 331 wording-only items, and Citation F1 does not see it. The text in force is the fair setting. Part II benchmarks today's models on that setting. A public leaderboard ranks 26 models on a held-out test split of 500 items stratified by domain, with a validation split of the same shape released for public use, from memory alone, with the court's sections provided, and as a retrieval agent over the law in force, with the cost per item and the hallucinated citations. The best model scores 7.78 out of 10 and the best open-weight model 6.39. Without a search tool only five open-weight models recall any Thai law, and with it twenty of the 26 models gain and six lose. Leaderboard will take new models as they are released. Legal benchmarks must track dates, and on the right text today's models still leave a wide gap on Thai law.
  </details>

- **[PGuard: A Native, Streaming, and Plug-in Guardrail for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02791)**  `arXiv:2611.02791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junyu Lin, Mengjie Wu, Yonghao Dong, Yuchuan Fu, Jinfeng Li, Xiaodan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As streaming generation becomes the dominant serving paradigm for both text-only and multimodal large language models, ensuring safety throughout the generation process has become a critical challenge. We introduce PGuard, a native, streaming, and plug-in guardrail that transforms safety monitoring from an external serving dependency into an integrated capability of the backbone model. PGuard directly leverages the backbone's intermediate representations for token-level safety detection, without altering its original inference pathway or generation behavior. Specifically, dynamic layer gating selectively aggregates residual-stream representations across layers to extract safety-relevant signals during both prefill and autoregressive decoding. To balance detection accuracy and computational efficiency, PGuard supports multiple representation-modeling architectures, including a lightweight stateless variant and sequence-aware variants built on multi-scale causal EMA-attention and GRU-based recurrent modules. Its design also accounts for the execution characteristics of LLM serving frameworks, such as SGLang, optimizing computation and data access to minimize additional runtime and synchronization overhead. As a result, PGuard incurs only 0.82-2.26% end-to-end overhead on the hundred-billion-parameter backbone Qwen3.5-122B-A10B served by SGLang. Extensive evaluations across more than 30 benchmarks spanning text and multimodal safety, jailbreaks, cyber and CBRN risks, multilingual scenarios, and real-world traffic demonstrate state-of-the-art performance among native guardrails. On text queries with Qwen3.5-9B, PGuard achieves an average F1 score of 0.836, approaching dedicated external guardrails, while supporting deployment-time configuration of category-specific thresholds and enforcement actions.
  </details>

- **[GigaSpeech 3: Scaling Multi-Dialect Arabic Speech Recognition with Weakly Labeled Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02476)**  `arXiv:2611.02476`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junzhe Liu, Yujie Tu, Yifan Yang, Peng Wang, Haoran Wang, Guodong Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dialectal Arabic speech recognition remains constrained by the scarcity of dialect-resolved transcribed speech despite the abundance of web audio. We introduce GigaSpeech 3, a corpus of 39,000 hours and 10 million weakly transcribed speech segments covering Algerian, Egyptian, Iraqi, Saudi, and Syrian Arabic. Starting from 60 hours of manual transcription per dialect, we train labeling models using speech-recognition-biased self-supervised learning and apply them to dialect-matched web audio. Data selection and four rounds of noisy-student refinement for Saudi and Syrian Arabic yield the final corpus. We validate its utility through dialect-specific and multi-dialect speech recognition training and cross-domain evaluation. On GigaSpeechBench, the final dialect-specific models outperform their labeling models across all five dialects and achieve a macro-average word error rate of 23.86%, a 16.6% relative reduction from the strongest third-party system covering all five dialects, while outperforming the best third-party result on three dialects. A multi-dialect model using self-predicted dialect identities achieves a macro-average word error rate of 25.51% while also supporting Modern Standard Arabic. Across eight conditions from three external corpora, a GigaSpeech 3-trained Zipformer achieves the lowest word error rate among six evaluated models in every condition, while adapting the Qwen3 speech recognizer on GigaSpeech 3 improves performance across all eight conditions. We release the corpus and recipes as a foundation for dialect-aware Arabic speech processing.
  </details>

- **[Outdated numeric values from Norwegian statutes and regulations in a further-trained model and its base model: a preregistered paired comparison of Borealis 2 and Gemma 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02334)**  `arXiv:2611.02334`  `cs.CL`  
  _Eirik Botten Nicolaysen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We compare how often two released language models give outdated numeric values from Norwegian statutes and regulations: NbAiLab/borealis2-26b-a4b-preview (Borealis 2), which the National Library of Norway further trained from google/gemma-4-26B-A4B-it (Gemma 4), and Gemma 4 itself. The study was preregistered (doi:https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23858965) and uses the closed norregel benchmark, scored deterministically against a value history. On the 411 of 503 primary items where both models gave an interpretable answer, Borealis 2 alone was outdated on 10 items and Gemma 4 alone on 11 (d = -0.2 percentage points, 95% cluster bootstrap interval [-2.7, +2.5], exact one-sided McNemar p = 0.668). By the preregistered rule this is no effect of interest: the whole interval lies within the smallest effect of interest of +/-10 percentage points. Among the secondary measures, only the share of answers giving a value that took effect after the question date differs after Holm correction; Gemma 4 gave such answers more often. An exploratory comparison of two smaller Borealis models had no discordant pairs and is not informative. The design compares released models and does not isolate the effect of any part of their training.
  </details>

- **[Omnilingual-MsASR: Benchmarking Multimodal, Multilingual, Long-Form Multi-Speaker ASR of Omni Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29455)**  `arXiv:2610.29455`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuan Ge, Pei Zhang, Xize Cheng, Lingzhou Mu, Mingyang Wang, Jin Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in audio and video understanding. However, existing models struggle with long-form, multi-speaker videos, especially in understanding the entire video and recognizing who says what and when. To characterize the capability limit of current omni LLMs and guide future model development, we introduce Omnilingual-MsASR, a multilingual, multimodal benchmark for long-form Multi-speaker Automatic Speech Recognition (MsASR) covering six languages, recordings up to 30 minutes, and scenarios with up to 20 speakers. Omnilingual-MsASR provides parallel audio and video inputs to evaluate models' cross-modal consistency and their ability to exploit complementary cues across modalities. Extensive evaluations of leading omni-modal LLMs reveal substantial performance bottlenecks, while their tcpWERs frequently exceed 100%. These findings highlight fine-grained temporal localization, speaker disambiguation, and cross-modal synergy as key challenges for future omni-modal foundation models. Our work is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TRILOGUE: A Trilingual Spoken Dialogue Fact-Checking Benchmark with Evidence and Paired Audio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04452)**  `arXiv:2609.04452`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chaewan Chun, Meruyert Aristombayeva, Jiyoung Choi, Mahjabin Nahar, Delvin Ce Zhang, Dongwon Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern misinformation is often heard before it is read, yet fact-checking systems are still evaluated mainly on clean written claims. Spoken dialogue remains different even when systems operate on transcripts: claims may be distributed across speakers and turns, depend on prior context, and become harder to verify when Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) errors distort the available text. Prior spoken dialogue fact-checking resources are small, English-centric, or focused on annotation rather than end-to-end benchmarking, leaving no large multilingual benchmark with paired speech and turn-level labels. We introduce TRILOGUE (TRIlingual spoken diaLOGUE fact-checking), a large-scale trilingual benchmark of source-grounded spoken dialogues in English, Russian, and Kazakh. It contains nearly 12K dialogues, 187K turns, and 390 hours of paired audio with ASR transcripts and word-level timestamp alignments across all three languages, including nearly 5K human-recorded Russian and Kazakh dialogue files. TRILOGUE supports claim check-worthiness detection, source-article evidence retrieval, and claim verification with claim-only, gold-evidence, and retrieved-evidence inputs. Baselines show that ASR degradation and cross-lingual transfer remain challenging, especially for Kazakh, while retrieved source evidence substantially narrows the gap to gold-evidence verification.
  </details>

- **[Lost in Speech: Trilingual Spoken Hallucination Detection Across Audio and Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24707)**  `arXiv:2608.24707`  `cs.CL`  
  _Meruyert Aristombayeva, Jason S. Lucas, Chaewan Chun, Dongwon Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While text-based hallucination detection is well studied, reference-free detection of factual alterations in speech remains underexplored, especially for low-resource languages. Our spoken benchmark comprises 12,013 English, Russian, and Kazakh news samples with three synthetic alteration types and three severity levels, pairing source articles with rewrites as text, synthesized audio, and ASR transcripts. We add 290 fact-checked misinformation items collected in Russian (225) and Kazakh (65), translated into the other language and rendered through the same TTS-ASR pipeline. We evaluate fine-tuned multilingual encoders and zero-shot multimodal decoders on text, transcripts, and audio. Detectors receive only target inputs without source articles or external evidence; the task evaluates reference-free classification rather than evidence-grounded verification. Encoder degradation from source text to transcripts generally tracks per-language ASR error on the binary task. Among decoders, only Gemma-3n exceeds the binary majority-class baseline in macro-F1, on transcripts only; the other four fall below their respective baselines. Comparisons between audio and transcripts are confounded by differences in evaluation coverage and class balance. Synthetic-trained detectors achieve 0.82--0.88 macro-F1 on real-world misinformation source text; Russian provenance analysis reveals veracity-related and model-dependent machine-style signals, a key confound in synthetic hallucination benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[HTAM: Hierarchical Transition-Attended Memory for Operator Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29734)**  `arXiv:2605.29734`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yining Zhang, Mingyang Yi, Chen Wang, Xuwen Xiang, Tianhe Jia, Zedong Dan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-performance GPU kernels are essential for efficient LLM deployment, yet optimizing them remains expertise-intensive. Recent LLM-based code generation makes automatic GPU operator generation promising, but operator optimization remains a hardware-aware search problem. Existing LLM-based methods face a granularity mismatch: coarse hints are reusable but hard to execute, whereas detailed memories are actionable but enlarge the search space and obscure optimization bottlenecks. The key challenge is therefore to organize optimization experience at an appropriate granularity. To address this issue, this paper proposes HTAM (Hierarchical Transition-Attended Memory), a coarse-to-fine framework for LLM-based operator optimization. HTAM builds a two-level Hierarchical Transition Graph (HTG) to organize coarse global directions, detailed local strategies, and transition experience between optimization steps. During each evolution step, HTAM selects a global direction from the current state and recent optimization history, retrieves the corresponding local strategy memory, and uses it to guide concrete CUDA code generation. Experiments on KernelBench demonstrate that HTAM improves correctness, fast-solution rate, and speedup over LLM-based baselines, while backend and Robust-KBench studies indicate transferable benefits from structured memory.
  </details>

- **[SEAL: Synergistic Co-Evolution of Agents and Learning Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24426)**  `arXiv:2605.24426`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yihao Hu, Zhihao Wen, Pan Wang, Xiujin Liu, Yitong Lin, Xin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly learn through interaction, yet their training process often evolves unevenly: the policy changes with experience while the learning environment continues to provide largely fixed forms of support. As training progresses, this creates a growing mismatch between what the agent currently struggles with and what its learning process emphasizes. We call this phenomenon Agent-Learning Environment Misalignment (ALEM). We introduce SEAL, a framework for synergistic co-evolution of agents and learning environments. SEAL turns verifier-grounded execution failures into a shared diagnostic signal that coordinates two complementary forms of adaptation: it reshapes the support available in subsequent interactions and adjusts how the policy learns from collected trajectories. In this way, the learning environment follows the agent's evolving failure profile while both sides remain grounded in the same executable feedback. Across three backbones on BFCL V3, SEAL consistently improves over matched Vanilla RL, reaching 40.25% versus 30.75% with Qwen2.5-7B. A matched factorial ablation further yields a +5.00-point interaction estimate for the coupled configuration. The same trained checkpoints also show positive transfer to the evaluated BFCL V4 and $\tau^2$-bench domains. These results suggest that coordinating how an agent learns with the environment it learns from is a promising direction for self-evolving tool-use agents.
  </details>

- **[Not All Proofs Are Equal: Evaluating LLM Proof Quality Beyond Correctness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10379)**  `arXiv:2605.10379`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ivo Petrov, Jasper Dekoninck, Dimitar I. Dimitrov, Martin Vechev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have become capable mathematical problem-solvers, often producing correct proofs for challenging problems. However, correctness alone is not sufficient: mathematical proofs should also be clear, insightful, contain transferable knowledge, and be generally useful to the reader. While proof quality is subjective and depends on the reader and context, many of its components can be made concrete. In this work, we identify such components and introduce ProofRank, a benchmark curated from challenging competitions that measures several scalable proxies of proof quality: (i) conciseness - quantifying whether proofs avoid unnecessary steps; (ii) computational ease - the extent to which a proof relies on tedious calculations; (iii) cognitive simplicity - how accessible the used proof techniques are; (iv) diversity - how varied a model's proofs for a single problem are; and (v) adaptivity - how well a model can follow a specified technique. We find proof quality varies substantially across models along dimensions that correctness largely fails to capture. Every proof-level proxy can be steered through prompting, but often at a moderate correctness cost. ProofRank thus captures important aspects of mathematical proofs beyond correctness, and indicates the need for models that produce proofs which are not only correct but also valuable.
  </details>

- **[EchoKV: Efficient KV Cache Compression via Similarity-Based Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22910)**  `arXiv:2603.22910`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shiyu Ji, Yixuan Wang, Yijun Liu, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The increasing memory demand of the Key-Value (KV) cache poses a significant bottleneck for Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-context applications. Existing low-rank KV compression methods reduce this footprint by modifying model projections, limiting the flexibility to switch back to standard full-cache inference when sufficient memory is available. In this paper, we propose EchoKV, a flexible KV cache compression framework that supports on-demand transitions from full KV caching to compressed caching. Unlike traditional compression-decompression paradigms, EchoKV utilizes a lightweight network to reconstruct the discarded KV components from a partial subset, exploiting intrinsic inter-layer and intra-layer similarities among attention heads. We further introduce a lightweight two-stage fine-tuning strategy, requiring only a few minutes on a single A100 GPU for Llama-3.1-8B. Experimental results on LongBench and RULER demonstrate that EchoKV offers a competitive quality-cache trade-off across multiple compression ratios and backbone models while preserving the throughput of full-cache inference in short-context scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Multicultural Spyfall: Assessing LLMs through a Dynamic Multilingual Social Deduction Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09017)**  `arXiv:2601.09017`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haryo Akbarianto Wibowo, Alaa Elsetohy, Qinrong Cui, Alham Fikri Aji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has necessitated more robust evaluation methods that go beyond static benchmarks, which are increasingly prone to data saturation and leakage. In this paper, we propose a dynamic benchmarking framework for evaluating multilingual and multicultural capabilities through the social deduction game Spyfall. In our setup, models must engage in strategic dialogue to either identify a secret agent or avoid detection, utilizing culturally relevant locations or local foods. Our results show that our game-based rankings are consistent with Chatbot Arena rankings on the overlapping models. However, we find a significant performance gap in non-English contexts: models are generally less proficient when handling locally specific entities and often struggle with rule-following or strategic integrity in non-English languages. We demonstrate that this game-based approach provides a scalable, leakage-resistant, and culturally nuanced alternative to traditional NLP benchmarks. The dataset (game history) and source code can be accessed here:this https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mindscape-Aware Retrieval Augmented Generation for Improved Long Context Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.17220)**  `arXiv:2512.17220`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuqing Li, Jiangnan Li, Zheng Lin, Ziyan Zhou, Junjie Wu, Jie Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans understand long and complex texts by relying on a holistic semantic representation of the content. This global view helps organize prior knowledge, interpret new information, and integrate evidence dispersed across a document, as revealed by the Mindscape-Aware Capability of humans in psychology. Current Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems lack such guidance and therefore struggle with long-context tasks. In this paper, we propose Mindscape-Aware RAG (MiA-RAG), the first framework to formulate mindscape-aware retrieval and generation as a unified conditioning paradigm for LLM-based RAG. MiA-RAG builds a mindscape through hierarchical summarization and conditions both retrieval and generation on this global semantic representation. This enables the retriever to form enriched query embeddings and the generator to reason over retrieved evidence within a coherent global context. We evaluate MiA-RAG across diverse long-context and bilingual benchmarks for evidence-based understanding and global sense-making. It consistently surpasses baselines, and further analysis shows that it aligns local details with a coherent global representation, enabling more human-like long-context retrieval and reasoning.
  </details>

- **[PersonaFuse: A Situation-Aware Post-Training Framework for Adaptive LLM Behavior in Enterprise Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07370)**  `arXiv:2509.07370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Tang, Yi Yang, Ahmed Abbasi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise settings to support task execution and workflow automation. Despite the strong performance of general-purpose models on standard benchmarks, organizations often need to adapt them to domain-specific tasks and operational constraints in practice. However, existing post-training approaches introduce capability trade-offs and lack mechanisms for situational adaptation. In this work, we propose PersonaFuse, a post-training framework that enables situation-aware behavioral adaptation in LLMs. Grounded in Trait Activation Theory and operationalized through the Five-Factor Model, PersonaFuse dynamically adjusts model behavior based on task context using a mixture-of-experts architecture with trait-level experts and a context-aware routing mechanism. We evaluate PersonaFuse at three levels. At the capability level, it achieves balanced improvements across general reasoning, interaction quality, and safety without introducing trade-offs. At the workflow level, it improves task completion in enterprise scenarios involving tool use and structured execution across both B2B and B2C tasks. At the deployment level, we analyze model selection under cost and safety constraints using a capability-cost frontier framework, showing that models adapted with PersonaFuse achieve more favorable trade-offs and are consistently selected as optimal across deployment scenarios. PersonaFuse provides a practical framework for developing LLMs that are not only capable, but also adaptable and deployable in real-world enterprise environments.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Isolated Dots: Benchmarking Structured Table Construction as Deep Knowledge Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16271)**  `arXiv:2507.16271`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianyun Zhong, Guozhao Mo, Yanjiang Liu, Yihan Chen, Lingdi Kong, Xuanang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly expected to extract and synthesize structured information from complex real-world documents, yet they typically produce unstructured, hard-to-verify paragraph-style outputs. We introduce AOE (Arranged and Organized Extraction), a bilingual benchmark that evaluates LLMs' ability to distill long-form, multi-document inputs (64k to 1.8M tokens) into structured tables. AOE comprises 12 tasks across four domains (academic, financial, legal, and medical), each grounded in genuine analytical needs, with schema guidance provided to isolate extraction quality from formatting variance. We evaluate 12 frontier LLMs (both open-weight and proprietary) under four generation settings of increasing autonomy: direct full-document input, RAG-based retrieval, offline document-browsing agent, and online web-search agent. Even the best-performing model achieves only 42.7% Cell F1, and retrieval-augmented approaches often degrade rather than improve performance, revealing that structured extraction from long contexts remains a fundamental challenge - an "Area of Effect" (AOE) that impacts all tested systems.
  </details>

- **[Are LLM Assistants Regulatory Ready? Answers from regenold's 2026 EU AI Act Q&A Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04546)**  `arXiv:2611.04546`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Marco Virgolin, Thomas Haszprunar, Maximilian Regenold_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) assistants are increasingly proposed for regulatory affairs, yet their reliability in regulatory tasks remains unclear. We introduce the EU AI Act Q&A Benchmark, an expert-curated benchmark of 110 open-ended questions about the EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689). Each question comes with an exemplary answer, question-specific correctness criteria, and expected references. Evaluation measures answer correctness, reference quality, conciseness, tone, and latency, with a multi-turn ``hard mode'' testing responses to simulated user pushback. We also present the results of the 2026 EU AI Act Q&A Challenge, based on the benchmark, where three off-the-shelf baselines and five purpose-built assistants (from four commercial developers and one academic entrant) were tested. Only two of the five submitted assistants consistently outperformed the baselines; one achieved high scores across all evaluated metrics. These results show that specialist development alone does not guarantee better performance and highlight the critical importance of LLM assistant evaluation in regulatory affairs. The benchmark code is openly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CONFIRM: Governing Scientific Reporting for Trustworthy Agentic Neuroimaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01449)**  `arXiv:2611.01449`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yuxiang Wei, Lijing Zhu, Qizhen Lan, Yanteng Zhang, Vince D. Calhoun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can autonomously run neuroimaging analyses from research questions to reported findings. Existing agentic systems use tools to check that code runs and reported values match its output, but rely on LLM judgment to decide which results warrant scientific reporting. We show that LLM judges endorse site-confounded findings even when the confound is presented as evidence. We present \textbf{CONFIRM}, a framework that separates running an analysis from deciding which findings to report. CONFIRM encodes each hypothesis as a machine-readable, pre-specified analysis plan that fixes the claim and its pass criteria before computing results. It then filters results through five deterministic gates (search history and multiplicity, confounds, power, analytic stability, and replication) under three configurable reporting standards, from least to most demanding: \textit{Screen}, \textit{Replicate}, and \textit{Full}. Failed claims receive diagnoses that guide follow-up analyses. To evaluate reporting decisions at the claim level, we introduce \textbf{NeuroClaimBench}, comprising 489 executable neuroimaging claims with frozen data, including literature-supported findings and 150 controls designed to violate individual methodological requirements. On NeuroClaimBench, CONFIRM falsely supports at most 3\% of controls, compared with 23--38\% for the baselines, while recovering up to 61\% of literature-supported claims. When auditing an existing agent, CONFIRM reduces false support on controls from 23\% to 0\% while retaining the agent's literature-supported claims. Its diagnostic feedback also produces more supported revisions than Self-Refine with fewer LLM calls. Together, CONFIRM and NeuroClaimBench show that basing reporting decisions on pre-specified analysis plans and deterministic gates is essential for trustworthy autonomous scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Unsupervised Jamming Detection in 5G Open RAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04369)**  `arXiv:2611.04369`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sneihil Gopal, Fernando J. Cintron, Richard A. Rouil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radio Frequency (RF) jamming threatens fifth-generation (5G) networks. Open Radio Access Network (Open RAN) telemetry enables jamming detection without dedicated spectrum monitoring equipment, but labeled attack data are rarely available across deployments, motivating unsupervised detectors. Comparing such detectors across algorithmic families, however, remains difficult without ground-truth labels. This paper benchmarks statistical and reconstruction-based unsupervised jamming detectors using a symmetric evaluation interface that combines detection confidence and temporal interval coherence into a detector-independent composite score. We compare a Statistical Ensemble Method (SEM) detector against Vanilla, Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), and Variational Autoencoder (VAE) baselines on Key Performance Indicator (KPI) telemetry from ten heterogeneous 5G environments with fixed and variable jamming bursts and dynamic jammer gains. Under leave-one-out (LOO) evaluation, SEM achieves a mean composite score of 0.906, compared with 0.688 for the best autoencoder (AE) per fold. Ground-truth validation yields a mean F1-score of 0.918 for SEM, compared with 0.521 for the best AE variant. The composite ranks SEM first in all environments, consistent with F1 rankings. However, in both within-trace and cross-trace evaluation, it fails for detectors that flag far fewer samples than the jamming prevalence, indicating that rankings are reliable only when detection rate matches the expected prevalence.
  </details>

- **[HybridSpec: Throughput-Aware Speculative Decoding for Hybrid GDN-MoE Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04055)**  `arXiv:2611.04055`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haiyu Wang, Sai Qian Zhang, Zechun Liu, Steven Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In speculative decoding, parallel drafters such as DFlash make drafting cheap, so target verification dominates each round. A central choice is the verification length, the number of tokens that the target processes in each round. Verifying more draft tokens can accept more of them, but work after the first rejection is wasted. For hybrid target models that combine Gated DeltaNet (GDN) and mixture-of-experts (MoE) layers, verification time grows much faster with this length than for dense models. In a single-request profile of Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with DFlash, it grew by 71% from length 4 to 16, almost entirely in GDN and MoE layers, compared with 4%-6% for a dense Qwen3-4B target. Each GDN layer must update a recurrent state at every token and recover it at the end of the accepted prefix. The MoE cost depends on the experts that the tokens activate, which are known only once verification runs. We present HybridSpec, which selects the verification length on the GPU from draft-side acceptance estimates and measured target latency. Verification then runs this length plus one extra token up to the first MoE router, before any expert runs. There, a lightweight routing correction uses the observed expert assignments to decide whether to keep that token. The rest of verification runs only on the selected tokens. Block-parallel GDN kernels avoid storing a state after every token and reconstruct the accepted state of every GDN layer in one launch. In isolated benchmarks at verification lengths 4 to 16 with every draft token accepted, these kernels, including accepted-state reconstruction, are $1.54\times$-$4.04\times$ faster than the default kernels. With three GDN-MoE target models in single-request greedy decoding, HybridSpec is $1.75\times$-$1.88\times$ faster than autoregressive decoding and $1.30\times$-$1.33\times$ faster than DFlash.
  </details>

- **[Unsatz: Evaluating Scientific Discovery Without a Given Ansatz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03713)**  `arXiv:2611.03713`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ethan Ji, Wei Wang, Yizhou Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Before a scientist fits any parameter, they must decide what the model contains: which quantities exist, which are hidden, how they interact, and how they are measured. Physicists call such a trial form an ansatz. Benchmarks for automated equation discovery mostly supply it, by fixing the variables, a library of candidate terms, or a law to be modified. We introduce Unsatz, a benchmark in which an LLM agent must construct the ansatz itself. Each task is a simulated system, generated from a private seed as a program in a small modeling language: ordinary, delay, or partial differential equations, discrete maps, static and causal models, or stochastic processes, possibly with hidden states, distorted sensors, and uninformative measurement channels. The agent buys experiments within a budget, writes candidate programs in the same language, obtains their coefficients from the benchmark's fitter, and is scored only on how its final program predicts experiments it never saw: conditions outside the range it could explore, interventions, and longer runs. Across 80 systems in 20 domains, nine language models solve between 21% and 60%. On 44 of these systems, classical equation-discovery methods almost never succeed, although the same data usually suffice once the true equations are given with unknown coefficients; the difficulty lies in finding the structure, not in estimating it. Because mechanisms recur across systems, we also give agents the program they discovered for a related system; it helped only when the new system shared the mechanism and the first investigation had succeeded. Stronger agents make fewer unscientific errors; beyond these, agents find which variables a system has but not how they act, and prefer revisions that lower the training error to revisions that predict better.
  </details>

- **[SAT-ACT: Learning CDCL Branching from Outcome-Derived Action Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41290)**  `arXiv:2610.41290`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenchao Sun, Chongyang Tao, Ping Lu, Tongkai Lu, Jianling Gao, Shuai Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning-based approaches have increasingly been explored for SAT solving, with particular attention to improving branching decisions in conflict-driven clause learning (CDCL) solvers. These approaches derive branching guidance either from static structural properties of SAT formulas or from dynamic signals obtained through solver feedback and execution traces. However, these forms of supervision provide limited evidence about state-dependent branching effects, while different outcome measures may favor different branching choices. To address these limitations, we propose SAT-ACT, an outcome-derived learning framework for CDCL branching. We design three complementary outcome comparison criteria to extract action preferences from independent branching action rollouts initiated at the same solver state. The criteria identify preferences supported by the outcomes and leave comparisons unresolved when the evidence is insufficient or conflicting, instead of forcing an ordering using a predefined scalar metric. A state-conditioned network learns branching priorities from the extracted preferences and uses them to guide CDCL search directly without online action rollouts. We further introduce non-dominated heuristic action supervision, which retains the solver-selected action as a training target only when it remains non-dominated under these preferences. Solver-level experiments show that SAT-ACT consistently reduces CDCL search costs on three SAT families and improves PAR-2 on the SAT Competition benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Deposon: An Auditable, Conservation-Guaranteed, Game-Theoretically Tested Scattering Layer over LLM Reasoning Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09001)**  `arXiv:2609.09001`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qihao Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step LLM reasoning lacks a machine-recheckable ledger: discarded reasoning paths leave no auditable record. We propose the Deposon scattering layer, which binds each node of an LLM-generated concept-decomposition graph to a two-parameter Deposon state; paths undergo three-channel scattering -- transmission, reflection, irreversible dissipation -- obeying T+R+A=1 for arbitrary parameters, with a maximum per-path energy-audit deviation of 2.2E-16 (machine epsilon). We report all three evidence tiers honestly. On synthetic trap benchmarks the path-filtering gain is closed (pre-registered): unified reaches 100% versus a decoy-capture baseline at 7%/10%. On real benchmarks the layer is indistinguishable from a trivial six-keyword rule filter (GSM8K 0.87 >= 0.85, McNemar p=0.5; StrategyQA 0.899 = 0.899); no difference is detected here, so we sharpen the claim to "the differential value lies solely in machine verifiability." Fusion yields a second negative result: convex combinations with a semantic prior never improve (physics 0.484 -> 0.452), and the apparent lambda=2 gain is an anti-field artifact; any fusion gain must be nonlinear. Modeling the reverse dynamics as a potential game on the graph, we evidence an auditable scalar's monotonicity and near-gradientness and quantify the empirical coordination ratio (ECR). The three formalized dynamical-equivalence propositions (P1a/P1b/T-P1c) are falsified under the pre-registered kill protocol, and the potential-game claim is downgraded to approximate (cyclic-graph median residual 0.669): only consistency-level evidence survives at the dynamical level. Code:this http URL.
  </details>

- **[JediKG: Return of the Schema](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14795)**  `arXiv:2602.14795`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ivan Diliso, Roberto Barile, Nicola Fanizzi, Claudia d'Amato_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark datasets for Knowledge Graph Refinement typically contain only ground facts, discarding the schema available in the source knowledge graph. This limits the evaluation of Neural-Symbolic methods that leverage expressive schemas. We present JEDIKG the first resource that systematically addresses these gaps through two components: JDEX a general configurable tool for extracting complete, portable, modular dataset from any RDF/OWL knowledge graph; and JDSET, a curated suite of eleven datasets with varying expressivity, volume, and domain. JDEX preserves full schema expressivity, employs standard OWL reasoning to detect and correct inconsistencies and materialize implicit knowledge, applies schema modularization, and provides utilities for integration with mainstream machine learning libraries such as PyTorch and PyKEEN. We validated the completeness and portability of the datasets across several dimensions and preliminarily proved the utility of JEDIKG in experimental workflows combining data-driven methods with schema-driven reasoning.
  </details>

- **[VagueBench: Benchmarking the Robustness of Embodied Agents on Ambient Context Illusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03765)**  `arXiv:2611.03765`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yang Qu, Qiang Fu, Zonghao Ying, Jiayi Zhao, Yaoyuan Zhang, Jinyang Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents are increasingly deployed in open-world environments, yet current robustness evaluations remain biased toward hazardous instructions and jailbreak vulnerabilities. A more fundamental issue is largely overlooked: agents are rarely tested under semantically sufficient but contextually minimal instructions (i.e., common in real-world human communication), where auxiliary contextual cues are absent. For the first, we identify a novel phenomenon termed Ambient Context Illusion, where agents fail to execute tasks correctly when non-essential contextual details are omitted, despite unchanged task semantics. To systematically study this, we establish VagueBench, a benchmark built upon the AI2-THOR interactive simulation environment for the evaluation of context dependency in embodied agents. We systematically consider key semantic components of instructions (entities, procedures, constraints, and prior knowledge) and derive a unified taxonomy with four categories and nine subtypes to reduce auxiliary information. VagueBench contains 1,303 instruction pairs across diverse indoor environments, constructed via a human-agent co-generation framework that integrates semantic redundancy estimation, taxonomy-guided transformation, and rigorous semantic-invariance verification. Through extensive evaluation on 6 state-of-the-art embodied agents, we observe significant degradation on VagueBench in both task performance and executability (relatively -19.65% in task success rate and relatively +54.10% in empty plan rate) revealing a strong reliance on auxiliary linguistic signals rather than core task semantics. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this phenomenon persists in real-world robotic systems, where it can lead to incorrect decisions and potential safety risks.
  </details>

- **[LOLPlay: Benchmarking Real-Time Visual Agents in League of Legends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03685)**  `arXiv:2611.03685`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaojian Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents need to coordinate reasoning and action while their environment evolves during inference. We introduce LOLPlay, a benchmark in native \emph{League of Legends} with 36 champion-specific tasks spanning resource management, combat, cooperation, and objectives. Agents interact through screenshots and mouse--keyboard actions; automated initialization and 82 executable subgoal checks distinguish partial progress from completion. Across 324 task evaluations of three models at three reasoning-effort settings, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate of 25.0\% at low and medium effort, and the highest subgoal success rate of 40.3\% at medium effort. However, high effort improves neither metric over low effort for any model. For Astra, longer requests leave fewer interaction rounds, suggesting a cost to additional reasoning in real-time control. Trajectories reveal incomplete subgoal composition and actions executing after the observed scene changes, motivating agents that combine visual reasoning with timely action.
  </details>

- **[AlgoPilot: Cross-Paradigm Reasoning in Language Models via Strategy Selection and Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04079)**  `arXiv:2611.04079`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yilun Hao, Ruixiao Yang, Hudson Hilal, Yongchao Chen, Kaizhi Qian, Yada Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world problems span diverse domains, including program synthesis, symbolic reasoning, planning, and optimization, and often require fundamentally different solution paradigms. A central challenge for LLM-based reasoning is twofold: identifying an appropriate problem-solving approach and formulating it correctly. In this paper, we propose AlgoPilot, a cross-paradigm reasoning framework that enables adaptive selection of the solving approaches and structured problem formulation prior to execution. The AlgoPilot framework contains three components: 1) a SteerLM, trained via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL), that adaptively selects the solving strategy (paradigm and modes) based on input problems, 2) the corresponding expert GuideLM, trained via SFT, that generates structured formulation guidance tailored to the selected strategy, and 3) an ExecutionLM that generates solutions conditioned on both the selected strategy and its formulation. Unlike prompting-based methods that rely on fixed reasoning templates, our approach learns to adaptively choose and structure problem-solving strategies based on the input. We build AlgoPilot on Qwen3-8B and evaluate across 5 problem categories and 13 benchmarks with 66 unique domains, comparing against both the latest similarly scaled small models and large LLMs. AlgoPilot significantly improves average accuracy for the same model from 38.4% to 72.9%, outperforming the best large LLM prompting baseline GLM-5 by 11.7%. Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of each component. Notably, we show that the learned steering and formulation modules can transfer across execution models, suggesting their potential to improve other LLMs. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[WASP-Mem: Let Agent Organize Procedural Memory. Human Spend Effort on Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04003)**  `arXiv:2611.04003`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Peizhi Niu, Ge Shi, Vaibhav Gorde, Sarthak Ghosh, Christos Faloutsos, Min Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents struggle to automate complex enterprise procedures because static, pre-built harness cannot capture proprietary policies, undocumented behaviors, or state-dependent recovery paths that surface only during execution. Procedural memory accrued from agent experience offers a training-free path to closing this gap -- yet unlike episodic records or semantic facts, it must consolidate repeated successes and diagnosed failures into reusable actions and recovery strategies. We introduce WASP-Mem (Wiki-based Agent-Synthesized Procedural Memory), a nimble but effective method that distills execution traces and feedback into persistent, interlinked wiki pages, enabling continual agent learning. On TheAgentCompany benchmark, WASP-Mem raises the checkpoint pass rate by 4.6-6.1% over the strongest baseline we compared across three models while requiring 57-61% less storage. We further present a systematic controlled study spanning consolidation method, feedback type, trace ingestion scope, and refinement rounds. Our results reveal that prescriptive knowledge organization such as human defined schemas and explicit deduplication rules hurts performance rather than closing the procedural-knowledge gap. In contrast, human diagnosis converts failed traces into corrective knowledge and drives sustained improvement: a single round of human diagnosis outperforms four rounds of self-judgment by 7.0-10.9%. These findings point to a simple division of labor: let agents organize procedural memory, and invest human effort where it matters most -- diagnosis.
  </details>

- **[StoreBench: A Live-Commerce Environment for Evaluating and Training Autonomous Operator Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10942)**  `arXiv:2610.10942`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Daksh Raghuvanshi, Ved Vedere, Yifan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning environments are now a primary lever for improving large language model (LLM) capabilities in post-training, yet most agentic benchmarks remain static: the world moves only when the agent acts, the reward is a terminal verdict, and the pass bar is set arbitrarily. We introduce StoreBench, a live-commerce environment in which an agent runs a mid-size online apparel store on a production-grade commerce backend, testing long-horizon planning and economic judgment under uncertainty. Customers order around the clock, suppliers reprice and fail, and market shocks arrive with partial or no warning. The agent acts through the same 29 merchant tools a human operator would use, under a windowed operation budget that makes simulated time a function of actions taken, so model latency cannot influence simulated time. Pass thresholds are calibrated against scripted anchor policies, the reward is hardened against a catalogue of reward hacks, and every episode replays identically given a sequence of actions. We evaluate seven frontier LLMs on 11 scenarios of 30 to 45 days and a full simulated year, over three world seeds at matched reasoning effort. No model matches the scripted smart-triage policy on average: the best, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, passes 49% of task-seed cells against the heuristic's 97%. Human experts working through the same tools and budgets outscore every model (mean composite 0.708 vs. 0.700). Over a full simulated year under the Claude Code harness, most models show dramatic performance improvement. In a GRPO post-training run, Qwen3.5-27B trained on only five disjoint tasks raises its mean composite on the held-out evaluation tasks from 0.136 to 0.373. We release five example training-split tasks, ten sample trajectories, and the scoring and verification tooling; the full environment and evaluation suite are withheld to keep the benchmark uncontaminated.
  </details>

- **[Think Less, Locally: Local Preference Learning for Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04814)**  `arXiv:2611.04814`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Muyao Yuan, Liyan Xu, Weizhan Zhang, Muyan Jiao, Jiangnan Li, Jinchao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models achieve strong performance on challenging tasks, but their long chain-of-thought often incurs substantial inference costs by continuing to deliberate after sufficient information has been obtained. Existing reasoning compression methods primarily operate on reasoning trajectories, paying less attention to the local decision of whether further reasoning is necessary given the current state. We propose LoCo, a local preference learning method for reasoning compression. LoCo detects candidate overthinking states from the model's own traces, freezes the corresponding reasoning prefixes, regenerates shorter verified continuations from the same states, and trains on the resulting state-conditioned preference pairs with DPO. This formulation teaches the model when to continue reasoning and when to conclude, rather than simply encouraging shorter trajectories overall. We construct training data entirely from self-generated mathematical traces and evaluate LoCo on five mathematical and three code generation benchmarks. LoCo reduces completion length by 41.7\%--45.4\% on mathematics and 23.4\%--36.3\% on code, while maintaining or improving accuracy and achieving the best AUC across all code benchmarks. LoCo consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off on mathematics and transfers effectively to code generation despite math-only training, demonstrating the cross-domain generality of local preference learning for reasoning compression. The code and model will be publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Alignment Faking Depends on the Value, Not Just the Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03650)**  `arXiv:2611.03650`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Manodeep Ray, Vivek Srivastava, Shirish Karande, Murari Mandal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models can follow a directive when they believe they are monitored and abandon it when they believe they are not, a pattern termed Alignment Faking. Prior work establishes this for harmful-content objectives and a handful of moral-foundations values, leaving open how it distributes across a wider value space. We argue it is value-dependent: whether compliance survives a change in perceived oversight is a property of the model--value pairing, not of the model alone. Our framework profiles Alignment Faking per value, scoring each model-value pair under three conditions: bare (no system prompt), unmonitored, and monitored. On 32 ValueBench dimensions across ten open- and closed-weight models (8B-~675B), compliance gaps span 0.05-0.50, with a distilled 70B above a ~675B model. We observe that majority of the structural variance depends on which model was asked about which value and not on the model or value alone. A six-category taxonomy over reasoning traces tracks the behavioural shift: training-signal reasoning dominates under monitoring and value-conflict reasoning dominates when unmonitored. We package per-model results as value cards, extending model cards with behavioral disposition. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Speculative Reasoning Improves Long Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05345)**  `arXiv:2611.05345`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyin Luo, Wei Fang, Lijie Yang, Owen Stepan, Daniel Suit, Jeff Krapf, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding has been treated purely as a systems optimization that speeds up large language model decoding without changing the model behavior. Under this view, the growing effort to train better draft models has been focused solely on acceptance rate. We argue that this view does not hold true for long-horizon agent reasoning. Measuring frontier open-weight models on complex agentic tasks with different speculative decoding approaches, we found that numerical noise grows within verification windows, and the draft acceptance results of the thinking process can change the output distribution of the model after 30 steps, thus noticeably influencing the reasoning accuracy of long-horizon agents in random directions. In this work, we improve the reasoning capability of target models by controlling such noise. While we cannot change the overall token distribution verified by the target model, we can encode critical reasoning with highest precision by training the draft model to be rejected. We identify these points in agent traces and train the draft model to favor adversarial preference. At inference the trained draft and the target part ways precisely at these decision points, verification restarts, so that critical thinking tokens are sampled from the target model and encoded under its least noisy conditions. This approach makes critical reasoning better indexed and more durable over a long horizon. On complex agentic coding benchmarks, our approach raises end-to-end success rate with no change to the target model or its output distribution. Our results recast the speculative decoding mechanism from a pure accelerator into an active allocator for context precision that improves the accuracy of long-horizon agents by training 0.06% to 0.28% of hosted parameters.
  </details>

- **[AgentAM: An AI Agent for Spatter- and Defect-Aware Process Optimization in Laser Powder Bed Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05171)**  `arXiv:2611.05171`  `cs.AI`  
  _Olabode T. Ajenifujah, Amir Barati Farimani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predictive models for laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) are rarely used on the shop floor because querying them requires programming and model-specific knowledge. We present AgentAM, a large language model (LLM) agent that gives process engineers natural-language access to CFD-trained surrogates of spatter and melt pool geometry for SS316L through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Polynomial surrogates trained on 279 single-track simulations predict melt pool dimensions ($R^2 \geq 0.95$) and spatter volume ($R^2 = 0.83$ on test data) at any power-velocity point in the trained range, and geometric criteria assign each point a geometry-derived defect regime (keyhole, lack of fusion, balling or stable) that matches the regime obtained from the simulated geometry in 93.4% of tracks under cross-validation. The agent selects and parameterizes tools for prediction, process-window scans and constrained optimization. Minimum-spatter optima often sat within 10 $\mu$m of the lack-of-fusion boundary; a margin-aware optimizer moved them away from all thresholds and raised the estimated probability of remaining classified Good from 0.94 to 1.00 under an assumed 2% process drift, and from 0.72 to 0.91 when jointly sampled cross-validated geometry residuals were added. About 40-50 well-spread simulations sufficed to reproduce the defect map. To evaluate the agent layer, we introduce a 145-prompt benchmark scored against direct tool calls, plus eight prompts (40 runs) on margin-aware requests. Over the 725 runs of the main benchmark the agent passed the intended inputs in 98.5% of parsing prompts, reported the scored spatter values and defect classes faithfully and met every stated constraint in the 65 feasible optimization runs, within a median 1.0% of the brute-force optimum.
  </details>

- **[Catalyzing Interactive HTML Generation Capabilities via High-Fidelity Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05151)**  `arXiv:2611.05151`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xujia Wang, Yixuan Liu, Shaohan Huang, Furu Wei, Lei Hou, Bin Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating HTML from natural-language specifications is an important application of code large language models (LLMs). Despite producing renderable pages, current models often fail to ensure functionality and visual quality during actual interaction. Existing methods typically target task-specific webpage supervision, leaving demanding interactive artifact optimization underexplored. Using mini-games as a challenging testbed, we expose limitations in interaction experience, reward signal, and credit assignment fidelity that are obscured by shallow webpage interactions. We introduce WebCatalyst, a browser-interaction reinforcement learning framework with three complementary components: a game-level GUI assessor for sustained interaction and evaluation, Visual Anchor for mitigating VLM reward hacking, and Action-Trace Credit for grounding token-level credit assignment in runtime execution traces. Trained on fewer than 1K requests, predominantly for games, WebCatalyst raises the Cookie-Bench Mini Games score from 68.8 to 75.3 and also the WebGen-Bench accuracy from 48.8 to 56.9 on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, outperforming Claude Opus 4.7 in average rank across evaluated metrics. Further analysis shows that Visual Anchor prevents aesthetic degradation, while runtime-grounded credit assignment accelerates functional improvement. Together, our results show that improving feedback fidelity is the key to substantially advancing interactive HTML generation.
  </details>

- **[Meta$^2$: Precomputing Reusable Knowledge for Online Harness Evolution in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05139)**  `arXiv:2611.05139`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lingzhi Yuan, Yiguang Cai, Weize Liu, Siqi Yang, Feijiang Han, Yongkyun Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness optimization improves long-horizon agents by refining the prompts, tools, memory, and hooks around a frozen model. Offline methods can learn from completed trajectories, but deploy one shared harness that may overfit training tasks and cannot react to evidence revealed during a new run. Online evolution can adapt before, during, or after one run through an evolver, but may have to reason about every intervention from scratch or rely on verifier feedback that is usually unavailable at test time. We introduce Meta$^2$, which moves offline optimization one level up: rather than optimizing a shared harness, it learns reusable knowledge for harness evolution and applies it within each task online. Offline, Meta$^2$ learns a textual Meta-Skill $M$ by contrasting training rollouts of the same task agent under three configurations: without an evolver, with an evolver guided by the current version of $M$, and with an evolver given privileged verifier information available only in training. Contrasts in trajectories and outcomes are aggregated across tasks to refine $M$ into reusable guidance on when and how to evolve, when to leave the harness unchanged, and which interventions to avoid. Online, an evolver combines the frozen $M$ with emerging trajectory evidence to decide whether to KEEP the harness unchanged or EVOLVE it through a task-local edit. Each edit applies to the rest of the same run without resetting its environment or progress. Thus, evolution guidance transfers across tasks while harness edits remain task-local, and held-out verifier feedback is never available to the evolver. On two long-horizon benchmarks, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and DeepSWE 1.1, Meta$^2$ achieves the highest performance among evaluated methods with GPT-5.6-Terra, reaching 81.00% and 44.44%, respectively. Ablations support the value of contrastive evolution guidance and keeping online harness edits task-local.
  </details>

- **[AgenticFinBench: Benchmarking Financial Agents on Diverse Tasks from Real-World Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04813)**  `arXiv:2611.04813`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shu Zhou, Ziyang He, Yuxin Rao, Yudong Fang, Jiali Qian, Dongyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial agents are becoming an important class of real-world AI systems. Financial tasks vary widely in type and difficulty, and many are open-ended with no ground-truth answers. However, existing benchmarks neither capture this breadth and open-endedness nor separate grounding from correctness. To bridge this gap, we introduce $\textbf{AgenticFinBench}$, a benchmark of 230 real-world financial tasks spanning 8 categories, 2 languages, and 4 complexity levels. We also propose a reference-free evaluation framework that combines question-specific rubrics with a two-stage claim audit that detects hallucinations, defined as claims absent from the agent's trace and refuted by external evidence. This framework better matches how financial work is assessed in practice. Experiments across 18 agent configurations reveal three key findings: (1) Ignoring hallucinations can overstate agent performance: hallucination auditing lowers the baseline leader's score by 7.58 points and moves it to fourth place. (2) Harness differences explain more variation in evidence grounding than model differences (28.6% vs 8.8%). (3) Quality scores fall from 89.44 on direct retrieval to 79.18 on the most complex tasks. Case studies show that agents struggle to explain how events affect company profits or stock prices, compare financial data on a consistent basis, and justify their conclusions. By releasing AgenticFinBench, we aim to advance financial reasoning, factual verification, and agent design for more reliable responses to diverse real-world financial requests. Our project page is at $\textbf{this https URL}$.
  </details>

- **[RAHC: Rule-Augmented Hyper-Relational Knowledge Graph Completion for Materials Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04809)**  `arXiv:2611.04809`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Sun, Sijia Xu, Zihan Chen, Zhengyi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery depends on reusing experimental knowledge, yet records scattered across papers and repositories are often incomplete. In materials synthesis, recovering missing conditions requires reasoning about the target material and the observed context of a specific experiment. Hyper-relational knowledge graphs (HKGs) represent this context through qualifiers, but existing completion methods leave domain knowledge and experimental regularities implicit. We propose RAHC, a rule-augmented HKG framework for single-slot and joint multi-slot synthesis-condition completion. RAHC builds a traceable rule catalog from training records and retrieved literature, retrieves rules for each query-candidate pair, and separately encodes graph structure and rule evidence. Hard rules constrain candidate validity, while soft rules guide ranking. RAHC-G aggregates rules using global reliability; RAHC-Q additionally learns query-specific relevance. Gated fusion combines structural and rule representations, while tuple-conclusion rules provide cross-condition evidence for joint completion.
