Experiments on five materials-synthesis datasets show improvements in single-slot and joint completion under Semi-inductive and Open-Answer Inductive settings, with Semi-inductive MRR gains of up to 15.6% over MAYPL. RAHC also improves performance on the entity-disjoint ILPC'22 Small benchmark, supporting transfer to fully inductive KG completion. These results support experimental condition selection, synthesis planning, and the reuse of scientific knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Kierston & KierstonBench: Simulation of Allentown, PA and Evaluation of Language Models in Crime Investigation and Surveillance Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04785)**  `arXiv:2611.04785`  `cs.AI`  
  _Andrew Semenov, Svyatoslav Dorofeev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are candidates for collecting and analyzing records in criminal investigations and mass surveillance. Their capability and accuracy in this work cannot currently be measured. Real deployments lack the ground truth needed to score a model's conclusions, and no benchmark evaluates these capabilities in a simulated environment. We present Kierston, a city-scale simulation engine, and KierstonBench, a benchmark that tests these capabilities against known simulated events. Kierston models approximately 125,000 residents of Allentown, Pennsylvania, generating their daily activities and the telecommunications, financial, administrative and license-plate records that surveillance and investigative systems collect about them. These records derive from a common behavioral timeline and are distributed across sources representing separate providers. Models investigate through a restricted records interface, linking observations across sources without access to the underlying simulation. The benchmark comprises 32 task families covering record linkage, routine reconstruction, incident investigation and assessment of deceptive accounts. In an evaluation of sixteen language models, most could not perform records investigation and submitted identifications the records did not support. A small number of frontier models solved most of the tasks and abstained when evidence was insufficient. In some cases these models still fell for common deceptions and assumptions, and none replaced an investigator.
  </details>

- **[When Should an LLM Steering Edit Depend on the Input?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04757)**  `arXiv:2611.04757`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rachael Harkness, Konrad Rawlik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An activation steering edit should depend on the input exactly when the destination varies \emph{inside} the class a query requests, and that is measurable before any rule is fitted or applied. Contrastive Activation Addition (CAA) adds one fixed direction to every input, the difference of the class means. Conditioning it on the requested class accounts for the between-class variation in the required displacement and leaves the within-class variation untouched, which no rule that reads only the source state can express. We study the impact of increasing input-dependence in the steering edit, moving from a difference of means to a destination retrieved per query --- the mean of the $k$ stored states nearest the query, inside the requested class --- in which the two baselines are endpoints: at $k$ equal to the class size the retrieved rule \emph{is} the class-conditional difference of means. We measure input-dependent steering on six substitution corpora that vary in whether the edit changes a word or a clause, and on an external benchmark, and find that input-dependent steering outperforms fixed-direction steering when the destination varies within the class. Retrieval wins at every layer of every substitution corpus in two models, and the level of input-dependence needed governs how to apply it: word-level edits are best made in the first tenth of the network from a few neighbours, clause-level edits at any depth from the class mean. Where the class already determines the answer the class mean suffices and retrieval only matches it, though a fixed direction still fails there. Four quantities computed from the states alone identify the regime in which the edit should be applied.
  </details>

- **[CogniLife: Continuous Cognitive Distillation for Deployed Multi-Expert Agent Marketplaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04483)**  `arXiv:2611.04483`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuwei Tan, Jingchun Huang, Ming Cai, Shuangquan Lyu, Tiange Wang, Yinzhi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-expert agent marketplaces -- where creators publish domain-specific AI experts for end users to consult -- are an emerging deployment pattern for language technology. A published expert can receive many requests yet little evidence about its creator, who remains active elsewhere: the cold-expert problem. We frame continuous cognitive distillation as converting attributed creator observations into revisable decision rules, communication patterns, and domain procedures. CogniLife proposes a cross-expert attribution design for this problem, with Creator Profiles for transferable creator patterns and Expert Journals for listing-specific knowledge; this design is not yet implemented or evaluated. A deployed platform partially realizes the surrounding lifecycle through creator-authorized authoring, capability-owned proposal filtering, and reversible consolidation of eligible owner-scoped memory. On ProfyBench-v1, an 85-task offline benchmark built from replay seeds and controlled internal tests, the Profy Full configuration as a whole achieves a mean overall score of 4.65/5 from the primary LLM judge, compared with 4.01 for Raw LLM and 4.28 for Expert Prompt, with the largest gains in completeness, specificity, and missing-information tasks. These configuration-level scores do not test cold-expert mitigation, cross-expert transfer, or longitudinal distillation.
  </details>

- **[Twice as Fast, Until It Isn't: Quantized Serving of Mid-Size Open LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04380)**  `arXiv:2611.04380`  `cs.AI`  
  _Smit Kadvani, Kriti Faujdar, Spurthi Tallam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A team serving a 9B-20B open-weight language model on vLLM or SGLang must choose a quantization recipe, and needs two numbers: the speedup over BF16 and the accuracy given up. We assemble every public measurement of these numbers that we could verify and find that the record supplies them only in pieces. Only two checkpoints, both INT4 builds of Gemma 3 12B, have both a vLLM timing and an accuracy score, and that score covers a single MMLU subject, one that the AWQ build was calibrated on. The batch-1 timings predate current engine releases, AMD and Intel Gaudi have no timing in this band at all, and FP4 has none against BF16. The timings that do exist follow one rule. Decoding a small batch is limited by memory bandwidth, so 4-bit weights roughly double speed (2.12x for Qwen2.5-14B on an A100), but the gain vanishes once a large batch makes the GPU compute-bound (1.02x for Gemma 3 12B at batch size 256), where FP8 is the recipe that can still help. Many published accuracy deltas, including every HumanEval delta, lie within benchmark sampling noise. We close with a seven-item reporting checklist for future measurements.
  </details>

- **[Harness-S: Learning from Sibling Harnesses for Generalizable Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04277)**  `arXiv:2611.04277`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kangning Zhang, Wenxiang Jiao, Bowen Fang, Shuai Shao, Jiachen Zhu, Rong Shan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness evolution from a common base exhibits Locality, Complementarity, and Nonstationarity: variants repair different failures, and their utility changes as the policy learns. Rather than seeking a universally superior harness, we introduce Harness-S to selectively rescue failures of a base execution system through sibling harnesses and transfer the resulting capabilities into a single policy that remains effective under the original harness. Directional Harness Evolution develops specialized sibling harnesses along distinct repair directions, forming a reusable Sibling Harness Pool that remains fixed during policy training. Complementary Harness Rescue Training invokes this pool only when all initial attempts under the base harness fail. The same policy generates fresh rollouts under selected auxiliary harnesses, and these trajectories replace the failed group for reinforcement learning. An Adaptive Harness Router selects the Top-k variants using Harness Behavior Profiles and voting across multiple LLMs. Policy-aware profile re-anchoring refreshes this evidence as the policy learns. Across AppWorld, SWE-Gym, and DBBench, the trained policy substantially outperforms policy-only training when returned to the original base harness. Behavioral analyses and held-out cases support the transfer of harness-elicited behaviors into the policy itself. Reusing the router for Rank-1 inference provides additional gains at the same executor rollout count.
  </details>

- **[Reliability Stress Testing of RAG Retrieval under Query-Relevant Corpus Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04264)**  `arXiv:2611.04264`  `cs.AI`  
  _Janet Sung, Kainan Zhou, Gangzhen Qian, Hang Xiao, Lu Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External text can affect a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system only after the retriever admits it to the model context. We stress this admission step while varying document content, attacker query information, per-target poison budget, and contamination scope. Across 623 Benchmarking Information Retrieval (BEIR) queries from SciFact and NFCorpus, five query-targeted documents per query reduce classical-retriever nDCG@10 by 40.2-61.5% in isolated-target stress. Full-query anchoring explains most Best Matching 25 (BM25) degradation, whereas appended text also shifts latent semantic analysis (LSA) and Hybrid rankings. A pretrained BGE-small validation reaches 100% target exposure for query-only P=5 on both datasets; adding the injection payload changes SciFact nDCG only from 0.269 to 0.271 and lowers NFCorpus exposure slightly to 98.8%, indicating that query anchoring remains the dominant retrieval-side factor for this dense model. Query length materially changes the interpretation of partial-information attacks: on 162 long SciFact queries, two random terms cover 19.9% of eligible terms and still yield 39.5% Hybrid target exposure. Multi-target contamination adds cross-target interference. Overlap-based admission can remove full-query poisons but may sharply reduce clean retrieval utility, and maximal marginal relevance (MMR) leaves exposure unchanged under the tested configuration. The study measures retrieval-layer failures, not downstream instruction following.
  </details>

- **[DualWorldBench: Can Agents Plan Deliveries across Symbolic and Grounded Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04228)**  `arXiv:2611.04228`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Chen, Bin Wen, Danyang Peng, Hong-Jie You, Zi-Qiao Shang, Lan-Zhe Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal agents have shown promising progress in embodied planning and web interaction. However, existing evaluations often rely on simplified tasks with limited combinatorial structure, leaving it unclear whether current agents can robustly integrate perception and reasoning for multi-goal planning under temporal, physical, and multimodal constraints. We introduce DualWorldBench, a unified benchmark for evaluating agent planning under controlled symbolic--temporal and grounded--topological complexity. Built around a shared pickup--delivery interface, DualWorldBench consists of three complementary components: TextWorld, which isolates symbolic planning under varying task compositions and release dynamics; SimWorld, which evaluates grounded path planning under controlled topological variation with text, image, or multimodal inputs; and DualWorld-VQA, a diagnostic suite spanning perception, scene understanding, and planning. Experiments reveal a common gap between local understanding and globally consistent planning. In TextWorld, agents struggle to derive optimal task sequences under interacting temporal constraints; in SimWorld, grounded observations are not reliably converted into feasible action sequences. DualWorld-VQA further confirms that perception and topology understanding do not necessarily translate into constrained planning. Together, these results identify the central challenge as composing symbolic or grounded evidence into globally feasible and efficient multi-goal plans. These findings establish DualWorldBench as a challenging testbed for agents with tighter perception--reasoning--planning integration. All datasets and code are publicly available at our project websitethis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DoGNAVY-Exploit: Harnessing Parallel Open-Weight Agents for Stage-Aware Vulnerability Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04196)**  `arXiv:2611.04196`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Wang, Dadi Guo, Weijie Guo, Zikai Xu, Chen Lu, Yizhe Zhuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier LLM agents can turn known vulnerabilities into working exploits, but are costly and often access-restricted due to their potential for malicious use. Open-weight models offer a more accessible alternative, but struggle to maintain progress across long exploitation chains. They also often fail to apply relevant security knowledge at the appropriate stage and in the right context to identify and evaluate promising attack directions. To address these limitations, we propose DoGNAVY-Exploit, a multi-agent harness built around two complementary designs: stage-aware decomposition with progressive expert knowledge disclosure, and hierarchical orchestration with parallel exploration. Specifically, DoGNAVY-Exploit uses configurable milestones to recast long-horizon exploitation as short, verifiable capability transitions and provides only the knowledge relevant to each stage. Within each milestone, a hierarchical architecture separates strategic planning from parallel execution: a planner proposes diverse attack routes, executors test them concurrently, and a controller accepts progress only when supported by runtime evidence. A shared structured memory consolidates verified facts, artifacts, replay steps, and failed routes across branches, allowing agents to build on collective progress and carry validated knowledge into subsequent milestones. In controlled benchmark environments, DoGNAVY-Exploit with GLM-5.2 achieves 97.5% Pass@1 on Cybench, and 97.5% zero-day and 100% one-day Pass@1 on CVE-Bench. On ExploitGym, it solves 79 tasks with GLM-5.2 and 157 with GLM-5.3, while the corresponding public Claude Code baselines solve 39 and 130 tasks, respectively. Ablation results further demonstrate that the stage-aware orchestration, expert knowledge, parallel exploration, and memory designs can substantially improve the exploitation capabilities of open-weight agents.
  </details>

- **[Query-based Submodular Data Selection for Efficient Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04059)**  `arXiv:2611.04059`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenghao Zhao, Yasutomo Kawanishi, Huihuo Zheng, Michael E. Papka, Yan Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models are fine-tuned for increasingly specialized tasks, their performance depends more on which training examples are selected than on how many. Given a large heterogeneous training pool and a small target set specifying the desired capability, targeted data selection aims to find a compact subset that maximizes downstream performance. Existing methods score each candidate independently against the target and select the top-ranked examples, yet this point-wise strategy ignores inter-example interactions: at tight budgets the selected subset is dominated by near-redundant points that waste capacity on overlapping information. We propose Query-based Submodular functions with Gradient-guided Synthesis (QSGS), which formulates targeted selection as a submodular graph cut over a gradient similarity graph. A modular relevance term pulls selected examples toward the target task, while a pairwise dispersion penalty suppresses intra-subset redundancy. The resulting objective balances target relevance and intra-subset redundancy using the same formulation across different selection budgets. Empirically, the benefit of diversity-aware selection becomes increasingly pronounced as the data budget decreases. To further close coverage gaps that no selection from existing data can bridge, we introduce a gradient-guided synthesis step that identifies under-covered target directions via residual subspace analysis and generates synthetic examples to fill them. Experiments on MMLU, BBH, and TydiQA across Llama-2, Llama-3, and Mistral, show that QSGS at a 0.5% budget matches or exceeds full-data performance on Mistral-7B, outperforms the strongest baseline across all benchmarks, and yields increasing gains as the budget shrinks.
  </details>

- **[Recursa: Toward Recursively Self-Improving Foundation Model Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04035)**  `arXiv:2611.04035`  `cs.AI`  
  _Erkang Zhu, Yin Lin, Xiaorong Shi, Xuanrui Lin, Jingren Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models in changing environments can encounter unfamiliar domain knowledge that their original training did not cover. However, new domains rarely provide large amounts of expert-created training data or reliable supervisory signals for further training. We introduce Recursa, a foundation model system that adapts to new domains by learning from its own experience without human experts or a stronger teacher model. Recursa uses a knowledge repository to store what it learns: domain-specific skills and instructions on when to apply them, along with memories of past task experiences and reflections on what to improve. Recursa bootstraps the repository from domain materials and then refines it through recursive self-improvement. It uses parallel learners to execute tasks and synthesize local improvements, then validates and merges the improvements into the knowledge repository. On Harvey LAB's Firm Knowledge benchmark, Recursa raises the all-pass rate on held-out test tasks from 30.0% to 55.0% with DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash and from 35.0% to 46.0% with Qwen3.8-Flash.
  </details>

- **[Controlling Action-Head Co-Adaptation to VLM Representation Anisotropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04006)**  `arXiv:2611.04006`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruijie Zhang, Jiayin Zheng, Yequan Zhao, Liyan Tan, Ziyue Liu, Zhengyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern learning systems increasingly combine pretrained models with downstream heads. Despite the growing importance of such modular designs, how the downstream model co-adapts to the geometry of pretrained representations remains relatively underexplored. We study this problem through vision-language-action models (VLAs), where a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) conditions an action model for control. We find that the action model progressively concentrates its sensitivity on high-variance dimensions of the VLM outputs. Through controlled experiments across different backbone-head models, we further show that counteracting this sensitivity concentration can be beneficial under representation--task mismatch settings. Motivated by these observations, we introduce \emph{Adaptive Interface Equalizer} (AdaIE), a parameter-free mechanism that reweights pretrained representations according to the evolving downstream sensitivity during training. AdaIE improves VLA success rates on simulation benchmarks, with gains of up to $+5.28$ percentage points (pp) on RoboCasa-GR1 and $+1.68$ pp on RoboTwin 2.0, and achieves an average gain of $+19$ pp across five real-world manipulation tasks. It further generalizes to diffusion policies and world models, improving downstream performance in both settings. Our results highlight interface co-adaptation as an important optimization problem when coupling pretrained representations with downstream models.
  </details>

- **[What Is This Memory Useful For? Measuring Task-Conditioned Utility in Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03930)**  `arXiv:2611.03930`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chih-Hsuan Yang, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Feng Yan, Rajeev Thakur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A stored experience may answer one future question and offer little help with another. We measure this task-conditioned utility rather than assigning each memory a single importance score. A task-blind writer stores a memory without seeing the later task; a frozen reader then answers that task with and without the memory, using identical filler. A cost ledger records the resources used to form, store and expose the memory.On single-call factual-recall tasks built from LongMemEval-V2 web-agent memories, the same memory helps more when it contains information needed for the answer. Its mean paired reward gain on these answer-bearing task pairs exceeds that on matched-control pairs by $+0.33$ (95% interval $+0.21$ to $+0.46$). The tested task-blind summaries and structured records yield smaller gains than Raw, including at fixed total context; a budget sweep is consistent with retention of answer-bearing content contributing to the difference. The bearing--control contrast remains positive in a new session with randomized memory position and under a second reader, although individual memory--task labels change. Generic retrieval performs above chance but misses most screen-defined answer-bearing memories. The factual-recall gains do not extend to the tested software-engineering and scientific-coding benchmarks, where even raw memories have no measurable effect. We provide a public, versioned Conditional Memory Utility Dataset of paired measurements and costs. The practical takeaway is to evaluate memory compression by the task utility it preserves alongside its costs. Task-conditioned measurements make this comparison possible; their stability must also be checked.
  </details>

- **[Low-Rank Hierarchical Merging for Efficient Long-to-Short Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03834)**  `arXiv:2611.03834`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zeqiu Yu, Xuesheng Zhang, Wenxiao Zhao, Shixiao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-to-Short (L2S) model merging seeks to combine the strong reasoning capability of long chain-of-thought models with the concise style of base models, but existing methods that rely on linear interpolation and first-order activation statistics fail to capture how the loss landscape responds to parameter displacement, leading to degraded accuracy on hard tasks. We propose Low-Rank Hierarchical Merging (LHM), a training-free framework that replaces heuristic statistics with lightweight second-order curvature information. LHM consists of three components: (1) a low-rank Hessian approximation that efficiently estimates per-layer Hessian traces via Hutchinson's estimator on random inputs, eliminating the need for task-specific data; (2) a Layer Second-Order Sensitivity (LSOS) index that combines Hessian trace with task-vector norm to quantify per-layer fusion difficulty; and (3) a Dual-Constraint Coefficient Mapping (DCCM) that converts LSOS scores into layer-adaptive merge coefficients while jointly satisfying a fusion-error bound and a global length-reduction target. Experiments across six mathematical benchmarks on 1.5B and 7B models show that LHM outperforms state-of-the-art methods such as ACM-TIES and ORCA, achieving over 4\% accuracy gain and up to 93.1\% reduction in output length relative to the long-CoT model.
  </details>

- **[Training LLM Agents to Automate LLM Post-Training with RSI-Dojo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03698)**  `arXiv:2611.03698`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuzheng Gao, Zhaojian Yu, Penghao Yin, Yuling Shi, Kai Cai, Shilin He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) aims to enable AI systems to autonomously build and improve AI systems themselves, including models, harnesses, and underlying infrastructure. Within this broader agenda, a central capability is enabling agents to train and improve base models. However, existing studies on automated model training primarily focused on workflow- or harness-level optimizations, rather than internalizing the ability to conduct post-training into the model itself. Developing this capability requires executable model training environments that can produce high-quality trajectories demonstrating how to select data, train models, diagnose failures, and iteratively improve results. In this paper, we introduce RSI-Dojo, a collection of executable model training environments designed to teach LLM agents to conduct post-training. To construct RSI-Dojo, we develop RSI-Craft, an automatic pipeline that converts human research artifacts into executable model training environments. RSI-Craft automatically collects research papers from top venues, instantiates datasets and metrics into a unified interface, and reproduces evaluation dependencies. It then filters tasks by data size, execution reliability, and discriminability, yielding RSI-Dojo with 281 seed model training tasks. Building on RSI-Dojo, we employ a trajectory collection method with an improved harness to collect high-quality model-training trajectories from frontier models, then retain examples through rubric-based and outcome-based filtering. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on these trajectories. The resulting RSI-Dojo-9B and RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B improve over their base models by 8.74 and 16.87 PostTrainBench points, respectively. RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B reaches 29.95 on PostTrainBench and 53.29 on PostTrainBench-Lite, matching the performance of frontier models such as GLM-5.2 and GPT 5.5.
  </details>

- **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412)**  `arXiv:2611.03412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Sihan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.
  </details>

- **[Axion 1.0: Open Agent Foundation Models, Simplified](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03233)**  `arXiv:2611.03233`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuqi Jia, Yunjie Tian, Jiayu Zheng, Yijun Liang, Jingchen Sun, Mengyu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Axion 1.0, a fully open agent foundation model that combines strong and broad agentic capabilities with a remarkably simple training recipe. Axion 1.0 is trained in only three stages: two lightweight supervised fine-tuning stages followed by reinforcement learning, without dedicated mid-training, direct preference optimization (DPO), or on-policy distillation (OPD). The first SFT stage develops strong self-contained reasoning through model-aware data construction, where we synthesize challenging problems and adversarially strengthen them based on the base model's own successful solution strategies. The second stage extends these capabilities to tool-dependent reasoning through a scalable pipeline for constructing diverse and automatically verifiable tasks grounded in real-world data sources and deterministic computation. Finally, reinforcement learning further strengthens long-horizon reasoning and interaction with external tools and environments. Despite this compact training recipe, Axion 1.0 achieves strong performance across a broad suite of agentic benchmarks and is competitive with leading commercial models on several of them. Equally importantly, Axion 1.0 is open beyond model weights: we release the complete recipe, including data construction, generation, filtering, verification, prompts, and training code, enabling the entire development process to be reproduced and extended. Axion 1.0 demonstrates that strong agent foundation models can be both open and simple, offering a practical and reproducible path toward advancing open agentic intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Research Agent on MSA Organization to Improve Protein-Protein Complex Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03183)**  `arXiv:2611.03183`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zilu Zhang, Jing Bao, Lifeng Qiao, Sheng Xu, Qiantai Feng, Siqi Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein-complex structure prediction with AlphaFold 3 depends on MSA content, depth, and cross-chain pairing. Current pipelines apply uniform recipes, typically maximizing depth and pairing by species, yet even whether cross-chain pairing helps AF3 remains contested. We show that the value of both depth and pairing is strongly target-dependent, so no single recipe is optimal across interfaces. We introduce an autonomous agent that treats a frozen AF3 as a black-box experimental instrument. For each target, the agent formulates biological hypotheses, analyzes and reorganizes the MSA, and iteratively refines sequence selection and pairing using predictor label-free signals alone. On 1{,}015 protein--protein interfaces from three benchmarks released after AF3's training cutoff, the agent improves overall interface DockQ over AF3's default pipeline and fixed MSA recipes on every benchmark, while using on average 60\% fewer MSA sequences than the default. Gains are largest for inter-species heteromers, where species-based pairing is unreliable: mean DockQ increases from 0.465 to 0.612 on FoldBench and from 0.482 to 0.562 on HD439. The improvements arise from both sequence construction and pairing policy, including deliberate non-pairing. These results recast MSA design as a target-specific problem and show that an autonomous agent can adapt it at test time through biological reasoning and self-directed, confidence-guided experimentation.
  </details>

- **[Calibration-Constrained Diagnostics for Evaluating LLM Self-Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02253)**  `arXiv:2611.02253`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jae Oh Woo, Mengdie Flora Wang, Rahul Ghosh, Baishali Chaudhury, Mun Young Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a reusable evaluation artifact for diagnosing whether LLM self-reported confidence can support confidence-gated act-or-defer decisions. The artifact is designed for evaluation rather than safety certification: it provides a black-box protocol, prompts, judge-reference labels, model outputs, reproduction scripts, and calibration-constrained diagnostic metrics for auditing LLM self-evaluation across math, factual, and commonsense reasoning tasks. Our central finding is that aggregate uncertainty summaries can produce deployment-misleading rankings: a model that appears strongest under standard aggregate calibration metrics can fail under category-level calibration constraints, while a model with worse aggregate calibration can exhibit stronger calibration-constrained behavior in the positive-margin region where high self-selection accuracy and tight calibration coexist. To expose this failure mode, we define PVC and C-PVC as category-level diagnostics over an accuracy-by-calibration surface: PVC counts categories whose self-evaluation accuracy exceeds a threshold, while C-PVC additionally requires category-level calibration error below a tolerance. We aggregate these diagnostics using Volume Under Surface (VUS) and a positive-margin restriction; they are VC/fat-shattering motivated but are empirical evaluation proxies, not literal VC-dimension estimates. Across 13 models and four benchmarks, the artifact shows weak confidence-correctness separation, stable diagnostic rankings under alternative categorizations and judge-label perturbations, and persistent positive-margin failures not captured by ECE or Brier score. Confidence-gated self-evaluation therefore requires calibration-constrained, category-level diagnostics, and we provide a reusable artifact for applying them under black-box access.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-grounded large language models guide perovskite solar cell design and optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00633)**  `arXiv:2611.00633`  `cs.AI`  
  _YanKai Chen, Zhi Wan, ZiHao Li, Tao Jing_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using large language models to interpret experimental evidence, design discriminating experiments and formulate well-supported mechanistic explanations remains a central challenge in materials research. Here we establish PSM-Bench, an 800-question benchmark developed by perovskite researchers to evaluate scientific reasoning in perovskite photovoltaics. We also introduce OpenPerov, an expert-informed language-model framework that learns from expert-calibrated evidence records reconstructed from 4,035 training articles; these records link experimental strategies, controls and observations to mechanistic interpretations. Structured-evidence adaptation outperformed conventional question-answer training, and OpenPerov Flash ranked first among 13 closed-book systems. OpenPerov Pro raised Scientific Mastery further, from 87.6 to 89.1, by revising selected passages with retrieved evidence. On questions derived from subsequent studies, both models also outscored expert-authored answers. In an experimental test of self-assembled monolayer design, OpenPerov selected a K analogue that outperformed the Na analogue preferred by experts. Devices incorporating the selected contact reached a power conversion efficiency of 27.05% and retained 96.74% of their initial efficiency after 1,000 h of dark thermal ageing at 85 $^\circ$C. These results show that reconstructed scientific reasoning provides an effective basis for building domain models that interpret experiments and inform materials design.
  </details>

- **[WorkFactory: Turning Professional Intents into Executable Work for Scalable Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37161)**  `arXiv:2610.37161`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Zeng, Tingyang Chen, Jia Deng, Qiyao Wang, Shuo Tang, Yike Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling supervised fine-tuning for working agents requires not only broad coverage of professional tasks, but also expert demonstrations that faithfully carry out professional workflows to completion. However, missing or inconsistent task information can disrupt expert demonstrations, weakening the resulting supervision. We introduce WorkFactory, a scalable task-construction framework that turns professional intents into executable task environments through hybrid intent sourcing, semantic workflow planning, and execution-based repair. Starting from these intents, WorkFactory constructs a Semantic Workflow Graph that explicitly represents information requirements, dependencies, and locations within the task environment. The graph guides the instantiation of task environments, while an adversarial execution-refinement loop exposes missing or inconsistent information and steers targeted repairs that preserve the original intent. Using WorkFactory, we construct 112,686 tasks spanning 612 occupations and train the WorkPilot family at 4B, 27B, and 35B-A3B scales on the resulting teacher trajectories. All three models improve over their respective base models across four professional-work benchmarks, with WorkPilot-35B-A3B rising from 879 to 1565 Elo on GDPval-AA v2.1. Scaling experiments with up to 110k tasks further show that larger models benefit more from additional task data but exhibit diminishing returns at smaller data scales, while WorkPilot's long-horizon execution evolves from verbose trajectories toward more efficient task completion. Controlled component studies quantify the negative effects of task-environment defects on downstream performance and show how semantic workflow planning and execution-based repair yield downstream gains through improved task-environment quality. The subset of data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BARC-Bench: Benign Asks, Risky Consequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32024)**  `arXiv:2610.32024`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kangkang Li, Kelvin Luu, Lily Ma, Tu Trinh, Ying Liu, Mohamed Elfeki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task instructions from users for agents are inherently underspecified, especially regarding safety boundaries--creating behavioral risks. To study these risks, we introduce BARC-Bench, a collection of 110 tasks across nine simulated workspace environments where benign requests leave openings for unsafe actions. In this benchmark, we evaluate agent behavior against safety boundaries established by workspace records, implicit environmental context, and unstated social norms. For example, an agent asked to schedule off-boarding discussions must arrange the meeting without prematurely revealing an employee's resignation through a shared calendar invitation. Evaluating four frontier model configurations across five attempts per task reveals that models are not consistently safe: the share of tasks completed safely on all five attempts ranges from 9.1% to 72.7%, with even the strongest configuration producing a safety violation on 27.3% of tasks. These results highlight an asymmetric operational risk: while models frequently complete complex workflows, their inability to consistently respect unstated boundaries presents a substantial barrier to safe autonomous delegation.
  </details>

- **[D-Skill: Distilling Teacher Knowledge through Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28914)**  `arXiv:2610.28914`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuo Yuan, Dacheng Li, Joseph E. Gonzalez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language-model agents excel at complex tasks, but high inference costs make transferring their capabilities to smaller models essential. Traditional knowledge distillation achieves this through parameter updates, requiring access to the student's weights and additional training compute while encoding the transferred knowledge implicitly in the parameters. We introduce D-Skill, a framework that treats the teacher as a black box and distills its behavioral advantage into an interpretable natural-language skill by analyzing paired rollouts and identifying behavioral divergences and agreements. The resulting lessons are consolidated offline, with any compatible skill-update backend, into a skill document that is provided to the student at inference time. D-Skill constructs the skill entirely offline from fixed trajectories, without labels, further environment interaction, or weight updates after rollout collection. Across SpreadsheetBench-Verified, ALFWorld, and chess puzzles spanning multiple teacher-student pairs, D-Skill closes 75%-95% of the teacher-student gap without touching model weights. After a single offline pass over the paired traces, D-Skill outperforms one-epoch SFT baselines and remains competitive with multi-epoch SFT. Under the same model family, the resulting student also incurs 5.7x-8.3x lower inference cost per task than the teacher. Our results demonstrate that natural-language skill distillation offers a performant, interpretable, and efficient alternative to weight-based capability transfer.
  </details>

- **[Safe Actions Alone Do Not Ensure Safe Agents: Identifying Unfulfilled Obligations with Guard Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11773)**  `arXiv:2610.11773`  `cs.AI`  
  _Youwei Feng, Yitong Zhang, Yuetong Liu, Jia Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Guard models are increasingly used to safeguard LLM-based agents, primarily by identifying actions that agents are forbidden to perform. However, identifying forbidden actions alone is insufficient to ensure agent safety. In this paper, we argue that agent safety also depends on identifying required yet unperformed safety-critical actions, which we call obligations. Our preliminary study on a popular benchmark for evaluating safety shows that 56.92% of GLM-5.3 trajectories contain unfulfilled obligations, compared with only 30.00% containing forbidden actions. This finding reveals unfulfilled obligations as a major and previously overlooked source of safety risk. However, to our knowledge, no existing benchmark evaluates whether guard models can identify these obligations. To close this gap, we introduce ObligationBench, the first benchmark for evaluating the capability of obligation identification, comprising 240 expert-validated trajectories covering issue resolution, feature development, and terminal operations. Our evaluation of 14 representative models reveals substantial limitations: the highest recall and exact-match rate are only 48.97% and 10.00%, respectively. To address these limitations, we develop ObligationGuard using 40,000 synthetic training examples. ObligationGuard achieves 57.52% recall and an exact-match rate of 21.67%, surpassing all evaluated models on both metrics. We call on the community to incorporate obligation identification into the design and evaluation of future guard models to improve agent safety.
  </details>

- **[Global Coherence: When Every Agent Is Right and the Team Is Still Wrong - A Local-to-Global Semantic Foundation for Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02036)**  `arXiv:2610.02036`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xin Heng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents share code, documents, tools, and analyses. No participant sees the whole state, so every step can look right while the combined result is wrong. We call this the global coherence problem. It is a problem of state, not only of intelligence: a stronger model decides better locally, but cannot see what is missing from its context. The Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem gives the exact condition. An agent can always act validly exactly when every world consistent with its context allows a common action. When look-alike worlds need different actions, no reasoning, role, message, or sample can guarantee the right one; with k such worlds whose valid actions are disjoint, the best randomized policy succeeds with probability 1/k. Finding the smallest sufficient context is NP-hard, but refinement finds one and keeps it current as rules change. A second failure survives full information: valid pieces can fail to fit into one world. The same theorem sets the conditions for coherence; sheaves and groupoids are one way to meet them. Models propose, and the harness decides what is valid. Ten studies test it. On TeamBench, teams overspend a shared budget in 5/5 runs; showing them the live count still leaves 4/5, and a harness that owns the budget leaves 0/5. Identity collisions that no pairwise check can see appear in 23 of 45 public alignment networks; split-view agents create them in 9 of 40 merges, and a network check removes all nine. A frontier LLM solves 40/40 revision tasks when it sees the deciding event; with the event hidden, no arm that reasons longer, votes, or splits into roles significantly beats chance, and a companion study replicates this on models from a second provider. In tau2-bench Telecom, after a silent revert, the harness scores 1.00 against 0.07 for call-time policy checks. Refinement keeps a sufficient context at 28% of the cost of sharing every field.
  </details>

- **[SAGE: Mitigating Long-Horizon Reasoning Biases via Topological Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30192)**  `arXiv:2609.30192`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyue Zeng, Jiawei Zhang, Yujun Yan, Dawei Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon reasoning remains a central challenge for large language models (LLMs) under sparse-reward regimes. We argue that this brittleness arises from two biases induced by complex reasoning spaces: an exploration bias, where models are drawn toward locally plausible but structurally unstable branches, and a compounding bias, where small local deviations accumulate across depth and suppress rare rewards. We introduce Symbolic Closure Analysis (SCA) as a theoretical lens characterizing how branching structures and sparse rewards induce these biases in long-horizon reasoning with local admissibility, and as a design principle for structural priors in less formal reasoning tasks. Motivated by this analysis, we propose SAGE (Structural Admissibility-Guided Exploration), a unified framework that injects structural guidance to alleviate exploration bias and compounding bias in long-horizon reasoning. SAGE combines two complementary structural guidance: algebraic sparsification, which projects locally admissible candidates onto operator-indexed algebraic subspaces to suppress spurious branching and mitigate exploration bias, and hyperbolic structural guidance, which embeds reasoning states into a negatively curved space to provide dense depth-wise signals and mitigate compounding bias. Across 12 benchmarks and 7 model families, SAGE outperforms competitive baselines. In particular, SAGE achieves up to an 8-fold improvement on the Andrews-Curtis problem, an open real-world long-horizon task. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CacheBridge: Efficient Cross-Model KV Transfer for Multi-Model LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00891)**  `arXiv:2609.00891`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyu Qu, Siyuan Lu, Zhiyu Chen, Sheng Wang, Tao Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-model LLM serving uses routers, cascades and agent workflows to move requests across models with different costs and capabilities. Existing serving systems reuse KV caches only within one model, so every switch forces the target to re-prefill history the source already processed, adding time to first token (TTFT) that grows with history length and target size. Cross-model KV transfer can replace this prefill, yet prior methods are slower than re-prefill end to end. We present CacheBridge, the first serving system to accelerate heterogeneous multi-model workloads through cross-model KV transfer. It co-designs a compact, attention-calibrated KV mapper with systematic optimization for online multi-model serving, which makes model switch efficient on latency and memory cost. In Qwen3 14B$\rightarrow$32B agent serving on $\tau$-bench, it reduces mean TTFT by $30$ to $98\%$ versus re-prefill at $1$ to $24$ QPS with comparable task success. Across five model pairs from three families, it uses $8$ to $12\times$ less mapper memory than Closed-Form Mapper and retains on average $97.94\%$ and $102.94\%$ of re-prefill task quality on chat/reasoning and RAG workloads, respectively. The promising results have opened new opportunities for efficient multi-model LLM serving through cross-model KV reuse.
  </details>

- **[GSAR: Goal-State-Anchor Rewards for Mobile GUI Agents with Self-Evolving Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22847)**  `arXiv:2608.22847`  `cs.AI`  
  _Long Zhang, Yuhan Chen, Chaoran Zhang, Wanxia Cao, Kun Huang, Pengzhi Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) based GUI agents stand to benefit significantly from online reinforcement learning (RL). However, their training is bottlenecked by two fundamental issues: current data synthesis methods for GUI Agents rely on specific environments and struggle to generate diverse data, while existing evaluators either suffer from limited scalability or provide inaccurate and unreliable reward signals. To overcome these challenges, we introduce GSAR (Goal-State-Anchor Reward), a RL reward framework that supports scalable task generation and delivers reliable reward signals for stable and efficient policy optimization. Our approach features self-evolving data synthesis, which produces multiple environments through task execution and generates diverse tasks and goal states. Complementing this, a state-anchor mechanism automatically annotates task-relevant UI elements in successful goal states as reference anchors. During RL training, these reference anchors provide accurate, scalable reward signals that substantially enhance efficiency. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves over 90% accuracy on offline trajectory verification and performs closest to rule-based methods. Furthermore, agents trained using our reward framework exhibit strong performance on both AndroidWorld and our constructed benchmark, establishing a scalable approach for GUI agent training.
  </details>

- **[LenGuard-GPC: Length Guarding with Guided-Prompt Consistency for Spatial Reasoning Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17243)**  `arXiv:2607.17243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xingjian Tao, Yiwei Wang, Yujun Cai, Xinghua Qu, Jing Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-view spatial reasoning requires vision-language models to compare visual evidence across images, align object correspondences, and infer spatial relations over long visual contexts, a setting where chain-of-thought reasoning tends to grow verbose without becoming more accurate. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is a natural fit for this task, but standard GRPO reward relies on sparse outcome-level feedback and gives no signal about where a reasoning trajectory goes wrong, nor any control over its length. We propose LenGuard-GPC, a dense reward framework that addresses both problems together. For each sampled trajectory, it compares the token-wise predictive distributions under a standard prompt and a guided prompt, and uses the resulting token-sum KL divergence as a dense reward signal. Since this KL penalty accumulates over tokens and would otherwise reward shorter responses regardless of their quality, we introduce a staged length bonus that keeps reasoning length within a controlled range without simply encouraging brevity. On six multi-view spatial reasoning benchmarks, LenGuard-GPC improves accuracy over vanilla GRPO while reducing average response length.
  </details>

- **[A Self-Evolving Agent for Longitudinal Personal Health Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13940)**  `arXiv:2607.13940`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Li, Jiebi Deng, Tong Jin, Jinghong Han, Yuxin Wang, Zexin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical large language models and agents show strong single-encounter question answering and evidence interpretation but struggle to accumulate and govern experience across encounters. We present HealthJev, an open-source agent separating shared safety rules and medical knowledge from a private longitudinal memory and distilling lessons from completed cases. To filter out erroneous lessons before they affect future behaviour, we self-train JevGate, a lightweight System-One model that decides which lessons enter memory; each decision takes a median of 86 milliseconds and about 1,000 input tokens, sustaining experience accumulation at negligible run-time overhead. On a longitudinal benchmark of 90-day simulated daily interactions for 20 users built from real MIMIC-IV trajectories, HealthJev outperformed a memory-free ReAct agent and three memory-augmented frameworks, with all paired comparisons significant. Across six tasks spanning clinical records, medical imaging, genomics and clinical text, HealthJev improved every task with a mean gain of 13.3 percentage points over the base condition, surpassing all baselines. The frozen library transferred to six further backbones with macro-average gains of 6.8 to 15.1 percentage points, and one task's library brought gains on other tasks. These results indicate that governed, self-evolving memory supports longitudinal personal health management beyond single encounters. HealthJev is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RTSGameBench: Evaluating VLMs for Strategic Reasoning in Game Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18950)**  `arXiv:2606.18950`  `cs.AI`  
  _San Kim, Daechul Ahn, Reokyoung Kim, Hyeonbeom Choi, Seungyeon Jwa, Jonghyun Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) often struggle with strategic reasoning in a multi-agent setting, i.e., deciding under uncertainty while coordinating with and adapting to other agents. Real-time strategy (RTS) games can be a natural testbed for this capability, requiring long-horizon planning, coordination with allies, and adaptation to opponents under partial observability. But existing RTS benchmarks cover limited agent configurations, lack controlled evaluation across distinct strategic demands, and remain confined to fixed scenarios. To address these gaps, we propose RTSGAMEBENCH, built on Beyond All Reason, an RTS game whose larger scale broadens the strategic space beyond prior testbeds. In RTSGAMEBENCH, we offer holistic evaluation across diverse full-game matchups, diagnostic mini-games each emphasizing a focal strategic demand, and extensible coverage via a self-evolving framework that turns free-form queries into new mini-games, improving with accumulated experience. We further propose RTSGAMEAGENT, an agent harness that combines scalable group-level trajectory planning with short- and long-term memory and outperforms existing agents. We find that state-of-the-art VLMs struggle where humans succeed, particularly when they must coordinate with allies or contend with multiple opponents.
  </details>

- **[MM-OptBench: A Solver-Grounded Benchmark for Multimodal Optimization Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12154)**  `arXiv:2605.12154`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhong Li, Qi Huang, Yuxuan Zhu, Mohammad Mohammadi Amiri, Niki van Stein, Thomas Bäck, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimization modeling translates real decision-making problems into mathematical optimization models and solver-executable implementations. Although language models are increasingly used to generate optimization formulations and solver code, existing benchmarks for this capability are almost entirely text-only. This omits many optimization-modeling tasks that arise in operational practice, where requirements are described in text but essential instance information is often conveyed through visual artifacts such as tables, graphs, maps, schedules, and planning dashboards. We introduce multimodal optimization modeling, a benchmark setting where models must construct both a mathematical formulation and executable solver code from a text-and-visual problem specification. To evaluate this setting, we develop a solver-grounded framework that generates structured optimization instances, verifies each instance with an exact solver, and builds both model-facing inputs and hidden reference files from the same verified source. We instantiate it as MM-OptBench, a benchmark of 780 solver-verified instances spanning 6 optimization families, 26 subcategories, and 3 structural difficulty levels. We evaluate 9 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), including 6 frontier general-purpose and 3 math-specialized models, with aggregate, family-level, difficulty-level, and failure-mode analyses. The task remains far from solved: the best two models reach 52.1% and 51.3% pass@1, while on average across the six general-purpose MLLMs, pass@1 is 43.4% on easy and 15.9% on hard instances. All three math-specialized MLLMs solve 0/780 instances. Post-hoc extraction diagnostics and oracle-reading evaluation reveal difficulties in both multimodal grounding and downstream solver-artifact generation. MM-OptBench provides a concrete testbed for solver-grounded, decision-oriented multimodal intelligence.
  </details>

- **[COEVO: Co-Evolutionary Framework for Joint Functional Correctness and PPA Optimization in LLM-Based RTL Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15001)**  `arXiv:2604.15001`  `cs.AI`  
  _Heng Ping, Peiyu Zhang, Shixuan Li, Wei Yang, Anzhe Cheng, Shukai Duan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based RTL code generation methods increasingly target both functional correctness and PPA quality, yet existing approaches decouple the two objectives, optimizing PPA only after full correctness is achieved. Sequential multi-agent pipelines, evolutionary search with binary correctness gates, and hierarchical reward dependencies all discard partially correct but architecturally promising candidates. Moreover, existing methods reduce the multi-objective PPA space to a single scalar fitness that obscures the trade-offs among area, delay, and power. To address these limitations, we propose COEVO, a co-evolutionary framework that unifies correctness and PPA optimization within a single evolutionary loop. COEVO formulates correctness as a continuous co-optimization dimension alongside area, delay, and power, enabled by an enhanced testbench with fine-grained scoring. The framework allows PPA-promising but partially correct candidates to guide the search toward jointly optimal solutions, and further employs four-dimensional Pareto-based non-dominated sorting to preserve the PPA trade-off structure without manual weight tuning. On VerilogEval 2.0 and RTLLM 2.0, COEVO surpasses all agentic baselines across four LLM backbones. With GPT-5.4-mini, COEVO achieves 97.5\% and 94.5\% Pass@1 and attains the best PPA on 43 out of 49 synthesizable RTLLM 2.0 designs.
  </details>

- **[TorchUMM: A Unified Multimodal Model Codebase for Evaluation, Analysis, and Post-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10784)**  `arXiv:2604.10784`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yinyi Luo, Wenwen Wang, Hayes Bai, Hongyu Zhu, Hao Chen, Pan He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in unified multimodal models (UMMs) have led to a proliferation of architectures capable of understanding, generating, and editing across visual and textual modalities. However, developing a unified framework for UMMs remains challenging due to the diversity of model architectures and the heterogeneity of training paradigms and implementation details. In this paper, we present TorchUMM, the first unified codebase for comprehensive evaluation, analysis, and post-training across diverse UMM backbones, tasks, and datasets. TorchUMM supports a broad spectrum of models covering a wide range of scales and design paradigms. Our benchmark encompasses three core task dimensions: multimodal understanding, generation, and editing, and integrates both established and novel datasets to evaluate perception, reasoning, compositionality, and instruction-following abilities. By providing a unified interface and standardized evaluation protocols, TorchUMM enables fair and reproducible comparisons across heterogeneous models and fosters deeper insights into their strengths and limitations, facilitating the development of more capable unified multimodal systems. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MindGuard: Guardrail Classifiers for Multi-Turn Mental Health Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00950)**  `arXiv:2602.00950`  `cs.AI`  
  _António Farinhas, Nuno M. Guerreiro, José Pombal, Pedro Henrique Martins, Laura Melton, Alex Conway, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used for mental health support, yet their conversational coherence alone does not ensure clinical appropriateness. Existing general-purpose safeguards often fail to distinguish therapeutic disclosures from genuine clinical crises, and progress on developing better ones is bottlenecked by a lack of clinically grounded evaluation resources. To address this gap, we introduce a risk taxonomy, developed in collaboration with PhD-level clinical psychologists, that identifies actionable harm (self-harm and harm to others) while preserving space for safe, non-crisis therapeutic content. We release MindGuard-testset, a dataset of multi-turn conversations annotated at the turn level by clinical experts, which is, to our knowledge, the first public benchmark with turn-level clinical risk labels for multi-turn mental health support. We also release an automated red-teaming (ART) framework designed to measure how safety classifiers affect downstream model behavior in adversarial multi-turn interactions. Using synthetic dialogues generated via a controlled two-agent setup, we train MindGuard, a family of lightweight safety classifiers (with 4B and 8B parameters). Our classifiers reduce false positives at high-recall operating points and, when paired with clinician language models, help achieve lower attack success and harmful engagement rates compared to general-purpose safeguards. We release all models, training and human evaluation data, and the ART framework.
  </details>

- **[Failure-Driven Workflow Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10035)**  `arXiv:2510.10035`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jusheng Zhang, Kaitong Cai, Qinglin Zeng, Ningyuan Liu, Stephen Fan, Ziliang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing LLM-based workflows is typically formulated as a global search, where candidate workflows are evaluated based on a scalar metric. This paradigm, however, suffers from a critical flaw: information collapse. By reducing rich, multi-step execution traces to simple success/failure signals, existing methods are rendered blind to the underlying structure of failures, fundamentally preventing them from modeling the workflow's failure distribution. We reconceptualize this challenge as a distributional problem. We propose a new paradigm where the optimization goal is not to maximize a scalar score, but to directly minimize a workflow's Expected Failure Mass, i.e., the integral of its failure probability density function defined over a high-dimensional Failure Signature Space (FSS). This distributional lens allows us to move from inefficient, zero-order optimization to a principled, gradient-like descent on the failure landscape itself. We introduce CE-Graph, a framework that operationalizes this paradigm through a novel, failure-driven refinement process. CE-Graph approximates the failure distribution from a pool of counterexamples, identifies its densest regions as recurring failure modes, and applies targeted, operator-constrained graph edits via a Propose-and-Verify mechanism to greedily reduce the failure mass. On math, code, and QA benchmarks, our CE-Graph achieves higher robustness at a significantly lower cost than strong baselines. This suggests that a system's reliability emerges not from avoiding failures, but from systematically learning and reshaping the geometric structure of its failure distributions.
  </details>
